## A.21. Executive summary (max 750 words; approximately 1.5 pages)

1. **Historical context**: Jordan has experienced significant inter-annual rainfall variability and temperatures in summer (June-August) that often exceed 40°C. Approximately 94% of Jordan's annual rainfall is lost through evaporation, with the remaining 6% being used for irrigation or drinking water. This high evaporation rate results in Jordan's current ranking as the fifth most water-stressed country in the world, with only 125 cubic meters per capita annually.

2. **The positive water stress is exacerbated by climate change**, with climate projections under RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 showing increasingly dry conditions in this country. This drying effect is a result of an increase in mean annual temperature (MAT) and the lengthening of the growing season, which leads to increased evaporation rates from soil and vegetation. The warming also results in a shorter rainy season, with an increased frequency of high-intensity rainfall events. The increasing demand for water resources due to population growth and urbanization further exacerbates this situation.

3. **Notably**, the impacts of climate change will result in a considerable reduction in Jordan's agricultural production by 2050, leading to a significant decrease in food supply and consumption, which will severely affect local food supplies and consequently also food security in the country. The drought conditions experienced between 1987 and 2012 — worst drought recorded in Jordan over the past 900 years. During this period, rainfall decreased by up to 45% compared with the long-term average (1961-1990), leading to significant economic and environmental impacts! The drought caused a reduction in cereal harvests to only 0.5% of the country's total production, resulting in a non-recoverable crop destruction by 73%, and reduced meat and dairy production by

40%* Such impacts will also affect livestock production through disruption in the supply of fodder. Already, livestock numbers have fallen to 77% to the amount of fodder available for livestock have been observed and are projected to further decline due to climate change over the past decades. Therefore, there is a need to not only strengthen the supply of fodder but also to improve its quality. Livestock producers in Jordan are mostly small-scale,

4. Climate change is also strongly affecting the functioning of ecosystems in Jordan and consequently the services they provide — particularly maintenance of the hydrological cycle. Ecosystems in the country are already under pressure from human activities (e.g., urbanisation and agriculture; overgrazing; unregulated water harvesting; hiving; water over-extraction;
V) increased soil erosion; desertification; land degradation; deforestation; air pollution; and water pollution). Climate change is accelerating this degradation — with numerous adverse impacts, including increasing temperatures, changes in precipitation patterns, more frequent and intense droughts, increased forest fires,
droughts, changes in species composition, desertification, habitat degradation and animal losses. Many of the ecosystem services Jordanians depend on rest on these ecosystems.

In the absence of urgent climate change adaptation interventions at the landscape level, the projected increase in groundwater withdrawals will have increasingly severe impacts on water supply to 687 MCM/year by 2040). Combined with the compounding impacts on ecosystems, this would certainly lead to significant impacts on livelihoods and well-being of local communities across Jordan.

6. The Government of the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan is requesting GCF grant finance to respond to these climate change adaptation needs and impacts. GCF funding will be used for the implementation of
the Jordanian National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) and the Green Climate Fund (GCF)
mandate (and donor Consortium of NGOs on financing to initiate a paradigm shift in the approach to land use, water management and agriculture in Jordan, with a view to achieving sustainable development nationwide.

7. The objective of the proposed project is to reduce the negative impacts of climate change on water resources through improved aquifer recharge in a landscape-level approach. The proposed
approach includes: i) improved irrigation management through land restoration, improved agricultural practices and better water use efficiency; ii) increased groundwater recharge by building up infiltration galleries,
and iii) increased solar energy for evaporation reduction — leading to an ultimate increase in the country’s annual renewable water resources from 150 MCM/year (current) to a projected 246 MCM/year, or
1.9M of floating solar panels for evaporation reduction — leading to an ultimate increase in the country’s annual renewable water resources from 150 MCM/year (current) to a projected 344 MCM/year, or 2.8M of
floating solar panels for evaporation reduction — leading to an ultimate increase in the country’s annual renewable water resources from 150 MCM/year (current) to a projected 344 MCM/year and after 25 years.
Upscaling of all four on-the-ground interventions by others is expected to deliver an additional 94 MCM/year, or 7.6M of floating solar panels for evaporation reduction — leading
to an ultimate increase in the country’s annual renewable water resources from 150 MCM/year (current) to a projected 344 MCM/year and after 25 years. Furthermore, the project will demonstrate to decision-makers how climate change adaptation can be integrated into national development plans.
The project has the potential to enable Jordan to balance its water demand with water supply in the long term despite climate change.

8. Project interventions will directly benefit a total of 246,042 people (1/2% of the Jordanian population) —
135,736 men (55%) and 110,206 women (45%) — in the Jordan Valley by reducing their climate vulnerability. The project will also benefit a total of 1,828,920 (80.6% men and 19.4% women) from the Yarmouk area — Khaldia

Bin Al Waseef and Al Safi municipalities: (a) 105,941 (87.3% of men and 63,662 women) are from the King Faisal Canal, (b) New Anbar, Dara Abb and Mirdit municipalities, and (c) 78,855 (60,500 men and 43,235 men) are from the Kafraan site – Shoura Alwasta and Melees municipalities. Direct beneficiaries include: (i) 19,000 households in the King Faisal Canal area; (ii) 68,000 households in the areas under OUPt 2.1, 1,200 smallholder farming households (~45,000 people – an average of 5 people per household), and 3 other villages with a total population of ~7,000; (iii) 68,000 households across the sites (~14,000 people) will benefit directly from rainwater harvesting infrastructure introduced in the OUPt 2.1 area; and (iv) 59,300 households in the areas under OUPt 3.1. In addition to these areas, they are all expected to benefit from the augmentation of water resources under OUPt 2 and 3 other villages with a total population of ~7,000. The total number of direct beneficiaries is thus 189,546 people (127,179 people living in the municipalities of the three target sites [and related municipalities] listed above will benefit directly from the augmentation of water resources under OUPt 3). These households are expected to be resilient and adaptive to climate change conditions. Specifically, they will directly benefit from enhancements in the quality and quantity of ground and surface water resources, as well as improved access to water for irrigation and domestic use. The interventions in Component 2 are targeted at the entire 12,036 ha with potential for restoration to support water extraction and storage. These households are expected to be resilient and adaptive to climate change conditions and will benefit directly from the project.

The indirect beneficiaries of improved watershed management across the northern Jordan Valley will include approximately 574,890 people (monetary amounting to 756,000 people [350,320 women and 401,570 men]). Indirect benefits will include: (i) the enhancement of the quality and quantity of water resources; (ii) improved access to water for irrigation and domestic use; (iii) increased agricultural productivity; (iv) enhanced potential associated with re-vegetated landscapes; (v) improved health outcomes from cleaner water and sanitation; and (vi) reduced vulnerability to climate change impacts. The proposed project will support a transformation in the water sector to one in which women are knowledge leaders, decision-makers, and agents of change. This is achieved through capacity building and being involved in monitoring of water resource use and maintenance of community-level water infrastructure. This will be achieved by involving women in all aspects of the project from planning and implementation. The project’s community-level gender-responsive approach also includes promoting gender-inclusive climate-resilient livelihoods, which are more likely to benefit women (e.g., through the strengthening of adaptive capacity). Tailored awareness-raising and outreach programmes will also allow women to become leaders in the response to climate change adaptation at their communities.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Project rationale and context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Context

The Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan (hereinafter referred to as Jordan), a Middle Eastern country situated on the eastern bank of the Jordan River. The country divides into three main topographic regions, namely: the north; the central plateau; and the south. The northern region is characterized by the hilly terrain of the Golan Heights, the Jordan Valley, and its eastern desert and basin (Judea), which covers 80% of the country*. Most of Jordan's population of approximately 10 million people live in the north and central regions. The southern part of the country (predominantly in the Jordan Rift Valley, 1,194 people/km²), which is attributable to the area’s fertile soils, higher rainfall levels, and proximity to Israel, has experienced a significant population increase over the past six decades; the population of Jordan has increased 12-fold*, driven by both a historically high rate of endogenous population growth and an influx of refugees from neighboring countries, particularly Syria. The rapid increase in Jordan’s population has contributed to several developmental challenges, including: (i) increasing demand for water resources; (ii) pressure on the agricultural sector due to groundwater recharge and increased erosion resulting from overexploitation and degradation of ecosystems linked to agriculture; (iii) urbanization and associated environmental impacts such as air pollution and traffic congestion; (iv) the education system, health services, and urban infrastructure*. Of these challenges, water insecurity is a primary concern. The country’s per capita share of renewable water resources is 120 cubic meters/year, which is below the internationally recognized threshold of 1700 cubic meters/year per person*.

### Climate profile

Jordan is characterized by an arid climate and hot and dry summers contrasted with short cool, wetter, average temperatures in winter. The mean annual temperature ranges from 15°C to 24°C. The summer months (from April until November)* are the driest; the big Badia in the east receives less than 200 mm of rainfall annually, while the central and northern regions receive between 300-600 mm per year*. Jordan is also prone to winter storms* events, increasing rainfall variability in Jordan has resulted in more frequent drought and flash flood events*, as well as increased levels of erosion in degraded landscapes.

## Detailed primary and secondary climate analyses for the proposed project are presented in Annexes 2: Feasibility Study (Section A) and Appendix 6, respectively. The information presented below is a summary of what is presented in the more detailed analyses, which should be consulted as necessary.

### Observations of historical climate trends (1950–2015) in Jordan show that the country has already experienced significant warming over this period. Annual maximum temperatures have increased by 0.4–2.8°C and 0.3–1.8°C, respectively[1] (Figure 1). In terms of rainfall, average annual values have decreased by approximately 5% in the last three decades compared to the long-term mean for this period[2] (Figure 2). This decrease is marked by extended drought periods, as well as periods of increased rainfall intensity. The combination of these two factors has resulted in prolonged drought periods in the country. Droughts are projected to increase in frequency and severity, with the number of meteorological droughts in a 30-year period expected to increase from seven (1961–2010) to 28 (2017–2046)[3].

## Figure 2: Observed rainfall trends in Jordan


Changes in the standard precipitation evapotranspiration index (SPEI) were compared over time to determine if climate change has already impacted water availability in Jordan. The SPEI is a good measure of drought severity and can be used to identify periods of increasing or decreasing drought conditions. A 36-month running average was used to determine long-term trends, with the exception of the period from 1954–2015 where a 48-month running average was used due to data limitations. In the late 1990s there is a strong shift towards the prevalence of dry months, with the gradual decrease in SPEI values indicating more extreme drought conditions in Jordan. The current SPEI trends differ from natural cyclic phases and are likely consequences of climate change. This means that Jordan is currently experiencing the impacts of

## Observed drought trends in Jordan

20. As Jordan is already impacted by extreme baseline climate conditions — resulting in ecosystems and communities existing on the threshold of survivability — even small changes in temperature or rainfall are expected to have pronounced impacts. The country will experience two distinct types of climate change: (i) a wetter climate — where, between 2070–2100, the average temperature is expected to increase by between 2.1°C (RCP4.5) and 3.6°C (RCP8.5) (Figure 3); (ii) a drier climate — where, between 2070-2100, the number of consecutive dry days in the country are expected to exceed 42°C (RCP4.5) and 44°C (RCP8.5) (Figure 4): a drier climate is likely to increase the frequency and severity of droughts — where from 2040 to 2070 the average number of consecutive dry days is likely to increase by a maximum of 13 days in the north, and up to 68 days in the south. This will have severe impacts on water resources, food security, and human health.

Changes in rainfall are likely to increase the frequency, duration and severity of drought events (Figure 6). The long-term (1980-2020) SPI average of Jordan is between Near Normal Dry to Moderately/Severely Dry (-0.29 to -1.27). Drought conditions in Jordan are also expected to be intensified by reductions in soil moisture and streamflow, as well as increased evaporation rates (Figure 6). According to the climate change scenario of RCP8.5, the probability of years having simultaneous drought as well as average annual temperature increases of greater than +1.0°C above the baseline is projected to increase from less than 10% to more than 90% by 2100.

# Water resources

## Jordan’s warming climatic conditions and the above-mentioned controlling factors have resulted in increased water stress on the country over the past decade, with water availability decreasing by 145 CMY per person between 2007–2017[3]. Extreme temperatures and the ongoing drying trend in Jordan are the primary causes of severe water scarcity. The total annual renewable water resources available to Jordan is estimated at 689 CMY of water in 2017[4], which is 94% of the annual rainfall Jordan received that year. Between 2007 and 2017, the country’s population increased by approximately 35%, from 5.5 million to 7.4 million people, consequently ranks as the fifth most water-stressed country in the world with only 125 CMY currently available per person.

## Most of Jordan's water supply (95%) is derived from 10 reservoirs and two non-renewable groundwater basins, which are currently being over-extracted by -169% and -134% of safe yield, respectively[5]. The current rate of abstraction in the Upper Jordan River Basin is estimated at 2.73 CMCM per year (108% of the safe yield) for renewable groundwater, while the rate of abstraction is 1.24 CMCM higher than the estimated safe yield of 1.49 CMCM for non-renewable groundwater[6]. The Jordan River Water Authority (JRWA), Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI) and its subsidiaries (such as the Jordan Valley Authority) hold the limit-on-extraction, water allocation rights over renewable groundwater resources in the Upper Jordan River Basin. Renewable springs, 130 have dry up by 2013[7]. Concurrently, the current number of wells in Jordan exceeds 3,200, a number that is expected to increase to approximately 4,500 by 2025 and to more than 6,000 by 2050. This represents an increase from 1990 period to 275 CMCM — which is 190 MCMY less than the current abstraction — which in turn is only a fraction of Jordan’s total water resources (see Table 3). The number of wells in renewable groundwater basins and non-renewable groundwater basins is 124% of the safe yield, while renewable groundwater basins show a deficit of -169%.

## Reduced rainfall and increased evaporation from higher temperature results in continued rise in temperatures and heat waves. For example, the impact of rainfall through the Jordan River Basin has declined by over 50% since 1973 (from 2.18 CMCM to 1.04 to 1.60 CMCM in 2000[8]). This total runoff in the Upper Jordan River Basin is projected to decline from an average of 375 MCMY during the period 1990–2009 to 289 MCMY, or about 24% less than the average for the 1990 period to 2009 period[9]. Overall, a 51% increase in runoff as a result of the combined effect of reduced rainfall and increased evaporation under climate change conditions is projected to occur in Jordan.

## As surface water resources become less reliable under conditions of climate change, and wet seasons become shorter and drier, groundwater will increasingly be relied upon for domestic use. In 2017, approximately 63% of total water supply — and 73% of the municipal supply[10] — already coming from groundwater; any reduction in groundwater recharge will have severe implications for water security to the country[11]. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 15 of 144

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND

### Climate change is strongly affecting the functioning of ecosystems and their services in Jordan, which are already under pressure from over-exploitation of natural resources (e.g., water, land, forests) and unsustainable agriculture: 
- **overgrazing:** unregulated fuelwood harvesting; 
- **irrigation:** water over-extraction;
- **species overexploitation:** hunting and fishing. 

### Climate change is expected to be exacerbated by climate change, which is predicted to impact ecosystems through increased forest fires, changes in crop yields, desertification, halting of vegetation growth, and species loss?

## Most notable is the context of Jordan: the climate change-induced vegetation decline is expected to have on water resources. The current annual summer experience runoff range of 0–21 mm/year; degraded vegetation increases these losses to ~28–56 mm/year by decreasing infiltration and promoting runoff. Forest loss and landscape degradation are also expected to increase soil erosion, which in turn will reduce the capacity of soils to retain water. 
- **Evaporation/Transpiration rates:** altering moisture and heat fluxes between terrestrial surfaces and the atmosphere;
- **Soil organic matter content:** reduced by 20–35% due to increased decomposition and decreased vegetation cover; 
- **Vegetation productivity:** expected to reduce rainfall in some regions by as much as 30%, further limiting water availability. The loss of biomass and soil organic carbon will also increase the risk of desertification, which is already a major concern for Jordan.

### Agriculture accounts for 55–75% of Jordan’s total water consumption? As a result, water scarcity poses a risk to current food systems and food security in the country as the diminishing water supply results in lower agricultural yields. The most climate-vulnerable sector in Jordan is agriculture, which includes livestock (cattle, sheep, goats), barley and wheat — is the most climate-vulnerable form of cultivation in Jordan? Under future climate change, a decline in crop yields will be observed.

reduction in precipitation of 20% (by 2050 under RCP8.5) will lead to a decline in expected use for cattle burdary by 90%, and wheat by 21%. The total land for imported agriculture is expected to decrease by 5% by 2050.

In addition to the impacts on crop agriculture, climate change will have a considerable impact on livestock production. Livestock production in Jordan has been severely affected due to droughts and water scarcity. The area of pasturands as a result of climate change already have led to a 77% shortage in livestock feed*. During the severe drought years (2010-2013), the total land for cattle burdary decreased by 54%, while the total land for sheep and goats production decreased by 29%. The area under wheat production has also been reduced by 68% year. Elevated temperatures and reduced rainfall will also increase the demand for water for irrigation of crops by 10% in 2030, and by 54% in 2050.

Under future climate change conditions, increasingly variable and decreasing rainfall, as well as more severe droughts, will not only limit the optimal time available for planting crops, but also the types of crops that can be grown. The area under wheat production is expected to decrease by 37%, while the total land for sheep and goats feed for rained by barley* decreased by 50% and for wheat by 21%. By 2050, the combined effect of a 20% reduction in rainfall and an increase in temperature will lead to a 48% decrease in the area under wheat production. The increase in temperature is also predicted to lower the yields of irrigated crops. At all above current values, irrigated vegetable yield is expected to decline by 15%, while the total land for sheep and goats feed for rained by barley* will be reduced by 20% due to an increase in temperature at C°. Elevated temperatures and reduced rainfall will also increase the demand for water for irrigation of crops by 10% in 2030, and by 54% in 2050. This stress will be compounded by reductions in overall water availability. Therefore, there is a need to develop new strategies that can help Jordanian farmers adapt to climate change and reduce their vulnerability to climate-vulnerable crops like barley due to those which are more diverse and resilient to climate change, such as quinoa and pearl millet.

Vulnerability of local populations

32. Vulnerability in Jordan is largely driven by income levels and poverty rates. Unemployment levels* in Jordan are high, with 18% of the population unemployed (World Bank 2019). The increase in unemployment since 2015 is attributed to a slowing economy and the refugee influx, particularly into urban centres*. According to the World Bank (2019), the unemployment rate has increased from 7.3% in 2014 to 8.6% in 2018. The governments of Amman, Mafraq, Tafas, and Zarqa* Since the beginning of the Syrian conflict in 2011, these governorates have experienced a significant increase in unemployment rates. In 2017, the rate was 35% compared to an unemployment rate nationally*. The rate in these areas has increased by *0.4% since 2011, compared with +1% decrease for the country as a whole.

34. Women and youths have the highest respective unemployment rates, with unemployed women particularly reflecting the greatest proportion of unemployed people in Jordan*. Employment amongst women is extremely low

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 (Page 17 of 144)

--- only 13% of women over 15 years of age are included in the paid labour force[1]. In this Global Gender Gap Index[2], which provides information on labour market participation, Jordan ranked 148th out of 165 analysed countries. Youth unemployment is also high in Jordan, particularly among those holding university degrees[3]. The main factors contributing to youth unemployment are the lack of job opportunities and a mismatch between the elevated levels of education[4]. While more than 75% of unemployed men in Jordan hold a high school diploma or more, only about half have completed tertiary education. Women with tertiary education are less likely to obtain tertiary qualifications[5]. Unemployment and insufficient disposable income interact and contribute to poverty in Jordan. The majority of the poor live in rural areas where they face many challenges such as lack of access to working poor[6]. Like unemployment, poverty in Jordan is concentrated in the governorates that have historically been more vulnerable to climate change. These include the northern governorates, which are located in urban areas of the governorates of Amman, Irbid, Zarqa and Ma'an[7]. Across the project sites of Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Al-Husseini, 20% of households have no access to piped water. The majority of the rural population lives in rural areas, where poverty is more prevalent than in urban areas and living conditions are difficult. All these factors make Jordan vulnerable to climate change[8]. In addition, the country's limited capacity for sustainable farming through land leasing is limited. The most vulnerable groups include large rural households with low literacy rates (60% of whom have no education), households 125% with low income and small households that do not own land or have very little land.

35. The project target area is located within the Jordan Valley — the primary agricultural region of the country. The Jordan Valley has a high population density, which makes it one of the most vulnerable areas in terms of poverty[9]. This dependence on agriculture combined with local poverty rates and the adaptive capacity, make these communities particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased temperatures, reduced rainfall, and changes in precipitation patterns. These factors have significant implications for the impacts of climate change, particularly reductions in water resources. As agricultural activities are vulnerable to climate change[10], the Jordan Valley is directly affected by climate change. Under future climate conditions, decreased agricultural yields will lead to a reduction in household income and food security. This will result in an increase in poverty rates without a transition to climate-resilient food systems.

36. Future climate change will additionally present multiple health risks to Jordan's most vulnerable inhabitants[11]. These include increased heat stress-related illnesses, which may be exacerbated by poor water quality and reduced access to clean drinking water. As the temperature increases, the demand for water resources is reduced due to secular trends in rainfall yields[12]. As a result of this, the water sector will face increasing pressure from both domestic and agricultural users. This leads to conflicts over water resources that may be difficult to resolve[13]. As a consequence, higher temperatures lead to a reduction in the number of households capable of growing their own food.

Institutional context

37. With regard to institutions of relevance to integrated landscapes and water resources management include inter alia the: (i) Ministry of Environment (MoE)[14] which is responsible for the country's legal and policy framework relating to environmental protection, including climate change; (ii) Ministry of Water Resources (MWR), which oversees the water sector, water supply and wastewater system. The MWR's mandate includes the formulation of national water policies[15].

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 18 of 144

## Related projects/interventions

### Project components and requirements are being made through public expenditure and donor-funded initiatives to increase the resilience of Jordan’s vulnerable communities to the impacts of climate change.

#### Learning and lessons from existing interventions

- **Component 2 - Climate change resilience for enhanced livelihoods and food security, and Component 3 - Scaling up climate-resilient development in Jordan** are two components of the Jordan’s Investment Plan (JIP) that will be implemented jointly by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), the European Union (EU), and the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The project focuses on addressing climate change impacts in the agricultural sector through improved water use efficiency, while also focusing on land management to address the climate change impacts of rising temperatures, droughts and floods not only on agriculture but also on other sectors. Table 1 provides a summary of similarities between these two projects.

#### Summary of key similarities and differences between JLM and BRCJ

| Project Aspect | Integrated Land Management (ILM) | Jordan River Basin Climate Change Joint Initiative (BRCJ) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Theme** | Integrated land and water resources management (ILRM) | Water use efficiency in the agriculture sector |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 19 of 144

## Project Title
Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative

### Target Area
Governments of Israel and Jordan in the northern Jordan Valley

**Goal**
If investments are made in sustainable land management, water resources will be conserved, livelihoods strengthened, THEN water security will be enhanced. RECOMMENDATION: Investment in climate-resilient technology adoption reduces exposure and vulnerability to climate change impacts.

### Approach
The objective is to reduce the negative impacts of climate change on water resources or through a landscape-level approach that integrates water management with land use planning, soil conservation, and riverbank harvesting, or aquifer replenishment. It improved livelihoods and food security while enhancing water security.

**Component 1:** Water under conditions of climate change

**Component 2:** Water under conditions of climate change

**Component 3:** Scaling-up adaptation climate policy and institutional capacity through three components:

* **Component 3.1:** JILM protection supply and use efficiency
* **Component 3.2:** Managed aquifer recharge
* **Component 3.3:** Water users' associations as an enabling mechanism

### Outcomes/results
Outcome 1: Increased public budget for adaptation investments in water resources and food security
Outcome 2: Improved water availability to existing water systems
Outcome 3: Increased water security for households in target areas
Outcome 4: Household behavioral change achieved through improved access to water services
Outcome 5: Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the communities living on the northern Jordan Valley and its tributaries, with respect to food and water security

### Results
* Improved access to water services
* High levels of ecosystem services

### Component 2: Climate Change resilience for existing livelihoods and food security

- **JLM will be implementing in last ever remaining wetlands and harvesting rainwater to increase water availability for agriculture on smallholder farms.**
- **Through JLM’s Component 2, farmers will adopt climate-smart agricultural practices with the aim of increasing their productivity and income through beneficiary communities that are based around existing livelihoods that are heavily dependent on ecosystem services responsible for ecosystem degradation.**

| JLM is a long-term project in the Dead Sea Basin and Jordan Valley, which will be implemented at sites across both basins together, with the aim of creating a broader group of beneficiaries. | As the projects are being implemented at different sites their beneficiaries are not necessarily the same. However, they do share some common goals and objectives. |
| JLM’s sites in the Dead Sea Basin and Jordan Valley will be complemented by the BRCCJ’s sites in the Dead Sea Basin and Jordan Valley. The two projects will provide examples of different approaches to climate change adaptation that can be scaled up across various areas of the rest of Jordan. |

### Component 3: Climate Change Adaptation

- **Livelihood interventions under BRCCJ are largely focused on livestock production related to restoration to acacia. While the activities within JLM are more directly related to rainfed agriculture and semi-arid farms which can thrive in a climate change context, they will also include climate-resilient agricultural interventions.**

## Project name, date, fund and amount.

### Description and complementarities

The HERD project was implemented with the objective of strengthening the restoration and sustainable management of degraded lands in Jordan through a strengthened governance and capacity for integrated landscape and management of water resources. The project is funded by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) under the Jordan Water Management Initiative (JWMI) under ODA. The proposed project will additionally adopt best practices for rangeland development, including the use of non-conventional water sources to be used in the design of the proposed project. The project will also implement a climate change adaptation strategy that includes ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) interventions as well as the implementation of an integrated land and water management approach.

### Jordan Water Management Initiative (JWMI - 2016-2021; GCF, US$337,849,500)

Increasing the resilience of poor and vulnerable communities to climate change through the use of water resources in Jordan. The project will be implemented by Jordan’s Ministry of Planning and International Cooperation (MOPIC), in close cooperation with the Jordanian Water Authority (JWA) and the World Bank. The project will focus on increasing the resilience of poor and vulnerable communities to climate change, while contributing to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), which is being developed by Jordan through a consultative participatory process.

### Jordan Water Management Project (JWMP - 2015-2020; GCF, US$837,690,000)

The project will be implemented by the International Centre for Agricultural Research in the Dry Areas (ICARDA), the project will focus on increasing the resilience of poor and vulnerable communities to climate change through the use of water resources. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), which is being developed by Jordan through a consultative participatory process.

### Sustainable use of Ecosystem Services in Jordan (SUSE - 2016-2019; GCF, US$458,370,000)

The project will be implemented by the International Centre for Agricultural Research in the Dry Areas (ICARDA), the project will focus on increasing the resilience of poor and vulnerable communities to climate change through the use of water resources. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), which is being developed by Jordan through a consultative participatory process.

### Managing Water Resources in Jordan (2016-2019; GCF, US$458,370,000)

The project will be implemented by the International Centre for Agricultural Research in the Dry Areas (ICARDA), the project will focus on increasing the resilience of poor and vulnerable communities to climate change through the use of water resources. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), which is being developed by Jordan through a consultative participatory process.

## Project name, date, total amount and funding sources

### Jordan Land and Water Management Project (JLWM)
(July 2015 - June 2021): GF - US$297,780,000

## Description and complementsarity

The project forms part of the Middle East and North Africa Regional Program for Promoting Integrated Land and Water Management in Agriculture (JLWM). The project is designed to address land degradation and mitigate impacts of land degradation and promote the incorporation of an ecosystem-based approach into Jordan’s national policies. It will build on existing institutional arrangements, including the country’s information management systems, which were developed to monitor land resources and link land use with water resources.

The project will also contribute to the implementation of the Global Environment Facility (GEF) decision-support tool for SUM for governmental stakeholders (Activity 1.2: Best practices from Jordan). Under Activity 2.0 of the JUWM project, the implementation of the proposed project-participatory integrated land and water management in agriculture (JLWM) will be supported by a series of institutional reforms to strengthen institutions and capacities enhanced through this project to provide the awareness of the importance of sustainable land use practices and their role in improving livelihoods.

### Securing Rights and Restoring Lands for Improved Livelihoods (2011-2013): EU

The three-year Jordan component of the project built the capacity of local communities to participate in decision-making processes related to land management, including the development of community income generation options to inform participatory climate-resilient LEM plans as well as climate-smart agriculture practices.

### Integrated Conservation and Natural Resource Management in the Jordan Desert (2011-2015): GEF - US$13.7 million

The project aimed at improving ecosystem services, including water supply, soil conservation, and restoration of ecosystems in drylands and rangelands. Specifically, the objective of the project was to improve land management practices that would lead to improved livelihoods.

### Community-based Operation of the Scource Water Resources in Agriculture in Central West Jordan (2010-2015): IFAD, AFEDS, OPEC Fund for Agricultural Development and GEF

The project aimed at improving water productivity and enhancing sustainable land management practices to increase water productivity and optimize water use within three rainfed agro-systems in the central west region of Jordan. The project will build on best practices regarding rangeland harvesting and rangeland rehabilitation developed by OMANA being pilot-tested on the ground previously.

# B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

## Proposal statement

Jordan is currently experiencing extreme climatic conditions, which contributes to its ranking as the fifth most water-stressed country in the world. These conditions are expected to worsen over time, compounding the ongoing dry trend across the country. These changes are already having and will continue to have a significant impact on the availability of freshwater resources. The first drying trend resulting from climate change is reducing the country’s naturally limited and over-exploited groundwater resources. Second, by increasing the severity of already existing water stress in Jordan, climate change will exacerbate existing challenges related to water quantity and quality. Second, by increasing the severity of already existing water stress in Jordan, climate change will exacerbate existing challenges related to water quantity and quality.

### Target communities

The proposed project will target local communities and small-scale farmers, including women and female-headed households, who are extremely vulnerable to the impacts of climate change on water resources, which continue to reduce water flow and livelihood security in these communities. This increased vulnerability will be considerable in the short term as a result of reduced rainfall and increased evaporation rates. The long-term impact is limited because of their inflexibility in cropping and irrigation systems. Specifically, farm systems are currently dominated by rainfed agriculture (80%), with only 20% under irrigation. These small-scale farmers are being under or regarding sharecropping agreements. These systems are allocated a water quota (of 500 CM/ha), with dry-irrigation systems using up to 1000 CM/ha. The average: i) maximum is USD$2,000/ha; ii) farm size in 2 ha; and iii) number of family workers is 7 per farm.

### Preferred adaptation strategy

For Jordan to mitigate the impacts of climate change and strengthen the climate resilience of vulnerable communities, it is necessary to secure the provision of water resources. This is achieved by shifting from a command-and-control approach to an adaptive management approach that is participatory, integrated, and participatory-adaptation-focused, and that contributes to gender-responsive climate-resilient systems. The adoption of an AM/RA approach at the community level in Jordan should include a focus on a combination of: i) improved ecosystem management; ii) investment in water resources infrastructure; iii) increased access to water resources for vulnerable communities; iv) governance on water resources in the Jordan Valley; and v) considers gender-related water needs and the unique challenges faced by women. The proposed adaptation strategy will be implemented at three levels: national, sub-national, and non-governmental levels. The proposed adaptation solution contributes directly to the GCF’s Adopted Strategic Objective 2025-2030: “To support countries in implementing climate-resilient development strategies.” This paradigm shift across sectors, including to climate-resilient ecosystems and food systems; ii) Adaptation Action Plan (AAP); iii) Water Resources Management (WRM); iv) Climate Change Adaptation (CCA); v) GCF Targeted Results for 2028-2027 Include: 4. Food Supply for developing countries that results in 190 to 280 million people being better fed and more resilient.

# Barrier to the Adaptation Solution

## Insufficient Institutional and Technical Cooperation Capacity (IATCC)

### Barriers to the Adaptation Solution

1. **Institutional barriers:**
   - Lack of institutional mandates for water resources management.
   - Weakness in the implementation of Jordan’s Water Law (JWL) and its associated regulations.
   - Limited capacity within the Ministry of Water Resources (MWR), which is responsible for implementing the JWL.

2. **Technical barriers:**
   - Inadequate technical cooperation capacity, including limited human resources and expertise.
   - Lack of coordination between different ministries and sub-institutions, leading to conflicting goals or duplication of efforts.
   - Existence of a culture of competition rather than collaboration among institutions.
   - Limited institutional capacity for project implementation.

48. Barrier 2: Limited availability of and access to climate change adaptation information and data. The constrained availability of information makes it more difficult for policymakers, knowledge and advisory to make particularly tough decisions related to climate change is one of the most considerable barriers to adaptation in Jordan. While there have been advances towards improving the availability of climate change information and data, these are still limited. For example, while the country has made some progress in providing information on drought management, including adaptation interventions, availability of and access to climate change adaptation information and data is still a major barrier to effective climate change adaptation planning and decision-making. The country is currently inhibited by the insufficient availability of technological information on adaptation and sustainable water resources management practices. This lack of information makes it difficult for policymakers to effectively integrate climate change adaptation into development strategies and planning, particularly in the water and agricultural sectors.

49. Barrier 3: Insufficient awareness by politicians, community leaders, decision-makers and the public of the impacts of climate change and appropriate adaptation responses. This relates to the limited awareness of policymakers, community leaders, decision-makers and the general public about the impacts of climate change on their livelihoods as well as the benefits of adaptation actions. While awareness of climate change of politicians within Jordan's government has increased in recent years, there is still a need for further improvement (Jordan’s 2015 National Communication to the UNFCCC). The country’s Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) remains[1]. Although recent steps such as the establishment of the Climate Change Unit and the creation of a national climate change strategy have provided some leadership towards the improved management of water resources under climate change condition is lacking. At the public level, there are limited awareness about drought and climate change adaptation in rural areas and particularly in the water sector[2].

50. Barrier 4: Insufficient financial capacity for climate change adaptation. The country’s budgetary priorities are not aligned with its adaptation needs. While a significant amount of funding gap exists between the country's adaptation goals and its public finances, Effective climate change adaptation requires substantial investment to implement adaptation measures. In Jordan, the government has acknowledged the need to commit funds to climate change adaptation and mitigation, there is no formal mechanism in place for the allocation of these funds. The lack of a clear policy framework for financing domestic projects impede the implementation of necessary activities[3]. This is compounded by a lack of access to international funding from multilateral institutions and global funds to support the needs of households and communities at risk.

51. Barrier 5: Limited awareness among local communities (particularly in Mafra) and farmers of the benefits and importance of climate-adapted water management, including the management of green water. Jordan is opposed to the use of grey water for irrigation purposes as it can lead to soil salinization. The integration and implementation of climate-adapted water management (such as the management of green water) into existing agricultural practices remains a challenge in Jordan. There are limited awareness among local communities and farmers about the benefits and importance of climate-adapted water management practices – particularly within the agricultural sector – its implementation on Jordan has been hampered by lack of awareness, knowledge and skills to implement such practices[4]. The integration of climate-adapted water management. Currently, local actors (such as WUAs in the agriculture sector), Development Advisory Committees (DACs) and other stakeholders are involved in the promotion of climate-adapted water management approaches such as rainwater harvesting through grants provided by international organizations and national government. However, the capacity of these actors to promote and implement climate-adapted water management is restricted, limiting their uptake[5]. Systemic challenges are also preventing the expansion of the climate-adapted

## Water management sector in Jordan: For example, subsidies for groundwater abstraction are causing overexploitation of the resource because water prices and short-term benefits of this practice are greater than their long-term costs.

### Barrier 8: Gender inequality
The socio-economic disadvantage of women in Jordan is categorized by high unemployment rates, economic inequality, political marginalization of youth and women, and a mismatch between educational skills and skills sought by the labor market. Inequalities remain in participation in economic and political spheres. Women are less likely to be employed than men (50% vs 68%), and they earn lower wages. Furthermore, that within society, boys are preferred to girls and decision-making within the home is largely in the hands of husbands. This gender inequality has a direct impact on women’s access to resources such as land, water, and women's empowerment in Jordan, preventing women from exercising their right to lead a full life in both public and private spheres. Women have limited access to productive assets (land, livestock, irrigation systems) compared with men. The capacity of women to participate in and implement climate change adaptation strategies is also reduced by a lack of access to income, along with their high illiteracy, unemployment, and poverty rates in Jordan.

### Project approach to overcoming barriers to adaptation
The project will address the identified gender inequality barriers and consequently enhance the climate resilience of Jordan’s water sector through four interrelated outputs, described in detail in Section 3.8. ### Barrier 1: Lack of institutional capacity and technical expertise of relevant actors to plan and implement climate-resilient IWRM
Barrier 1 will be addressed by increasing the institutional and technical capacity of relevant actors for example, through the establishment of a Water Fund. The Water Fund will provide financial resources at both national and sub-national levels as well as mainstreaming adaptation into national budget lines, which additionally contributes to addressing Barrier 6.

### Barrier 2: Lack of awareness among decision-makers and the public on the impacts of climate change
Barrier 2 will be addressed by increasing the institutional and technical capacity of relevant actors for example, through the establishment of a Water Fund. The Water Fund will provide financial resources at both national and sub-national levels as well as mainstreaming adaptation into national budget lines, which additionally contributes to addressing Barrier 6.

### Barrier 3: Lack of gender-inclusive adaptive strategies
Barrier 3 is addressed by implementing a gender-inclusive adaptive strategies under the project proposal. This will include both national and site-specific water management activities and will be implemented through the following actions:

1. **Awareness-raising**: The project will raise awareness among decision-makers and the public on the impacts of climate change.
2. **Capacity-building**: Capacity-building interventions will include developing and implementing a gender-sensitive IWRM framework at the national level that includes a gender-responsive action plan, as well as participatory climate-resilient watershed management plans for the target watersheds in the Jordan Valley.

At the policy-making and planning level, the project will generate and share evidence of the economic benefits of ILWM to project beneficiaries. The project will also support the establishment of a Water Fund in Jordan (Barrier 5), local communities at project sites, including smallholder farmers, will be trained on the installation and maintenance of appropriate technologies at community centres and local institutions, will build awareness for and provide a practical demonstration of the benefits of climate-resilient use of water resources. ## Gender inequality (Barrier 6) will be addressed by raising awareness on the different vulnerabilities to and impacts of climate change on men and women. This will also be addressed through the livelihoods- and water supply and management-related activities, which are gender-sensitive and promote the empowerment of women. The project’s gender action plan (GAP) will be complemented by the introduction of gender-responsive climate resilience indicators in the Water Fund’s monitoring system. As a result, women will be empowered to monitor and manage local water supplies more effectively. This will also provide for project monitoring and evaluation through a gender lens (100% female representation), and providing additional livelihoods for women.

60. Together, the aforementioned interventions will overcome the barriers to climate change adaptation in Jordan and within the Jordan Valley. The project will contribute to the achievement of SDG 2 (Zero Hunger) by promoting an integrated approach to land and water resources management. This shift will ultimately reduce the impact of climate change on local communities by improving the provision and management of ecosystem services, with a focus on water resources, and strengthening the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities.

## Project activity

## B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

The objective of the proposed project is to reduce the negative impacts of climate change on water resources in Jordan through a multi-sectoral approach that includes the Water Resources Management Framework (WRFM) – including improved ecosystem management, enhanced protection, supply and use of water; beneficial land-use practices; and integrated water resource management. The project will have a particular salient objective of transforming the Jordan Valley into an economically viable region by 2035. The project will be implemented in a partnership effective model with Jordan’s government organisations and civil society, as well as via water resources and land-management policy. This will be achieved through: i) strengthening the capacity of local communities to manage their natural resources; ii) strengthening the capacity of the northern Jordan Valley to strengthen its resilience to climate change, as well as increase their capacity to support economic development in the region; iii) increasing awareness among stakeholders about the impacts and impacts of climate change on the lives and livelihoods of total people, while also responding to gender-specific needs. The project will be implemented through a series of interventions that include: i) improved water harvesting interventions in vulnerable communities; ii) water harvesting and conservation practices on smallholder farms; iii) integrated land-use planning for sustainable agriculture and livestock production; iv) climate-smart agriculture and water is aligned with — and seeks to build on and complement — major GIZ development and agricultural planning programs.

The management and upscaling of ecosystem management and restoration activities will be financed by a Water Fund, which will leverage GCF resources to crowd-in private and public investment into ecosystems-based adaptation (EBA).

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 15 of 144

#### OVERCOMING THE KEY BARRIER OF INSUFFICIENT FINANCE FOR THE LONG-TERM SUSTAINABILITY OF ADAPTATION INTERVENTIONS

This is the first time we fund the long-term financing strategy, which will include securing agreements with major water users (private sector) who are willing to pay for ongoing ecosystem management in return for the water resources generated by the project. This will be a win-win situation for all parties involved.

In downstream areas of the Jordan Valley who would benefit from and rely on increased water resources generated via climate-resilient IWRM, plans will be implemented in the target watersheds, namely the Jordan Valley, Yarmouk, Arava, and Dead Sea. The Jordan Valley is a priority area for the implementation of climate-resilient IWRM as part of Jordan’s national climate-resilient IWRM into Jordan’s policy planning and legal frameworks in agriculture, land, water and natural resources management.

The development of a comprehensive institutional framework, including policies, strategies, guidelines, and technical capacity for this mainstreaming will be built within government—for example, MoIWA and JVC—and by non-governmental organizations (NGOs) such as the Jordan River Basin Association (JRBA). The development of climate change adaptation knowledge management under the project will contribute to capacity building activities and promote information sharing among all relevant stakeholders.

#### 63. OVERVIEW OF THE DESIGN OF THE PROPOSED PROJECT'S FOUR COMPONENTS

A detailed overview of the design of the proposed project's four components is provided below. Specific detail on the technical design of the activities under each component is presented in Section 10 of Annex A: Feasibility Study.

**Component 1:** Climate change adaptation objectives in Jordan

Output 1. Strengthened governance and capacity for climate-resilient integrated landscape and water resources management, including the development of a national policy framework and guidelines for IWRM as part of an overall adaptation approach

Output 4.1.1 and 1.2 will be cofunded by CCF Outcome A3.0—Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-resilient planning and development—and A3.6—Strengthened awareness of climate risks and threat reduction processes—by enhancing governance and capacity for climate-resilient IWRM in Jordan.

The project will work with the Ministry of Environment (MoE) and other relevant national and sub-national institutions such as NGOs stakeholders for landscape management across the Jordan Valley will be built through several initiatives, including training on policy and decision-making for climate-resilient IWRM. This will include training on relevant national and sub-national institutions to use climate data, the results of climate analyses and risk assessments for planning and decision making in the Jordan Valley.

The development of a comprehensive institutional framework, including policies, strategies, guidelines, and technical capacity for this mainstreaming will be built within government—for example, MoIWA and JVC—and by non-governmental organizations (NGOs) such as the Jordan River Basin Association (JRBA). The development of climate change adaptation knowledge management under the project will contribute to capacity building activities and promote information sharing among all relevant stakeholders.

The Ministry of Environment (MoE) will be responsible for mainstreaming climate-resilient IWRM practices into relevant policies, plans, strategies, and legislation.

67. To ensure a participatory and cross-sectoral approach to landscape management, WMCs will be established at each of the three pilot sites. The WMCs will be responsible for coordinating the implementation of landscape management to enhance technical capacity at both the local and national level. Training will differ according to the role of participants in the WMCs. In addition, a training manual will be developed by the project team and used as a training programme for climate-resilient watershed management.

68. Outputs 1.1.1 and 1.2 will see the establishment of an enabling environment for the implementation of climate resilient water resources management (LWRM). These outputs are interlinked with each other, and together they strengthen the mandate and capacity of main government institutions and non-state actors for LWRM. mainstreaming LWRM as an adaptation approach is a key element in this output. This will be achieved by developing guidelines and protocols for economic benefits of LWRM (and an adaptation approach) to use policy makers and planners. Through its policy advocacy, the project will also support the development of a national water strategy that includes climate change considerations (LWRM in particular), which along with the Water Fund Activity (Component 2) would support scaling up of the proposed landscape management approach at scale (see Section 6.2). This will support a long-term balance in the country's water resources in the face of climate change.

69. Under the project’s designed executive structure, MoCW will act as an Executing Entity for Component 1. Activity 1.1.1: Strengthen the mandate and capacity of main government institutions – including MoCW, MML and other relevant ministries and agencies – to mainstream LWRM in their respective mandates and capacities. This includes strengthening the mandate and capacity of national policy- and decision-making processes, supporting the scaling up and replication of the project’s interventions. These activities will be carried out through a combination of advocacy, technical assistance, institutional development and training national and sub-national policy- and decision-makers on the use of climate-related data. Institutional development will include strengthening the mandate and capacity of MoCW to mainstream LWRM in its overall mandate also includes oversight of water and irrigation policy implementation – to facilitate the uptake of LWRM at scale.

Sub-activity 1.1.1: Develop land-use guidelines, standards and protocols for climate-resilient LWRM in Jordan

71. Guidelines, standards and protocols will be developed in year 1 to facilitate a standardized and coordinated approach by all relevant stakeholders towards the implementation of climate resilient water resources management (LWRM) because knowledge, technical and institutional capacity to implement activities related to climate change adaptation are currently lacking at national level. The guidelines, standards and protocols will cover the following areas: land use and water resources management including the MoCW; NGOs; MML; LULS; DLS; AGRA; JVA; MoA; WAI; WAM; Jordan Valley Authority (JVA); Water Fund Activity (Component 2); and other relevant institutions. The guidelines, standards and protocols will be developed in close collaboration with the Jordan Valley, working within existing coordination and planning structures. It will also target both supply- and demand-side factors of water-use and prevalent practices.

73. The standard guidelines, standards and protocols will: a) define roles and responsibilities for the relevant actors; b) promote gender-inclusive community engagement and participation in landscape management; c) prioritise supply- and demand-side interventions to address climate change impacts on water resources; d) provide technical guidance and building on MoCW’s water-use quotas and water-saving policies, with a focus on enhancing the climate resilience of Jordanian communities. The guidelines will also include recommendations for policy changes that can be used to encourage poor rural communities to invest in rainwater harvesting infrastructure (such as rooftop water tanks) or other water-saving technologies. These guidelines, standards and protocols are expected to be developed by MoCW and implemented in the relevant watersheds are part of a coordinated and standardized LWRM approach to climate change adaptation. The guidelines, standards and protocols will also include a set of monitoring and evaluation protocols, guidelines and standards will seek to build upon existing planning and development processes, to coordinate and integrate activities; bridge gaps and address priorities previously identified (see Section 10 of Annex B: Feasibility

84. The economic analysis will be conducted to understand and appreciate the relevant interlinked ecological processes and how they directly produce economic benefits, as well as the costs associated with implementing these processes. This analysis will consider i) avoided costs – impacts or losses that would have occurred had the project not been implemented; ii) direct implementation costs – the cost of implementing grey infrastructure (as opposed to green natural resources); iii) indirect implementation costs – the cost of implementing LWRM elements in a way that maximizes the following sustainable development co-benefits will be included: flood attenuation, enhanced biodiversity, decreased pollution and improved water quality, increased agricultural food security, increased climate resilience of pastoral livelihoods and energy generation from floating solar.

The WWCs will be responsible for developing a gender-sensitive action plan to promote women’s participation in water management, including government institutions. WWCs will also appoint Technical Partnerships. Once established, the project will work closely with the MoWR to formally recognise and include WWCs as legitimate stakeholders (particularly in Jordan) and contribute to the operationalisation of their mandates. The involvement of local stakeholders (community in particular) but contribute to a broader environmental legislation will ensure their recognition and authority contributing to the representation of communities in water management.

The establishment of WWICs within each catchment, with the support of a consortium of NGOs involved in project execution, will contribute to successful engagement with local-level governance structures. This will be achieved by ensuring that WWCs are recognised as legitimate stakeholders through recognising them in legislation and formally defining their mandates; the medium- to long-term sustainability of WWCs is ensured by providing them with the necessary resources and capacity-building.

The presence of WWICs will ensure that larger communities’ needs and concerns are considered in the development and implementation of water monitoring and management plans. This will include specifically focusing on the needs of vulnerable groups such as women, youth, and children. WWCs will also be involved in the decision-making process, providing them with direct inputs into decision-making processes and ensuring that relevant information is always made available.

12) The Tolfi for the role and function of WWICs was presented in Annex 28. These will be further developed during project implementation by the project Technical Partners.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 43 of 144

#### Support Project (LENS), an ongoing project that aims to encourage sustainable economic growth and develop the vulnerable coastal communities by combining local economic development efforts with private sector initiatives.

Vegetable gardening has previously been promoted by projects such as the Growing the Desert Project, which encourages the use of rainwater harvesting systems in smallholder farms. The project also promotes agroforestry through permaculture sustainable livelihoods, and it donating wheat beds to vulnerable families (including female-headed households).

#### Output 2.1 will contribute to GCF Outcome A7.0 – strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks – by implementing an LWRRM approach and restoring ecosystems in a climate-resilient manner in the project target areas.

The three main ecosystem services that are being restored include: (i) water supply; (ii) food security; and (iii) flood threats, will be taken into account when developing site- and ecosystem-specific restoration protocols. In addition to the above, the following activities will also be implemented as part of the project’s restoration activities:

1. Passive restoration through the traditional Hima system;
2. Active restoration through reforestation and agroforestry;
3. Strengthening the supply of ecosystem services such as water, fodder, and NTFPs – such as fuelwood and edible and medicinal plants.

The project will also implement a rainwater harvesting system in the project target areas (around 250 MCM/year). Both contracted labourers and local communities will be involved in implementing the restoration activities under Output 2.1.

#### Active restoration activities will be led by the Project Partners who will oversee local women (predominantly female-headed households) and male farmers (including male and female workers).

The project partners will be trained on the relevant site- and ecosystem-specific protocols to ensure effective implementation in a gender-responsive manner, with particular attention being paid to the role of women as key stakeholders in rainwater harvesting systems. The benefits of the project’s restoration activities will be enhanced by the implementation of gender-sensitive livelihoods interventions that are designed to increase productivity under climate change conditions.

This will include the introduction of agroforestry and water management interventions to enhance soil organic matter, intercept runoff water at relatively short distances (interventions), as well as the conservation of these resources in the target areas. The project will also provide training for women on rainwater harvesting systems, with particular attention being paid to gender-responsive and appropriate for the local context, in addition to being complementary to the watershed restoration activities.

The project will be implemented by the Project Partners (including male farmers) who are experienced in agroforestry and permaculture. It will be carried out on smallholder farms (headed by male and female farmers) at project sites supported by National Agricultural Research Centre (NARC).

#### General information about the project objectives will be implemented on smallholder farms to increase productivity under climate change conditions, providing immediate benefits for local communities, while at the same time contributing to the achievement of the project’s overall objective.

The project will also provide training and technical assistance in the installation of in-field rainwater harvesting systems. These interventions have been designed to build up and scale on-site knowledge and skills among smallholder farmers (headed by male and female farmers) with regard to the development of female farmers. For example, rainwater harvesting has successfully been used in Jordan, in-field water harvesting structures are being implemented as part of the project’s restoration activities.

The project will also provide training and technical assistance on soil organic matter and intercepting runoff water at relatively short distances (interventions). These structures have also been designed to build up and scale on-site knowledge and skills among smallholder farmers with regard to the development of female farmers. The results from a socio-economic impact analysis undertaken for the Baal Bouchik Project, for example, demonstrated that planting shrubs with hawthorn trees (Cerasus) can increase economic internal rate of return (EIRR) estimated at +17%, with the addition of numerous environmental benefits.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.1.1 Implement climate-resilient water management interventions at relevant project sites.

Under sub-activity 2.1.1.1, climate-resilient water management interventions presented in the plans developed for each of the project's intervention sites (Sub-activity 1.4.4) will be implemented in collaboration with local communities and stakeholders. The implementation of these interventions will be monitored by the project team, and the implementation of WSM measures will be promoted. Implementation of the measures will be overseen by the project's technical advisor.

### Sub-activity 2.1.1.1 dependencies:

* Development of participatory climate-resilient integrated water management plans under Sub-activity 1.4.4.
* Establishment of mechanisms required for the implementation of gender-responsive climate resilient additional livelihoods and agricultural practices under Activity 2.1.3.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.1.2 Establish mechanisms required for the implementation of gender-responsive climate-resilient livelihoods — including vegetable gardening (hydroponics and permaculture) and beekeeping — and agricultural practices, as well as water management interventions in collaboration with local communities and stakeholders. The designed and established with support from the National Agricultural Research Council (NARC), WMCs and project TPs.

TPs will provide guidance to WMCs on how to establish a gender-responsive mechanism for the implementation of livelihoods and agricultural practices (to be implemented via Activity 2.1.3), as well as water management interventions in collaboration with local communities and stakeholders. The design of this mechanism is based on the principles outlined by TPs, and the guidance provided thereby will ensure that they fit within the water management system implemented by the project team.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.1.3 Establish and implement community-based participatory mechanisms for monitoring and evaluating change in community climate resilience and behaviour change, including community fora.

Under sub-activity 2.1.1.3, a community-based participatory mechanism will be established to monitor the impact of climate-resilient water management interventions on communities' livelihoods and agricultural practices. This mechanism will include a community forum that will inform and drive the project's knowledge-management interventions implemented under Activity 4.1.2 and will be overseen by WMCs and Local Development Committees (LDCs) with guidance provided by project TPs.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2 Restore ecosystems in the areas of the Yawar, King Talat Dam and Kafarn areas of the Jordan Valley

Sub-activity 2.1.2 consists of four sub-activities aimed at supporting and implementing climate-resilient ecosystem restoration, including the development of protocols, establishment of nurseries and the implementation of small-scale water management interventions to support the restoration of degraded ecosystems in the Yawar area (the extent of forest, rangeland, riparian and wetland ecosystems across the project's three intervention areas (Figure 3)). The extent of vegetation cover will be monitored by the project team using remote sensing data. Sub-activity 2.1.2 also includes the planting of trees in the Kafarn area. The ecosystem restoration interventions will prioritize increasing water resource supply and improving soil quality, as well as enhancing biodiversity and providing habitat for a range of other ecosystem services such as food abundance of the surrounding areas, as well as increased water and fodder supplies to livestock.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 Develop protocols for climate-resilient restoration approaches identified for each project site based on baseline assessments


---
**Page 50**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 46 of 144

## Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 Includes the development of detailed protocols for climate-resilient ecosystem restoration interventions at each site, and the Royal Botanical Garden (RBG). Protocols will be developed for activities including: i) passive restoration, via the traditional Hima system; and ii) active restoration through establishing degraded sites as priority areas for planting. The protocols will include information on species selection, plant species, restoration techniques, planting schedules, maintenance guidelines and recommendations for small-scale nurseries to produce seedlings and propagate plants. These protocols will be developed based on lessons learned from other projects and best practices from other regions. In addition, the protocols will be informed by baseline assessments of the target sites' ecosystems which will include information on soil quality, water availability, climate change projections, vulnerability assessment, and risk assessments, and suitability mapping using appropriate software such as the Open Forest portal (open source forest management software). The development of restoration protocols will be led by the project's TPI in collaboration with relevant stakeholders. The RBG and community-managed nurseries will also be consulted during the development of restoration protocols to ensure that traditional knowledge is considered.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.2 Implement climate-resilient restoration of degraded wetlands at sites across J. 3,178 ha within the Yarmouk 2 km south to the King Talal Site, and 4,512 ha within the Sultan Site.

#### V2. Sub-activity 2.1.2.2 will be implemented in year 2 of project implementation. Multi-use and climate-resilient plant species that can tolerate drought conditions without irrigation will be planted on degraded wetlands at sites across J. 3,178 ha within the Yarmouk 2 km south to the King Talal Site, and 4,512 ha within the Sultan Site. The selection of these species will be based on the site's ecology, climate change projections, and local community needs. These sites are located at the RBG and community-managed nurseries (primarily managed by women) — at least 75% representation of women.

#### 3.2.1.2.3 The restoration will use as many indigenous species as possible because species diversity will improve resiliency/resilience of the ecosystem during extreme climatic conditions. While most of the restoration will occur by passive means, some active restoration may be required to ensure that the restored wetlands are not dominated by too dry and/or too drought-tolerant natural regeneration to be successful. The exact mix of planting versus natural regeneration will depend on the site's ecology, climate change projections, and local community needs. Active restoration will enhance the livelihoods and well-being of the local communities through climate change adaptation. Some of the active restoration interventions may include the use of mulch (e.g., straw) to retain moisture in the soil, or planting (for example, timber from low-growing, hardwood species). Climate change will take place over the ensuing decades, and therefore, the selection of plant species for each site will be based on climate change projections. Scheduling schedules for each selected plant species will also be included in the protocols developed under Sub-activity 2.1.2.1.

#### 3.2.1.2.4 Climate change projections in the design of restoration protocols will climate-proof business-as-usual restoration interventions.

### Sub-activity 2.1.2.2.1 Development of restoration protocols under Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 — completed in year 1.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.2.2.2 Establishment of a minimum number of the RBG and community-managed nurseries (including the provision of seed and seedlings of species identified under 2.1.2.1 at each intervention site to grow the seedlings required for climate-resilient restoration.
