## Sub-activity 2.1.2.3: Implement small-scale rainwater harvesting interventions at restoration sites to enhance the resilience of livestock and agricultural practices

### 126. Female-headed community-managed nurseries will be established — supported by project Technical Partners — at restoration sites for providing seedlings for restoration activities. A central nursery will be established at the RBG to provide seed and seedling stock for the community-managed nurseries. Plant species to be propagated at the nurseries include those that are drought-tolerant, water-efficient, and have high ecological value. The community-managed nurseries for enhancing biodiversity and ecosystem services have only gained traction in the past decade in Jordan.

### 127. Sub-activity 2.1.2.3.2: Implement small-scale rainwater harvesting interventions at restoration sites to enhance the resilience of livestock and agricultural practices

#### 128. Plant species lists in restoration protocols developed under Sub-activity 2.1.2.1 — completed in year 1.

##### Sub-activity 2.1.2.3.2: Implement small-scale rainwater harvesting interventions at restoration sites to enhance the resilience of livestock and agricultural practices

129. Inter-site restoration will be complemented by the implementation of small-scale, in-field rainwater harvesting (RWH) systems on the restored lands. These systems are simple structures that can be constructed using locally available materials (e.g., plastic pipe, hose and pipe) within the restoration sites, improving soil moisture and accordingly plant establishment (see Section 7 of Annex 2 for more details). The design and implementation of rainwater harvesting interventions on the restoration sites will be detailed in the restoration protocols (Sub-activity 2.1.2.1).

### Sub-activity 2.1.3: Introduce gender-responsive climate-resilient additional livelihoods and agricultural practices in the project area

#### Under Activity 1.3, climate-resilient gender-responsive additional livelihoods and agricultural practices will be introduced to enhance the resilience of households that are heavily reliant on livestock production as their primary livelihoods which are highly reliant on water resources and responsible for ecosystem degradation.

##### This approach complements the existing livelihoods in the project area by introducing new livelihoods, such as beekeeping (see Annex 7). #### Under Activity 1.3, climate-resilient gender-responsive additional livelihoods and agricultural practices will be introduced to enhance the resilience of households that are heavily reliant on livestock production as their primary livelihoods which are highly reliant on water resources and responsible for ecosystem degradation.

##### This approach complements the existing livelihoods in the project area by introducing new livelihoods, such as beekeeping (see Annex 7). As a result, the communities will see ongoing benefits from these interventions. The project will only provide technical support to households that have already achieved some level of success with their current livelihoods.

### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1 dependencies:

* Establishment of VMGs under Sub-activity 1.4.1—completed in year 1.
* Establishment of mechanisms required for the implementation of climate-resilient additional livelihoods under Sub-activity 1.5.1.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.2 Technical partner site visits will promote and train local communities (at least 75% women) on vegetable gardening through hydroponics and permaculture (wicking beds as an additional livelihood).

* **Hydroponics:** Hydroponic systems are expected to be established at 30 households per km, while the remaining 150 households will benefit from the establishment of microsystem units.
* **Permaculture:** Permaculture will be introduced at beneficiary households in the form of wicking beds. Specifically for the use of hydroponics and permaculture, training sessions are planned for several weeks, depending on the climate, season and location.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.2.1 Hydroponic systems will be established at 50% of beneficiary households (at least 75% female-headed). Depending on the situation and needs at each of the beneficiary households, either hydroponics, permaculture, or a combination of both may be introduced.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.2.2 Permaculture will be introduced at beneficiary households in the form of wicking beds. Wicking beds consist of a container with water and soil that is kept moist by capillary action. The moisture content of the soil can reach up to 80%, which means that evaporation from the soil surface is reduced because of its dry soil surface.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.2.3 Training will be provided on the technical design and delivery of the vegetable gardening systems, including hydroponics and permaculture (wicking beds as an additional livelihood). These are presented in Section B of Annex 2. Training of communities will include four 2-day training workshops at each of the project sites in years 2 and 5 (4 days).

* It is expected that hydroponic systems will be established at 30 households per km, while the remaining 150 households will benefit from the establishment of microsystem units.
* Permaculture will be introduced at beneficiary households in the form of wicking beds. Wicking beds consist of a container with water and soil that is kept moist by capillary action. The moisture content of the soil can reach up to 80%, which means that evaporation from the soil surface is reduced because of its dry soil surface.
* Wicking beds of 1.5 m x 1.5 m will be constructed with beneficiary households, and each bed will contain a maximum of six plants per year per plant.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.2.4 Each beneficiary household will be equipped with 10 wicking beds. Inputs for the establishment of a wicking bed include: (a) soil; (b) seeds; (c) peat moss, wood ash or cow manure (the best); (d) plastic lining; (e) PVC piping; (f) complete set; and (g) wicking seedlings.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.2.5 A hydroponics and permaculture expert will be present to oversee the technical design and delivery of the vegetable gardening systems, including hydroponics and permaculture (wicking beds as an additional livelihood). These are presented in Section B of Annex 2. Training of communities will include four 2-day training workshops at each of the project sites in years 2 and 5 (4 days).

* It is recommended that garlic and onions be grown as plant disease control measures.

Sub-activity 2.1.3.3. Technical partner site visits to promote the update of climate-resilient agricultural practices on 1,200 smallerholder farms across the three intervention areas to improve productivity under climate change conditions:
   - Climate-resilient agricultural techniques promoted on 1,200 smallerholder farms through the project will include permanent soil cover (e.g., mulching), intercropping, use of drought-tolerant and heat-resistant crop varieties and post-harvest processing to reduce food loss and waste from year 2 to 6. Broken down across sites, these techniques will be promoted on 400 smallerholder farms from Kigali, 350 smallerholder farms from Gikongoro, and 450 smallerholder farms from Kiru.
   - Smallholder farmers from Kiru, Gikongoro, and Kigali will be selected through a series of selection criteria. The selection process will ensure that the majority of the beneficiaries are women. Selection criteria will include: (i) representation of farmers from across each of the three intervention areas; (ii) willingness to participate in project activities; (iii) willingness to share knowledge and experiences, promoting autonomous upscaling; (iv) willingness of farmers to adopt innovative agricultural practices; and (v) willingness of farmers to participate in training.

139. **Sub-activity 2.1.3.3 dependencies:**
   - Establishment of WMCs under Sub-activity 1.1.4.1 – completed in year 1.
   - Establishment of mechanisms required for the implementation of climate-resilient additional livelihoods under Sub-activity 2.1.2.2 – completed in year 2.

Sub-activity 2.1.3.4. Train selected smaller-scale farmers within the three project sites on the use and maintenance of improved water harvesting, conservation, and horticultural implements:
   - With support from the MWCs, MAE, NAMIC, and project Technical Partners, the smallerholder farmers selected under Sub-activity 2.1.3.3 will be trained in the use and maintenance of improved water harvesting, conservation, and horticultural implements.
   - A gender-inclusive training will take place on demonstrations at each of the three intervention sites: Kigali (50 women and men), Gikongoro (40 women and men), and Kiru (30 women and men).
   - Demonstration workshops for 1,200 smallerholder farmers during year 2 to 6.
   - The demonstrations will be held on selected farms in each of the three intervention sites. Each farm will have at least 50 beneficiaries.
   - All beneficiaries/farmers will be supported by the project to implement and manage their farms on their own.

141. An integrated approach for the promotion of climate-resilient agricultural practices, including improved water harvesting and conservation measures. These measures will include (as informed by consultations with WMCs): (i) construction of small-scale water harvesting structures (e.g., dams, ponds, tanks); (ii) use of improved horticultural implements (e.g., pits; vi) Micro-belts; (vii) grass, shrub and tree root-trunk strips; (viii) use of AI extension and pest and disease management services; (ix) establishment of small-scale water harvesting structures; (x) construction of terraces; and (xi) will also receive agricultural inputs to facilitate the implementation of climate-resilient agriculture approaches, including: improved seeds, fertilizers, and pesticides. The project will provide training on the use and maintenance of horticultural implements such as watering cans, hose wheels and sprinklers; and its equipment to implement in-field water harvesting and conservation techniques.

This will also include standardizing product quality, facilitating access to markets, and providing technical assistance in the development of micro-enterprises where feasible, promoting the sustainability of income-generating livelihoods.

**Output 2.2 - Water Fund established in the Jordan Valley to maintain landscape investment for enhanced water resources**

Under the proposed project, a Water Fund will be established as a model for the payment for ecosystem services (PES) in the Jordan Valley. The Water Fund will provide financial incentives for private sector landowners (agricultural and commercial) and public sector stakeholders (municipalities) for restoration (EIA) interventions in watersheds, including afforestation, reforestation, and rehabilitation of degraded lands. These interventions are to be implemented in the three large watersheds and as such promote a continued supply of ecosystem services – particularly those related to water resources – that will benefit downstream communities. The Water Fund will provide incentives for these communities to reduce practices that result in compromised water quality and quantity downstream, and thereby serve as custodians of the ecosystems in these areas. The general model of a Water Fund is presented in the figure below.

![](Figure 8 Water Fund model)

146. Several studies undertaken in Jordan have demonstrated a substantial willingness-to-pay (WTP) for the preservation and provisioning of environmental resources. For example, local communities in the Dead Sea region showed a WTP of $20 per year to preserve the Dead Sea ecosystem services such as water supply, protection of water for domestic and agricultural consumption(1). This study highlighted that the long-term benefits of improving the environment are more valuable than short-term gains from land-use activities. The willingness-to-pay (WTP) is compared with the short-term gains from business-as-usual activities in the region(2). In 2014, an economic valuation of the Dead Sea ecosystem services was conducted by the ELD initiative for the Jordan River and Dead Sea Basin(3). Springet, Dordrecht. Depending on the area of the land to be restored, the WTP per hectare is $500–$700. The economic evaluation shows that this value adequately reflects the social marginal WTP for water because of a direct connection between the quality and quantity of water resources and human well-being(4). The methodology used in the WTP of pastoralized communities for the restoration of rangeland ecosystems was determined under the implementation

## Anchored in the JVA, the Water Fund will be governed by a multi-stakeholder public-private steering committee that will oversee the design and establishment of the Water Fund, as well as its launch externally, with Technical Partners, government partners (such as MWR, MoEiW and MoA), water utilities (such as the Yarmouk Water Company), private sector companies, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders. The steering committee will provide oversight for: a) a governance structure that includes representation from all key stakeholders; b) a decision-making mechanism housed within the JVA for the collection and provision of resources for watershed conservation with a focus on water quality and quantity; c) a framework for engaging with private sector companies as technical partners for planning and decision-making; and d) a mechanism for watershed management also linked to the WMCs and other stakeholders. The steering committee will be responsible for overseeing the Water Fund’s design, which will include an on-grant financing and the Water Fund's grant mechanism. The design and establishment of the Water Fund will be completed within two years from the date of approval by the GCF Board. The steering committee will oversee the financial aspects of the design and operationalization of the Water Fund during its first two years, while also overseeing the implementation of the project.

### During the establishment, operation and mature phases of the Water Fund, capital provided by stakeholders including through PES, can be invested in financial markets through a trust fund, with returns on investment used to provide ongoing funding for the Water Fund. The steering committee will ensure that the trust fund is managed in such a way as to maximize its return while maintaining its value over time. This will allow for the continued conservation and attenuation of water resources in the project's intervention areas[1]. The use of a trust fund will also ensure that the interests of all stakeholders are protected, including those of smallholder producers can be certain that the multi-annual commitments of the fund will be fulfilled, and ensuring the credibility of the Water Fund. In addition to financial investments, other forms of capital such as human resources, technical expertise, government resources — including financial resources obtained from the country's national budget for natural resource management, and private sector companies can also be invested in the trust fund. Other strategies to capitalize the Water Fund may include: voluntary private donations — including funding from individuals, philanthropic organizations, foundations, and other entities; public-private partnerships (PPPs) with the private sector; government resources — including financial resources obtained from the country's national budget for natural resource management and protection; or investments in the trust fund by the World Bank. Depending on the terms of the agreement between the WMCs and the Water Fund, the WWUVA could potentially invest capital into the trust fund to support projects such as water facilities, hydropower/companies, or interest/sectoral organizations.

### Mechanisms for financing the Water Fund will be developed in accordance with the project implementation strategy, which will include securing agreements with major water users who are willing to pay for ongoing ecosystem management and protection. The steering committee will also consider other mechanisms such as payments to beekeepers such as those supported under Activity 21.3.2. Payments for ongoing ecosystem management would be made by the Water Fund to beekeepers in exchange for their continued participation in the project, with water resources generated via the Fund's interventions in upstream ecosystems. These include large-scale agricultural production and livestock farming, which are major users of water in Jordan[2], as well as the industrial sector (e.g., fertilizer producers, potash and phosphate mining, thermal power generation) that also use significant amounts of water to meet their demand, to continue to grow[3]. Potential payers may include agricultural producers such as the Alfeigh Group[4] and Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan Water Company[5], the Yarmouk Water Company[6] and Jordan Phosphate Mines Company[7], as well as water utilities such as the Yarmouk Water Company.

151. In terms of Water Fund viability, cost-benefit analysis of a reference Water Fund based on a 30-year horizon, with an investment of US$ 10 million being disbursed at a rate of US $1 million per year for 10 years, shows that the project will be viable in terms of net present value (NPV) and internal rate of return (IRR). However, this is a relatively long period; it reflects the spread of investment over a 15-year period[26]. In terms of size and replenishment scenario, the Water Fund can be considered as a medium-term project.

## Low replenishment scenario

Following an initial GCF investment of US$7,500 million via the Fund’s grant mechanism during project implementation (disbursed at a rate up to US $1,500,000 per year), this will need to be replenished annually with additional funding from other sources in order for the Fund to continue. Essentially, US$ 1,500 million per year will continue to be available through the Fund following project completion.

### Medium replenishment scenario

This includes the low replenishment scenario described above plus increased annual investment of at least US $2,750,000 annually from other sources in order for the Fund to continue. This could include a combination of public and private sector funding, including contributions from a water user is the Jordan Valley Authority (JVA). Assuming annual revenue of at least US$ 15,000,000 per year from the JVA, this would result in an annual investment of US $27,500 annually.

### High replenishment scenario

This includes the medium replenishment scenario described above plus a stronger enabling environment supporting policy and subsidy reform in the water sector that enhances the ability to attract private sector funding. This could include contributions from the JVA or ILWRM as an adaptation approach generated under Output 2.1 fosters interest in the Water Fund from multiple stakeholders. In addition, if there is sufficient demand for the Water Fund, there is potential for an annual investment of at least US$ 10,000,000 in the Water Fund annually.

### Sub-activity: 2.1.4.1, Design the Water Fund in a participatory manner, including workshops with local communities, institutions, private sector and NGOs—Technical Assistance (TA).

153. The design phase will be initiated by project Technical Partners, with technical support from the Nature Conservancy (TNC) and WRIsCA. During this phase lessons and best practices from other Water Funds around the world will be taken into account. The design phase will also include a review of the existing institutional arrangements for the Fund which will then seek to leverage financial support from stakeholders and investors (including the private sector) for long-term maintenance of the Water Fund. This will also include identifying and engaging with downstream water users, including agricultural producers such as those supported under Activity 2.1.3), as well as identifying and engaging with downstream water service providers such as those supported by TNC’s Water Finance Facility (WFF). The design phase will also seek to identify eligibility criteria for accessing the Water Fund. Preliminary eligibility criteria are presented in Table 7, ToF for the design of the Water Fund.

## Activity Scope

### Sub-activity 2.1.2 - Design an on-granting facility (under UNEP's on-granting accreditation) for the implementation of Water Fund projects in the LWRM pilot sites in the context of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) in support of the GCF RE TA

The on-granting facility will be designed and established during year 2 of the project. The facility will allow WMCs and their members (including local communities and farmers) to implement sub-projects that are aligned with the Water Fund's strategic objectives, as well as other projects in line with the GCF RE TA. A detailed description is presented in Table 6 below. The on-granting facility will target sub-projects at the community level which fall into one of three categories: (i) water resource management; (ii) climate change adaptation and mitigation; or (iii) climate change conditions. Sub-projects will focus on ecosystem management and stewardship activities that augment water resources, improve water quality, and reduce pollution in priority watersheds. The facility is aligned with the Water Fund's strategic objectives as well as other projects in line with the GCF RE TA.

The on-granting facility will be established by UNEP in its capacity as AE. A detailed description of the on-granting facility will be prepared during the Water Fund's design phase in Sub-activity 2.1.4.1. The on-granting facility will be open to all contributions and is expected to take over the on-granting mechanism as the exit mechanism for the GCF RE TA. Funds will be transferred from GCF to UNEP to IUCN for on-granting (see Figure 6). At no point in time, shall the on-granting facility be non-repayable/non-remunerative in nature.

## Sub-activity 2.1.4.6: Develop and disseminate demand-based RFPs for IWWM sub-projects under the on-granting facility to VMCA and their members

### 160. Access to funding
Funding will be accessed through requests for proposals (RFPs) for sub-projects for the implementation of SLMW measures across the project's three target watersheds. IUCN will launch demand-based RFPs in collaboration with VMCA and its members, including women's cooperatives and other civil society organisations such as women's cooperatives and non-government organisations (Table 6). The RFPs will focus on building capacity of local communities to manage water resources sustainably under climate change conditions. IUCN and project PTPs will consult with community representatives in the Jordan Valley's water resources under climate change conditions, IUCN and project PTPs will consult with community representatives in the Jordan Valley's water resources under climate change conditions, and prepare the RFPs accordingly. This will include a series of design/technical studies to help optimise which set of LWMW measures are most appropriate for each sub-project.

Implementing sub-projects will help build community and local capacity in the design and implementation of LWMW measures at small scale. The project will provide technical assistance, training, and mentoring to implementers. Implementing sub-projects will demonstrate tangible results of climate-resilient LWMW measures at a small-scale, building a case for scaling up these measures across the Jordan Valley's water resources under climate change conditions.

The RFPs will be developed in consultation with VMCA and its members (particularly women and youth) to provide support to the VMCA and their members (particularly women and youth) for grants and the selection criteria. Additionally, the structure of the proposals will be defined, and a template provided.

### Sub-activity 2.14.8.1: Dependencies
- **The Water Fund will be implemented through the development and implementation of long-term financing and scaling-up strategies. These strategies will focus on: (a) identifying potential investors, including private sector entities, to provide long-term funding for the Water Fund; (b) engaging and educated during the design phase (aspects of the long-term financing strategy are described in more detail below); (c) incorporating a business case analysis into the project design that will be used as an investment tool; (d) ensuring ownership; ii) monitoring and evaluating progress systematically and applying adaptive management where required; and iv) identifying potential investors, including private sector entities, to provide long-term funding for the Water Fund.**

### Sub-activity 2.14.8.2: Dependencies
- **The long-term financing strategy for the Water Fund will be distinct from a business case analysis, which only focuses on whether there will be financial benefit for investors. At the heart of the long-term financing strategy will be the identification and engagement of private sector entities that are willing to invest in the Water Fund and downstream water users. This will include securing agreements with major water users who are willing to pay for improved water quality, quantity, and reliability. These agreements would also provide a mechanism for adaptive management in upstream watersheds which could improve water availability and reduce pressure on downstream ecosystems (see above for additional details). These include large-scale agricultural producers such as commercial fruit growers, dairy farmers, and livestock producers; the industrial sector (e.g., fertilizer producers, potash and phosphate mining, thermal power plants and cement factories); and the urban sector (e.g., hotels, restaurants, and other businesses that use water to support their operations or grow crops). Potential payers may include agricultural producers such as the Alghaif Group178 and Haddadin Farms179.**

## Long-term financing strategy and a detailed model describing how the Water Fund's PES scheme can be utilized to channel finance into the Fund’s grant mechanism (see below) once GCF finance is no longer available post project.

This will comprise details on lessons learned and experiences generated during the project implementation, including the development of the Water Fund’s governance structure (e.g., Water Fund Board, Water Fund Secretariat, Development Bank), as well as leverage and unlock additional domestic finance and development banks which would contribute to long-term financing for the Water Fund.

## Long-term financing strategy will also consider how investments in water resources in the country can be linked into and integrated into the Water Fund, as well as potential future water funds in Jordan, supporting its scaling up and replication. The Water Fund’s long-term financing strategy will include three components: a) a community perspective, WMCs; b) through Component 2, identify investment opportunities and develop plans for sub-catchments within the watersheds of the Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Karak areas of the Jordan River Basin. The Water Fund, the plan will be considered for inclusion in the long-term financing strategy. Component 2 activities through WMCs will include: i) support to communities to develop their own water management plans; ii) support to communities to identify investment opportunities and develop plans for sub-catchments within the watersheds of the Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Karak areas of the Jordan River Basin. The Water Fund, the plan will be considered for inclusion in the long-term financing strategy.

### Activity 2.1.1. Support the implementation of participatory climate-resilient integrated watershed management plans (WMCs) through WMCs. These plans should include a clear mandate for using funds only for the purpose of the Water Fund and will be developed.

#### Activity 2.1.2. Restore ecosystems at the watersheds of the Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Karak areas of the Jordan River Basin. This activity will focus on restoring degraded lands in these areas through the establishment of additional sites and species for planting, supporting targeted investment for effective scaling up and replication.

#### Activity 2.1.3. Introduce climate-resilient livelihoods and agricultural practices in the project’s three intervention areas. Based on the success of this activity in the successful scaling of restoration activities, investments will be developed to scale up and replicate these activities in other locations for investment in the future.


## Development of a long-term financing strategy will be undertaken by a financial expert (under the oversight of the Water Fund manager – see section 2.14.1) who should have prior experience in the design of such a strategy, and should preferably also be involved in long-term financial studies relating to the Water Fund. The following principles will guide this process:

### Securing payments to the fund under its PES model (major water users including commercial agriculture, industry and households).

### The sources and intended use of the funds will be clearly defined.

### A mandate for using only for the purpose of the Water Fund will be established.

### Transparency and scrutiny by fund members will be encouraged.

### The Water Fund will be registered as a separate legal entity and will obtain all necessary permits in line with regulations of the country.

### Management structure and policies will be developed.

### A clear mandate for using funds only for the purpose of the Water Fund will be established.

### A clear and effective management structure will be developed and approved by all Water Fund members.

173. **Objectives for scaling up and replicating the Water Fund to other locations of Jordan will also be assessed through the development of a scaling-up strategy, aligning closely with the long-term financing strategy. Among others this scaling-up strategy will consider how the Water Fund can contribute to the sustainability of other GCF investments in Jordan. The Water Fund will also assess the potential for scaling up its activities and replicating them at other locations through consultations with its AE (PA), EEs and project management unit (PMU). During the development of the scaling-up strategy, the Water Fund will consider the potential for scaling up its activities to other locations in Jordan where downstream water users would benefit from cost and pay increased water resource supplies resulting from improved water use efficiency and management of upstream ecosystems. **Component 3: Improved protection, supply and use of water resources under conditions of climate change**

**Outcome 2: Enhanced water security for households in target areas**

**Output 1: Water availability is increased through improved water management practices under changing conditions.**

* **With a baseline of 125 cliklars of water available per capita – 375 cliklars being the internationally accepted water poverty threshold – Jordan’s water resources are already stretched to their limits. The supply of renewable water by spring is 410 Mcklars, resulting in abstraction of non-renewable groundwater sources. Consequently, the country has been experiencing a steady decline in per capita water availability from 250 cliklars per person between 2007 and 2017. Water loss through evaporation is a considerable challenge in Jordan, accounting for approximately 40% of total water use. The country’s high population density (386 people per km²) combined with its high evaporation loss, which accounts for 60% of global surface water use. Jordan’s high evaporation rate has ramifications for the sustainability of agriculture and other water uses in the country. In addition to the abstraction of groundwater and its irrigation canals. Most notably these include the Zardat, Yarmouk and Zarqa Rivers which are 105 km, 92 km and 84 km long respectively. The Jordan River is also a major source of water for agriculture in the country. However, as irrigation rates in the country, the use of rainwater harvesting is a particularly effective approach to overcoming challenges related to water scarcity. Rainwater harvesting has been practiced in Jordan since ancient times. In the case of well drilling and piping in the 1940s, the entire population of Jordan was dependent on rainwater harvesting for domestic purposes. The practice of rainwater harvesting has declined over time due to the introduction of piped water harvesting; however, rainwater harvesting remains the only source of potable water for many rural households in Jordan. Rainwater harvesting is also practiced by farmers and pastoralists who use it as a source of irrigation water. In addition to these traditional structures, there are now a number of modern rainwater harvesting structures in Jordan, including 15 (68%) IBCs; 129 ponds; and two dams. Ponds account for a total of 70% of the volume harvested by rainwater harvesting structures in Jordan. Further details about these common rainwater harvesting technologies in Jordan are provided in Section 6.8 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 61 of 144

## Outline of the project

### Background and Objectives

The project aims to enhance water security in Jordan by improving the quality and quantity of surface and groundwater, restoring vegetation and building natural check dams, as well as establishing rockfall dams to better capture rainwater runoff and contribute to aquifer recharge. This will be complemented by improved land management practices such as agroforestry and soil conservation techniques.

### Project Components

1. **Rainwater Harvesting Systems**: The project proposes the installation of 30,000 rainwater harvesting tanks with a capacity of 32 cubic meters each (at least 50% female-headed) to capture runoff from rooftops and other surfaces in urban areas.
2. **Solar Panels**: The installation of photovoltaic solar panels on the King Abdullah II Bridge will reduce water loss due to evaporation by minimizing the temperature of the surface water body.

### Expected Outcomes

1. **Improved Water Security**: Rainwater harvesting systems are expected to increase the availability of drinking water and improve the resilience of communities in terms of increased duration, frequency, and intensity of droughts under climate change conditions.
2. **Enhanced Community Involvement**: The project will contribute to GCF OUTCOME AT07 by strengthening adaptive capacity and reducing exposure to adverse effects of climate change through improved land management practices such as agroforestry and soil conservation techniques.

## Improving the community-level use and management of water under climate change conditions, gender-responsive community-based water monitoring and management plans will be developed for the largest areas.

These plans will ensure social accountability for water usage within beneficiary communities. The development and implementation of these plans will be guided by the principles outlined in the 2012 Guidelines for assessment and implementation of groundwater aquifer recharge (GAR) in semi-arid regions, particularly focusing on effective groundwater use in the Amazon Basin and Arica region.

### Implementing Measures to Improve the Quality and Quantity of Ground and Surface Water Supplies within the Project Area

At each of the project's three sites, interventions will be implemented to enhance the quality and quantity of ground water supplies. These measures include:

- **Construction of rock dams**: This involves building rock dams with a capacity ranging from 0.5 to 9 MCM/year.
- **Aquifer recharge**: Managed aquifer recharge (MAR) will be implemented by constructing rock dams to collect and store rainwater, which can then be used for irrigation or other purposes.

### Sub-activities

#### Sub-activity 1.1: Establish rock dams at 72 sites in target areas (with a target rate of 30% capture)

- **Rock dam construction**: This will involve building rock dams with the capacity to store up to 9 MCM/year.
- **Aquifer recharge**: MAR will be implemented by constructing rock dams to collect and store rainwater, which can then be used for irrigation or other purposes.

#### Sub-activity 1.2: Managed aquifer recharge (MAR)

- **Rock dam construction**: This involves building rock dams with a capacity ranging from 0.5 to 9 MCM/year.
- **Aquifer recharge**: MAR will be implemented by constructing rock dams to collect and store rainwater, which can then be used for irrigation or other purposes.

### Environmental and Social Risk Assessments

Environmental and social risk assessments will also be undertaken as part of feasibility assessments to ensure that the project is sustainable. These assessments will cover:

- **Noise and air pollution**: Interference with noise and air quality.
- **Landscape degradation**: Reduction in downstream water flows due to land use changes.
- **Water quality**: Potential sources of contamination, such as agricultural runoff or industrial waste.
- **Disturbance of archaeological sites**: Interference with historical artifacts.
- **Land tenure and exclusion of women**: Land ownership issues and the exclusion of women from land rights.
- **Flow availability in streams**: Reduction in water flow due to land use changes.
- **Water source quality**: Potential sources of contamination, such as agricultural runoff or industrial waste.
- **Permits for land use**: Need for permits related to land use.
- **Licensing or rights pertaining to land use and site suitability for technical design**: Licensing requirements for technical designs.

### Mitigation Measures

The following mitigation measures will be implemented:

1. **Noise and air pollution**: Implement noise reduction technologies and monitor air quality regularly.
2. **Landscape degradation**: Restore degraded landscapes through reforestation and soil conservation practices.
3. **Water quality**: Implement water treatment systems to reduce contamination.
4. **Disturbance of archaeological sites**: Avoid construction in areas with historical significance or relocate structures as needed.
5. **Land tenure and exclusion of women**: Ensure land rights are respected and provide training for women on land management.
6. **Flow availability in streams**: Maintain natural flow patterns through the use of water diversions and storage systems.
7. **Water source quality**: Implement water treatment systems to reduce contamination.
8. **Permits for land use**: Obtain necessary permits before commencing construction activities.
9. **Licensing or rights pertaining to land use and site suitability for technical design**: Ensure all required licenses are obtained.

### Implementation

Further details on the implementation of rock dam construction and aquifer recharge measures can be found in Section 8.2 of Annex II, with additional information provided in Annex III regarding the potential impacts of rock dams. The guidelines for assessment and implementation of groundwater aquifer recharge (GAR) in semi-arid regions are outlined in Section 6.12 of Annex II.

It is important to note that while these measures may provide drinking water, they also facilitate other uses such as irrigation and livestock watering.

- Development of restoration protocols under Sub-activity 2.1.2 — completed in year 1.
- Rehabilitation active freshwater springs across the project sites (Yarmouk and Kafarina).
- Rehabilitation of approximately five springs at each site during years 6 and 7 of the project.

### 188. Sub-activities under Activity 3.1.2 will be aimed at reducing evaporation of surface water resources and increasing electricity supplies through floating solar panel arrays.
- Using suspended or floating covers has the potential to reduce evaporation by up to 90% in Jordan Valley (Jordan River), evaporation reduction measures can be implemented on the King Abdullah II Dam, and on the Naimada irrigation canal. The floating solar panels will be connected to the power grid at year 3 of the project to support additional electricity generation.
- Floating solar panels have not been used in Jordan before the proposed project.

### 189. Sub-activity 3.1.2.1 will include the establishment of at least 5 MW of floating solar panels over the King Abdullah II Dam, and 40 MW of floating solar panels on the Naimada irrigation canal (Jordan Valley).
- A local electrical engineering firm, with specialization in large-scale solar installations, will oversee the design and installation.
- The project is expected to generate approximately 1.5 million kWh per year from the King Abdullah II Dam annually from the canal and provide a 1 MW of power per year to the MWI’s pumping stations on the canal.
- A co-investment model with private sector partners has been identified, which will help to finance the project, contributing to a transformative change in energy supply across the water sector in Jordan and contributing to a reduction in greenhouse gas emissions.

### 190. The MWI through the JWA will be responsible for operating and maintaining the floating solar installation once in place, and will ensure that the relevant permits for the infrastructure are acquired.

## IMPACT (EU) regulations, an EU is only required for solar installations of 5 MW or above! Consequently, an EU will not be sought for this installation. As the floating solar installation will be placed on an artificial water body (King Abdullah Canal), environmental risk is limited, while water quality is expected to benefit through algal growth suppression and reduced evaporation. The King Abdullah Canal is a shallow canal with low salinity and high levels of freshwater bodies. Algae blooms can be dangerous for human health if they develop in a source of drinking water. Algal blooms can also cause harm to fish, birds, and animals living in the body of water! Section 8.7.2 of Annex 2 provides a detailed review of floating solar.

    * Rainwater harvesting is being introduced as a priority intervention for climate change adaptation, as well as with Jordanian government support.
* An assessment of rooftop rainwater harvesting in northern Jordan indicated that while acknowledging rooftop rainwater harvesting as a key strategy for improving access to clean water under climate change conditions, the study revealed that only 14% of households own a rooftop rainwater harvesting system. The majority, 85%, of those surveyed households believe that government should provide incentives to subsidize the construction of rooftop rainwater harvesting systems. In addition, the assessment also identified through community consultations across the project's target sites that water supply from public taps is not always available and that many households are dependent on groundwater supplies via rooftop rainwater harvesting.
* Activity 11.2: The CMF will provide a total of 3,000 buildings to supplement and reduce the demand on domestic supplies during dry periods (i.e., serving as a complementary source) at the household level, as well as for other uses such as livestock watering and irrigation. This activity is expected to increase water resilience through improved water access! The water will be accessible from the tank as needed, via an outlet tap or spigot. The CMF will provide 100 cubic meters of water per day per household during dry periods. This amount would require 500 liters of supplementary water per day for domestic purposes during the dry season; a full 32 CM coldwater, could provide water, without any recharge, for up to 65 days. ## Sub-activity 3.1.3.1: Install small-scale rooftop rainwater harvesting interventions at target sites in the Jordan Valley

### Life cycle and implementation
The project will be implemented in community/religious centres, 50 institutional buildings and 100 schools in the target intervention sites with a total population of approximately 248,736 people. The criteria for the selection of sub-target sites include: (i) willingness to participate in the project; (ii) willingness to install rainwater harvesting systems, willingness to monitor their water usage and limited access to supplementary domestic water supplies.

### Sub-activity 3.1.3.2: Install small-scale rooftop rainwater harvesting interventions at target sites in the Jordan Valley

(100% of women trained in operation and maintenance)

The project will be implemented on 5,478 CM runoff rainwater harvesting tanks on 2,800 private houses (4–16 people) 80% of the target population; at least 50 female-headed households across the project’s three sub-target sites. The criteria for selection include: (i) willingness to participate in the project for implementation; ii) willingness of owners to pay for maintenance costs; iii) female-headed households; iv) poor housing conditions; v) limited access to supplementary domestic water supplies.

The project will be implemented on 1,028 CM runoff rainwater harvesting tanks on 540 private houses with a person with a disability or more than 6 dependents and households with limited or no access to supplementary domestic water supplies. The criteria for selection include: (i) willingness to participate in the project; ii) willingness of owners to pay for maintenance costs.

The project will take place between years 2 and 4 of project implementation. Local individuals – all of which will be women – will be trained on operation, maintenance and monitoring of rainwater harvesting systems.

### Activity 3.1.4: Empower local communities, including women, to monitor and manage local water supplies more effectively

#### Sub-activity 3.1.4.1: Develop participatory community-based water use monitoring and management plans for project beneficiary communities

Through three sub-activities under Activity 3.1.4.1, community-level management of water supply will be reinforced through the development of participatory community-based water use monitoring and management plans, outreach campaigns and the promotion of additional livelihoods that are related to the monitoring and management of rainwater harvesting systems.

The project will develop a participatory community-based water use monitoring and management plan for each of the 150 target sites. The plan will be developed by local communities with the support of WMCs, including local community members as representatives (at least 50% women). During the project’s implementation period, WMCs will provide technical assistance to the communities in implementing them to the project’s scope, objectives, expected results, as well as their roles – such under Activity 3.1.4. These activities are aimed at ensuring that local communities have a clear understanding of what they need to do and by providing clear project timelines and ensuring a common understanding that the project will not be addressing baseline development challenges, but impacts related to climate change.

#### Sub-activity 3.1.4.2: Develop participatory community-based water use monitoring and management plans for project beneficiary communities

The project will develop a participatory community-based water use monitoring and management plan for each target site with the support of project Technical Partners. WMCs and LGDs – one

## Output 4.1 Improved knowledge and awareness of climate change adaptation for land-based and water resources sectors

### Strengthened mandates and capacity for climate-resilient LWRM in Jordan (Outputs 1.1 and 1.2) will be complemented by the development of knowledge-management tools to facilitate sectoral and cross-sectoral learning and inform the development of policies, strategies, plans, and programs that are gender-responsive. The establishment of a national climate change adaptation framework for land-based and water resources sectors will contribute to CCIP Outcome A3.1 – strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk-reduction processes.

#### Activities under Output 4.1

- **207.** The activities conducted under Output 4.1 will build upon lessons established by both government institutions and civil society organizations in Jordan, including the Ministry of Environment (MoE) and the Jordan River Basin Authority (JRBA). These lessons will be used to achieve its mission to "maintain and improve the quality of Jordan's environment, conserve natural resources, and promote sustainable development." The activities will include: i) strengthening the MoE's capacity for climate change adaptation outreach and the support of decision-making through knowledge management; ii) establishing the governance system including institutional arrangements, roles and responsibilities, and decision-making processes to ensure effective monitoring, as well as by mainstreaming environmental policies into all national development plans; iii) To achieve this vision, the MoE will develop a National Climate Change Adaptation Framework for Land-Based and Water Resources Sectors. The framework will be developed in collaboration with relevant stakeholders and institutionalization of a culture of excellence and raising public awareness and promoting behaviour change for environmental sustainability.

- **208.** To conduct gender-responsive awareness-raising activities, project Technical Partners will be supported by MoE's specialist Outreach and Environmental Awareness unit. In addition to building on and scaling up government-led outreach efforts, the proposed GCF project will support the following: i) promoting best practices in Sustainable Land Management (SLM), and iv) promoting SLM knowledge-management at global and national levels; ii) developing a National Climate Change Adaptation Framework for Land-Based and Water Resources Sectors. The components directly align with the proposed GCF project and in particular Output 4 as they include: i) providing capacity building to MoE staff on climate change adaptation outreach, gender-responsive awareness-raising activities implemented by the proposed project to raise awareness within local communities and support decision-making processes; ii) promoting best practices in Sustainable Land Management (SLM), and iv) promoting SLM knowledge-management at global and national levels. The components directly align with the proposed GCF project and in particular Output 4 as they include: i) providing capacity building to MoE staff on climate change adaptation outreach, gender-responsive awareness-raising activities implemented by the proposed project to raise awareness within local communities and support decision-making processes; ii) promoting best practices in Sustainable Land Management (SLM), and iv) promoting SLM knowledge-management at global and national levels.

- **210.** Through six sub-activities under Activity 4.1.1, awareness of climate change adaptation responses including climate-resilient LWRM – among local communities, including local women, in the Jordan Valley will be strengthened through a combination of outreach activities, training workshops, and capacity building for gender-responsive decision-making. The project will also support the mainstreaming of climate-resilient LWRM into land-use and development-planning processes. Awareness will be raised on the importance of biodiversity as implemented by the Integrated Ecosystem Management in the Jordan Valley. The project will develop a National Climate Change Adaptation Framework for Land-Based and Water Resources Sectors, which will include institutional structures and capacities to promote awareness of sustainable landscape management, including by training over 5,000 farmers and students.

#### Sub-activity 4.1.1.1: Community engagements carried out during the project inception phase in the project's three targeted areas

Community engagements during the project's inception phase (year 1) will focus on introducing and sensitizing target communities to climate change adaptation issues, as well as identifying their needs and priorities. This will be followed by a series of early engagement activities that will be tailored to meet the needs and the realities of different community groups, including women and youth. The focus of these early engagements is on climate change adaptation, avoiding any misconceptions that it is a poverty-alleviation or environmental protection project. Early engagement activities will include awareness raising sessions, training workshops, and field visits. This is promoted. It will include making all relevant project documentation (such as the Funding Proposal, EIA and ESMP, etc.) available to communities in an accessible format. The early engagement activities will be carried out with women, youth, farmers, as well as local decision-makers associated with the agricultural and water sectors in both the Jordan Valley and the Al-Ma'arif Basin. These activities will also include a series of awareness raising sessions by project staff, with support provided by MCD's special Outreach and Environmental Awareness Unit.

#### Sub-activity 4.1.1.2: Upgrade community centres and local institutions (including NGOs) in the Jordan Valley and Al-Ma'arif Basin to serve as focal points for climate change adaptation awareness raising and learning centres

Sub-activity 4.1.1.2 will include equipping community centres and institutions — including mosques, churches, centres, as well as schools — across the Jordan Valley to function as climate change adaptation learning centres and focal points for outreach activities in their respective communities.

Selected community members from across the Jordan Valley will be trained (at least 50% women), and stationed at each of these centres. The training will focus on increasing the capacity of the selected community members to promote climate change adaptation-related adaptation responses during the implementation of the project's outreach programme, as described in Sub-activity 4.1.4. As part of this sub-activity, the project will also establish a system for the exchange and sharing of best practices of GIZ's Regional System. To align regions' centres, the project will build on the lessons learned and best practices of GIZ's Regional System to develop a regional outreach programme that can be used by other projects in the region.

#### Sub-activity 4.1.1.3: Public figures and local communities as focal points for climate change adaptation in Jordan Valley and Al-Ma'arif Basin

To stimulate interest from the local population in the outreach programme, public figures will be identified and trained to serve as focal points for outreach activities within their respective communities. The training will promote and contribute to the outreach programme through the locally available media, implemented under Sub-activity 4.1.3. The training will focus on increasing the capacity of the selected community members to: i) promote climate change adaptation; ii) baseline climate change impact and adaptation knowledge; iii) understand the project's objectives and activities; iv) identify the benefits and impacts of locally appropriate adaptation approaches (especially EIA and ESMP); v) understand ongoing development challenges that are exacerbated by climate change; and vi) understanding the design of the project, including objectives and activities. The training will be facilitated by the project's Technical Partners.

#### Sub-activity 4.1.1.4: Site- and ecosystem-specific gender-responsive climate change adaptation outreach programme in Jordan Valley and Al-Ma'arif Basin

The outreach programme shared via the outreach programme is contextually relevant to different areas across the Jordan Valley; it will be tailored to site- and ecosystem-specific and gender-responsive, through ongoing engagement with local communities. The training will focus on increasing the capacity of the selected community members to: i) promote climate change adaptation; ii) baseline climate change impact and adaptation knowledge; iii) understand the project's objectives and activities; iv) identify the benefits and impacts of locally appropriate adaptation approaches (especially EIA and ESMP); v) understand ongoing development challenges that are exacerbated by climate change; and vi) understanding the design of the project, including objectives and activities. The training will be facilitated by the project's Technical Partners.

The design of the programme will identify how best locally available media, such as radio, television and social media can be used to promote awareness raising on climate change issues and adaptation approaches via theatrical performances to relevant stakeholders, including schools.

## At-task tools will be developed under Sub-activity A.1.2.1 in year 1 of project implementation.

### Investments in knowledge management will enable sharing of information on global best practices, facilitating the gathering and sharing of lessons learned and evidence gathered during the project, and underpinning the integration of climate change adaptation into Jordan’s national development plans.
#### Tools to be developed include a content repository to manage and share knowledge context, a knowledge search tool, a communication tool (such as a blog or wiki) for sharing information on best practices and lessons learned (including as a smartphone application), and a knowledge visualization tool to visually communicate knowledge. As possible, AI will be used in the development of these tools.
##### The project will also develop a climate change adaptation knowledge repository, a publicly accessible online database, which will be housed and maintained by the Jordan Water Fund. This database will include lessons learned from the project’s implementation. Lessons learned from the restoration of wetlands, improved gender-responsive livelihoods and strengthened community-based water use and monitoring plans will be promoted through the knowledge repository, ensuring coverage of women and other vulnerable groups’ experiences.
##### Access to and use of the knowledge repository will be promoted by providing training for key stakeholders (including women) – will be promoted by project Technical Partner websites and social media pages. Within the project’s implementation period, a series of workshops will be organized in Jordan and neighboring countries (including women) – will be promoted by project Technical Partner websites and social media pages.
##### The project will also develop a knowledge management system to support the Water Fund’s community-based water use and monitoring plans and training of community members (100% women) to monitor and evaluate their own projects. The project will also develop a communication tool to promote the Water Fund’s campaign to promote awareness of the benefits of climate change adaptation among rural communities and stakeholders in Jordan.

### Sub-activity A.4.2.2: Collect lessons and best practices across Jordan (including on the project’s ILWRM investments) and between states in the Mediterranean region and Middle East, as well as those generated on the project’s long-term monitoring plan.
#### Knowledge tools developed under sub-activity A.1.2.1, including the national climate change adaptation knowledge repository, will be used to collect lessons learned from the project’s climate-resilient ILWRM investments and the Water Fund’s long-term monitoring plans (developed under Sub-activity A.4.2.3 – completed in year 2).
##### The project will develop a design and implement a long-term monitoring plan for the generation of lessons learned on the project’s climate-resilient ILWRM investments.
#### Lessons generated from the project’s climate-resilient ILWRM investments and the Water Fund’s operationalization of the Water Fund will be generated through a rigorous, frequent and long-term monitoring plan developed under Sub-activity A.4.2.3 – completed in year 2. The lessons learned will be captured and disseminated by using the knowledge management tools developed under sub-activity A.1.2.1.

### Sub-activity A.4.2.3: Establish partnerships (including the support of MoUs with Jordanian academic institutions and other organizations) to generate new research, develop innovative approaches, and generate relevant research findings and publications to inform innovation landscape-centric approaches to climate change adaptation.
#### Under sub-activity A.4.2.3, partnerships with academic institutions will support the generation of new research on climate change adaptation in Jordan’s water sector. The project will establish MoUs with Jordanian academic institutions via the long-term monitoring plan developed under Sub-activity A.4.2.3. Currently, NCAE is the only partner institution that has signed an MoU with the project. Other potential partners include the University of Jordan, University of Science and Technology, Jarash University, Mutah University and Al-Balqa’ Applied University). Considering their strong research engagement, they play a minor role in technology transfer or extension services.

### B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

The proposed project will be implemented over an eight-year period. UNEP will be the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project. The AE is responsible for the implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting and closure of the project in coordination with the national-level Project Steering Committee (PSC) and a national-level Project Management Unit (PMU). The PSC consists of key stakeholders including Jordan’s Ministry of Environment (MoEnW), the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN; Components 2 to 6). Further details on the suitability of the AE are provided in Section B.3.

## Executive Summary and Task Record of the EEF

### As the head ministry for the National Designated Authority (NDA), MoEnv has been identified as the appropriate government entity to lead the implementation of the GCF project. The Ministry is committed to leveraging its expertise for the efficient and effective management of financial resources for the implementation of project activities.

The Ministry has a long history in climate change adaptation, having participated in the UNFCCC National Communications to the UNFCCC and NAP process, and has been involved in a number of related projects in recent years. These include: (i) Jordan’s Second National Communication to the UNFCCC; (ii) the GEF-5 project on climate change adaptation; i) landscape and water sector management; and ii) sustainable development. Moreover, MoEnv is also a member of the Climate Change Secretariat for the Middle East and North Africa region.

The initiatives supported by MoEnv over recent years are listed below (more details on these initiatives are provided in Section 3.1.1 of this document).

- GIZ – Sustainable use of Ecosystem Services in Jordan, Energy and Climate Fund, 2014–2019
- GEF – Water Management and Mitigation Technology Pilot Project to face Climate Change impact in Jordan, 2006—2018 (USD7.6 million)
- FAO – Sustainable Land Management for the Conservation of Soil and Water Resources and the Management of Source Water Resources in Agriculture in Central West Asia and North Africa (CRHINA) Programme, 2014–2019
- GEF – Maintaining Sustainable Land and Water Management Practices, 2005—2011 (USD37.8 million)
- GIZ – Managing Water Resources in Jordan, 2006–2018 (USD10.5 million)
- GIZ – Climate Change Adaptation Project, Jordan Rift Valley Phase II, 2007–2013 (USD152.7 million)
- GEF – Badia Ecosystem and Livelihood Project, 2013–2017 (USD33.3 million)

### The MoEnv’s mission is to ‘monitor and improve the quality of Jordan’s environment, conserve natural resources and promote sustainable development’. To achieve this mission, it has established a number of environmental institutions – including other relevant government organizations – in initiating and driving policies, legislation, strategies and monitoring, as well as in promoting public awareness. These include: (i) the Ministry of Environment; (ii) the Jordanian Environmental Protection Authority; (iii) the National Environment Commission; (iv) the Water Resources Commission; (v) a number of specialist units, including the directorates of: i) Climate Change; ii) Nature Protection; iii) Environmental Inspection; iv) Waste Management and Recycling; v) Air Quality; vi) Noise Pollution; vii) Radiation Protection; and viii) Public Environmental Awareness. These are supported by the functional directorates of Human Resources and Administrative Affairs, Finance, Research and Development, and Information Technology.

The MoEnv is also a member of the Climate Change Secretariat for the Middle East and North Africa region. The recommendations of the National Climate Change Policy, the Climate Change Directions (CCD) was established in 2013 to provide a framework for climate change adaptation and mitigation activities at national level. It has six committees as a party to the UNFCCC. Additionally, the CCD is the coordinating focal point to global and regional environmental initiatives related to climate change.

The MoEnv’s role in the GCF project will be to ensure that the project is implemented effectively and efficiently, facilitating the participation of relevant stakeholders at all levels. It also overviews development of the internal capacity of MoEnv for the implementation of the project activities. The Ministry has established a dedicated team within the Jordanian Secretariat of the CCD to manage this role.

The CCD is made up of two departments, namely the Emissions Reduction Section and the Adaptation to Climate Change Section. One of the core of the organizational structure of MoEnv can be found in Section 3.1.1 Institutional Framework

## In Annex 2: Feasibility Study; UNEP, as the Accredited Entity, has undertaken a capacity assessment of the Executive Entity, particularly by reviewing the elements of governance and public accountability. UNEP provides the assurance that the MiDF has the relevant fiduciary and financial management capacity to act as the Executing Entity. UNEP will also provide technical assistance in developing the project proposal, including identifying key elements and to prepare appropriate mitigation measures. UNEP will also closely monitor the financial management of the project and ensure that it is being implemented according to the approved budget. UNEP will also provide strategic and establish internal controls for the project and project fund management.

### 236. IUCN's expertise and external network provide a solid foundation for a large and diverse portfolio of conservation projects in Jordan, which are designed to address the country’s pressing environmental challenges. The knowledge of local communities, their projects work to reverse habitat loss, restore ecosystems and improve people's well-being. They also produce a wealth of scientific information on species and habitats that can be used by policy makers and other stakeholders. IUCN supports and participates in scientific research, promotes and helps to implement national conservation legislation, policies and strategies. The Jordanian government has been working with IUCN for over 20 years. Some of the initiatives supported by IUCN over recent years are (more details on these initiatives are provided in Section 7 of Annex 2) a) Development of the National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP) (2013-2025); b) The Jordanian Green Climate Fund (GCF) Accession (2014); c) The Jordan National Forest Policy (JNFP) (2015). The value addition of selecting IUCN in GCF as EE is also includes the organisation’s presence and footprint in Jordan, which has been established over 20 years. This includes a strong relationship with the Ministry of Environment, the support of the organisation's locally-based Jordanian staff who are familiar with the local context and have extensive experience in implementing projects on the ground. The project will be implemented through IUCN Jordan. It also includes a strong working relationship with the consortium of NGOs that supported the development of this proposal and which are currently implementing projects under the umbrella of the GCF. This is an important factor for IUCN as EE, as opposed to UNEP for example, is strategic in that it provides a strong operational foundation for the implementation of the project. The combination of these factors gives IUCN comparative advantage and capacity in function as EE of Components 2-6+ are presented in Annex 2.

## Technical Partners

### 237. IUCN will draw on its expertise to implement project technical activities (Components 2 to 4) will be sourced primarily from members of the Consortium of NGOs, with due care and consideration for the best value for money and best interests of the project beneficiaries.

#### 238. IUCN will develop the Terms of Reference for the specific technical assistance to be provided under relevant project components. The terms of reference will include a description of the scope of work, deliverables, timeframes, outputs, expected outcomes and impacts, timeline, deliverables and requirements such as track record including experience with carrying out similar activities, qualifications, skills, expertise, etc., and other relevant information. Technical partners will be selected based on a competitive selection process, based on track record in delivering the technical assistance required for each component. The final selection of the technical partner will be communicated to the main technical partners. IUCN will be responsible for the competitive selection of technical partners in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the relevant TPs. Technical partners will be legally bound through legal agreements signed with UNEP (IUCN-TP contracts). TPs will produce regular progress and financial reports to IUCN, which will be reviewed by the PSC. The relationship between IUCN and the technical partner is akin to that of a client and service provider. The terms of reference for each component under IUCN TP’s in the form of cash advances (bank transfers) dependent on cleared expenditure reports and progress reports.

## Project Steering Committee (PSC)

### 239. The PSC will comprise representatives from (also sit) UNEP and alternating chairmanship, along with UNEP as permanent secretariat. It shall consist of the following members: CDD, WAM and JVA; all MiDFs; all TPVs; the National Technical Working Group (TWG); and six WMGs. The committee will primarily be responsible for providing technical oversight and advice on project implementation, including reviewing progress reports, approving expenditure reports, and its approving any changes to the project’s targets, activities or timeline. Biannual PSC meetings will be convened by UNEP as permanent secretariat. The TWG shall serve in a dual capacity: i) acting as the focal point for providing guidance and strategic advice; ii) making changes to the project’s Targets, Activities or Timeline (TATs); iii) serving as the focal point for engagements and interactions with project stakeholders and partner organisations. Toiffs for the PSC will be adopted after this first PSC meeting.

## National TWG (240)

A National TWG will be established to provide technical advice to National EE/MDM, to support the implementation of project interventions that are cross-cutting and not site-specific—i.e., the capacity building and mainstreaming activities which are being implemented at a national level. The membership of the National TWG will include representatives from the following organizations: i) the Ministry of Environment; ii) the Ministry of Energy; iii) the climate change adaptation, such as VAWA, WVA, MoA, MOPR, MRIC and the Jordanian National Commission for Women (JNCW); iv) the Water Sector Development Program (WSDP); v) the Water Resources Management Unit (WRMU); vi) the Climate Change Unit (CCU). The National TWG will also include local specialists and academics with expertise relevant to the project, such as those from the Jordan University of Science and Technology (JUST), the Water Resources Management Unit (WRMU). The National TWG will work closely with the Technical Partners and WCMs to ensure that both national and local perspectives are considered in the development and implementation of the project’s three target sites. Additionally, the TWG, together with TPs and the CTA, will evaluate proposals submitted in response to the Call for Proposals, including the facilitation facility and the Water Funds’ grant mechanism.

To promote the representation and participation of all relevant stakeholders—i.e., government institutions, NGOs, CSOs, local communities, and private sector entities—in the project’s three target sites (the Zarqa Valley, King Talal Dam and Kathm), with at least 50% women representation in each WMC. The WMCs will be established within each of the project’s three target sites (Zarqa Valley, King Talal Dam and Kathm). The presence of TWGs will provide a platform for dialogue between stakeholders on issues related to water resources management and climate change adaptation. The PMU will be headed by a Project Manager (PM), who is responsible for: i) overall coordination; ii) financial management; iii) an Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer (ESO); iv) a Communications Officer (CO); v) a Monitoring Officer (MO); vi) a Gender Advisor (GA); vii) a Technical Advisor (TA). The recruitment process will ensure that the PMU is gender balanced. The PC will be a full-time position, and the TA will be a part-time position. The ESO, CO, MO, GA and TA are all responsible for monitoring and management of the project. The PC will be primarily responsible for ensuring that the project achieves the targets

### B.5 JUSTIFICATION FOR GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

**247. Jordan is ranked as the fifth most water-stressed country in the world, with only 125 CM/yr of water available per capita and a population growth rate of about 3% annually. The country has experienced annual rainfall, higher temperatures, more frequent, extreme and prolonged droughts. As a result of the country’s existing vulnerability to critical water resources, any further reduction in water availability will have severe implications for the country’s water security.**

**248. Climate change is expected to exacerbate these consequences on climate-related development and the health and wellbeing of the country’s most vulnerable populations. The Jordan River Basin (JRB) is one such area where climate change has already had severe consequences on climatic-related development and the health and wellbeing of the country’s most vulnerable populations. Consequently, it is not expected that there are many easily implementable, low-cost options to increase water availability in the JRB. In fact, the only option available for increasing water availability in the JRB is through climate change adaptation. As a result, there is a strong economic argument for expending interventions that increase water sustainability and security in the JRB. This is particularly true when expected climate conditions make the case where expected climatic conditions make the probability of exploiting existing measures seem particularly high and where success is seen as possible.**

**249. Jordan’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for addressing the impact of water stress on Jordan’s inhabitants, ecosystems and economy requires a climate change adaptation response based on an integrated landscape and water resources management approach that takes into account the interconnections between different ecosystems, hydrological systems and various uses within each ecosystem. The Integrated Water Resources Management (IWRM) approach offers a pathway towards more resilient livelihoods, based on sustainable resource use that takes into account the interconnections between different ecosystems, hydrological systems and various uses within each ecosystem.**
