A landscape. However, while the climate change adaptation and mitigation potential of ILWIRM is clear, its implementation in Jordan through the JLM project represents a major investment.

250 To this end, the Government of Jordan (GoJ) is seeking US$434,200 under GCF grant financing to achieve this objective by reducing the negative impacts of climate change on water resources through a landscape-level approach. This will be achieved by: (a) increasing water availability; (b) improving soil and land quality; (c) ecosystem restoration; (d) managed aquifer recharge; (e) rainwater harvesting; (f) evaporation reduction; (g) diversified natural resource management; and (h) improved livelihoods. The GoJ has already demonstrated that it possesses a very strong rationale for investing in ILWIRM, particularly with a strong ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) component.

## Market failures: markets tend to be skewed toward hard infrastructure as they often do not account for any negative environmental impacts or the long-term benefits of investments. In Jordan, many of these ecosystem services are undervalued.

Barriers to implementation and adoption of approaches: buyers may not have the technical knowledge to understand and be able to compare ecosystem-based approaches with traditional man-made alternatives.

## Transboundary water management: Jordan is a transboundary river basin where there has been no landscape scale without coordination as a result of the costs of bringing stakeholders together and strengthening governance processes.

253 These factors provide a clear rationale for investing in an enabling environment that advances ILWIRM in Jordan as a climate change adaptation measure. The GoJ has already demonstrated that it possesses a very strong rationale for adopting ecosystem-based approaches which remain novel or unexploited. This is particularly the case for approaches with high upfront costs, such as managed aquifer recharge and rainwater harvesting.

254 Although Jordan is classified as an upper middle-income country*, the country continues to face major economic burdens due to its location in a conflict zone, ongoing political instability, and the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. These burdens will continue to compound the financial challenges that Jordan faces as it seeks to implement the ILWIRM project. The GoJ has an external debt of US$20 billion in January 2019**. This debt has been attributed largely by: (i) successive governments adding an expansionary fiscal policy to a fragile economic environment; and (ii) the Syrian refugee crisis, which has forced the country to borrow externally***, increased welfare spending for citizens and refugees****, exacerbated the already high levels of poverty in Jordan*****, and led to a significant increase in public expenditure. The GoJ also faces other financial challenges such as: (i) high unemployment rates; and (ii) VAT and tax collection losses – nearly 90% of the population is not subject to income tax. Compounding Jordan’s public spending burden are the country’s low levels of domestic revenue generation, with only 15% of its total expenditure funded by domestic fiscal debt (6.0% in 2018, although down from 12.7% in 2019). In terms of the refugee influx, a significant proportion of refugees are not registered and therefore do not pay taxes. The GoJ’s public spending burden is further exacerbated by its high levels of external debt (54%), which has increased to US$38 billion from $26 billion in January 2019. In addition, the country’s fiscal deficit has reached a record level of 7% of GDP in 2019. The direct cost of Syrian refugees is estimated at US$1.3 billion annually and the GoJ has been unable to meet this cost. The World Bank has recognised the strain placed on Jordan with these resources with needs-on-the-ground is increasingly difficult*****. The conflicts in Syria and Iraq, that are also sources of influx of refugees, have also resulted in a considerable increase in Jordan’s trade deficit. This has led to an increase in imports of goods and services from countries such as Turkey, the United Arab Emirates, Qatar, Saudi Arabia, Lebanon, Egypt, and Central Asia*****. From 2011, border crossings with Syria and Iraq were severely limited between 2013 and 2018 were completely closed, causing a profound negative impact on

253. Jordan has recently seen the approval of GCF FP155 – Building resilience to cope with climate change in Jordan through improving water use efficiency in the agriculture sector (BRCCJ) – which includes US$ 25 million in grant funding from the GCF. The project is a joint initiative between the Government of Jordan and the World Bank, and it will be carried out by the Jordan River Basin Authority (JRBA). The projects are being implemented in different areas of the country. BRCCJ in the Dead Sea Basin and the proposed project in the Wadi Al-Hasa Basin are both funded through the GCF. The project in the Dead Sea Basin is presented in Annex 29. This increases the scope of impact that GCF resources will have in Jordan, with the two projects focusing on water use efficiency in agriculture and water resource management.

254. Given the funding gap between Jordan’s climate priorities and its public finances, adaptation will require a shift in national development planning to ensure that all sectors are aligned towards sustainable development and operate under the existing paradigm of an uncoordinated and compartmentalised model of land and water resources management. The project Exit Strategy (hereafter referred to as “Exit Strategy”) is designed to help Jordan become increasingly vulnerable to the projected impacts of climate change. This will result in: reduced quantity and quality of water resources; increased salinity levels in groundwater, surface water bodies, and coastal areas; increased risks and impacts on pastoral and rural communities (livelihoods); decreased supply of ecosystem goods and services; and a loss of biodiversity. The Exit Strategy is designed to help Jordan adapt to the projected impacts of climate change by: i) identifying and addressing the root causes of vulnerability in order to avoid and mitigate the impacts of climate change will be avoided and a paradigm shift in the country towards climate-resilient sustainable development; ii) developing an integrated, participatory and adaptive approach for water resources management to ensure that all sectors are aligned under one national framework; iii) ensuring that there will be increased budgetary allocated to water resources management; iv) increasing the capacity of Jordan’s institutions to manage water resources; v) improving protection supply and use of water for domestic purposes under climate change conditions, and vi) behavioural changes.

A framework of the proposed project’s Exit Strategy is presented in the paragraphs that follow, focusing on the role of GCF resources to support Jordan in achieving its long-term climate change adaptation goals and objectives for sustainability and financial sustainability. Developing a scaling-up and replication strategy for the project will be critical as it will allow Jordan to use other sources of finance to cover the costs of long-term GCF, as well as to scale up and replicate its LWRM intervention.

This will ensure that the benefits from the project are sustainable over time and that the long-term goals and objectives of the project are sustained. ## The technical, financial and social sustainability of interventions has been accounted for during the design process to deliver optimal climate change adaptation benefits to vulnerable communities in Jordan beyond the lifetime of the project.

### Sustainability through technical and institutional capacity building

The project will contribute to the creation of an enabling environment for the implementation of a climate-resilient LWIRM approach in Jordan, by strengthening existing building institutional capacity at the national and local levels in a gender-sensitive manner. This includes engaging women farmers who are currently barred from adaptation in their communities, as well as ensuring that they have access to information on climate change (at least 50% of women) on the use of climate data, the results of climate analysis, and risk assessment in policy- and decision-making processes. The project will also support the development of gender-sensitive LWIRM adaptation actions and enhance the technical capacity of stakeholders for the planning and implementation of climate-resilient LWIRM interventions (Activities 1.1.2 and 1.1.3). This will be achieved through the drafting of amendments to existing laws, regulations, policies, and investment plans that are currently being implemented in Jordan, and the update of LWIRM principles in the decision-making process for sustainable socio-economic development. Specifically, this includes ensuring that women farmers have access to information on climate change (at least 50% of women) on the use of climate data, the results of climate analysis, and risk assessment in policy- and decision-making processes.

The project will also support the development of gender-sensitive LWIRM adaptation actions and enhance the technical capacity of stakeholders for the planning and implementation of climate-resilient LWIRM interventions (Activities 1.1.2 and 1.1.3). This will be achieved through the drafting of amendments to existing laws, regulations, policies, and investment plans that are currently being implemented in Jordan, and the update of LWIRM principles in the decision-making process for sustainable socio-economic development. Specifically, this includes ensuring that women farmers have access to information on climate change (at least 50% of women) on the use of climate data, the results of climate analysis, and risk assessment in policy- and decision-making processes.

## Social and environmental sustainability

The proposed project has been developed and will be executed through a gender-inclusive participatory and community-based approach, which is designed to ensure that all stakeholders are consulted in the design and implementation of Jordan’s climate change adaptation interventions. This approach will allow the project to be country-driven by enabling communities to identify their own priorities for adaptation (including women and youth), negating the need for a top-down approach to management and ensuring the long-term sustainability of projects. The project will also ensure that all stakeholders are involved in the design, implementation, and monitoring of climate change adaptation interventions at the community level, which is a paradigm shift in community participation in development projects. Once established, this model can be replicated across Jordan’s other vulnerable communities. This will secure the buy-in of stakeholders at the community level, which will ensure the continued maintenance of on-the-ground investments (including those related to water resources and land use) and the autonomous spread of climate change adaptation into school syllabi. This will promote the autonomous spread of adaptation awareness across the country, encouraging Jordanian youth to enroll in climate-related studies at tertiary institutions in the future, and thereby contribute to climate resilience.

### Gender-inclusive WMCs

Under Activity 1.1.4, gender-inclusive WMCs will be established at all target watershed to facilitate dialogue between women and men on upstream and downstream areas, and feed strong priorities into community-level projects. This will ensure that the voices of all stakeholders (including women) are heard in the design and implementation of climate change adaptation interventions.

### Passive restoration of ecosystems

Under Activity 2.1.3, the passive restoration of ecosystems will also contribute to both social and environmental sustainability by ensuring that communities have access to a wide range of ecosystem services while contributing to the management of resources in the pursuit of local pastoralist communities. This will similarly be the case for women-led community-based organizations (CBOs) and other groups that are involved in the design, implementation, and monitoring of restoration interventions, including long-term care and maintenance of restored areas and improving water quality. The project will ensure that all ecosystem restoration investments are maintained by protecting the ecosystems, minimizing human pressures and promoting natural ecological processes. This will be achieved through the establishment of community-based organizations (CBOs) to manage rockfall dams and风采 solar installations, with the requirement ensuring regular inspections take place, and that required repair work is undertaken.

### Long-term data sets

Finally, the project will ensure that all ecosystem restoration investments are maintained by protecting the ecosystems, minimizing human pressures and promoting natural ecological processes. This will be achieved through the establishment of community-based organizations (CBOs) to manage rockfall dams and风采 solar installations, with the requirement ensuring regular inspections take place, and that required repair work is undertaken.

### Long-term goals

Detailed long-term GIM plans will be developed for all ecosystem restoration investments, including those related to water resources and land use. These plans will be established in consultation with government institutions. Government funding for GIMs will include: (i) financing of ongoing restoration, conservation and management activities by the ecosystem restored by the project; and (ii) maintaining the mechanisms and tools established by the project.

## Financial sustainability

The project will be in addition to amendments to Jordan’s policy framework under Article 11.1 which will promote the allocation of sufficient funds to climate change adaptation via government budgets and ensure domestic sources of financing for climate change adaptation projects. The costs associated with operation and maintenance have been examined through the economic analysis (Annex E) to ensure that there is a clear incentive to ensure these costs are covered. In most cases this is both obvious and clear. For example, the expected cost of water supply infrastructure in Jordan is approximately 10% of the total project costs. If funds to pay for this should be generated directly, likewise, the alternative natural resource-based livelihoods examined (e.g., livestock, agriculture) are not likely to generate sufficient income to cover the level maintenance of equipment etc will become incompatible. Similarly given anticipated levels of water scarcity, the use of groundwater is expected to be a key adaptation strategy and therefore the cost of this should factor by a fifth or fifty, implying a direct incentive for households / public authorities to dedicate the time / money to ensure the health of their resources.

Where there are stakeholders involved both in delivering and receiving benefits, the key to financial stability will be having a mechanism in place to transfer resources from the beneficiaries to those bearing the costs of adaptation. This is likely to be achieved through the implementation of a water fund (Activity 2.1.4). The operation phase of this project will focus on: i) development and implementation of the Water Fund; ii) establishment of M&E activities; iii) communication of progress, and iv) improvement of the Water Fund under adaptive management principles. In addition to these activities, the project will also work towards the following: a) the ongoing implementation of interventions, b) development of a long-term strategy, routine review and continuous improvement of the Water Fund, c) ensuring that the Water Fund is financially sustainable and viable. Sustaining a safe-guarding and considerable impact on water security, the project will i) secure long-term funding for the Water Fund through the Jordanian government’s budget, ii) ensure alignment with the Water Fund into the country’s legal framework, iii) monitor and evaluate progress systematically and apply adaptive

# D. EXPECTED PREFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Investment Policy.

## I.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will contribute to the GCF’s fund-level adaptation objective of increased climate-resilient sustainable development by facilitating a shift towards conserving and augmenting water resources in Jordan. This will be achieved through the implementation of an integrated water resource management strategy that will clip environmental and natural resource-based livelihoods, improving the climate resilience of local communities and households accomplished through the GCF’s fund-level impacts and outcomes presented below[1].

### GCF Fund-Level Impacts[2]

- **ARA 1 — Most vulnerable peoples and communities:** ARRA 1.1: Water resources; ARRA 1.2: Livelihoods and economic activity;
- **ARA 4 — Ecosystems and ecosystem services.**

The project will strengthen the mandates and capacities of relevant Jordanian institutions to implement climate-resilient LWRM in water sectors, including WASH, agriculture, hydropower, and energy. The institutional strengthening and management policy, planning and legal frameworks will be drafted to promote climate-resilient LWRM. The drafting of these policies, plans, and legal frameworks will provide the necessary enabling environment for the implementation of LWRM in water planning and decision-making processes in the water and land sectors. The training will lay the foundation for the development of a national framework for climate change adaptation in Jordan’s water sector.

Second: Water Management Committees (WMCs) will be established in target watersheds to foster participatory LWRM, including the implementation of climate-resilient measures at the community and household level. This will strengthen capacity for the participatory implementation of the approach as a climate change adaptation measure. Following the establishment of WMCs, the project will support their operation through training and mentoring. Finally, LWRM will be facilitated through the development of site- and guidelines, standards and protocols.

Moreover, under the proposed project, a Water Fund will be established to facilitate investment in ongoing climate-resilient water resource management activities at the community level. The Water Fund will provide financial support for restoration activities and improvement of water resources in these areas but also foster a participatory approach to climate change adaptation by involving local communities in the design and implementation of projects. This includes introducing small-scale water harvesting infrastructure at the community- and household-level; ii) empowering communities to manage their own water resources through capacity building, training, and mentoring for on-field water conservation and harvesting measures on smallholder farms.

The project will strengthen the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities (including women and youth) in degraded areas by implementing a range of interventions aimed at improving the resilience of degraded watershed areas, as well as diversification and strengthening of livelihoods in a gender-responsive manner; ii) improved protection and provision of water resources through the implementation of climate-resilient measures such as the installation of an improved supply of ecosystem services — including the regulation of water resources and reduction of soil erosion. In addition to these interventions, the project will support the rehabilitation of degraded areas by means of aquifer recharge. These interventions will be complemented by the installation of 1 MW of floating solar panels over existing reservoirs in Jordan’s northwestern region (see Section 6.2.3 of this document). Additionally, streamwise[3] and off-stream[4] water harvesting measures will be implemented.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page:87 of 144

## Climate change adaptation benefits will be increased through the implementation of a gender-responsive approach to water resources management, focusing on female-headed households (see Section 6.0 and Annex 2). As a result, reduced water stress will buffer both communities upstream and downstream of project sites in the exposure of climate risks such as droughts. The project will also increase the resilience of women-headed households by providing them with access to improved livelihoods through climate-resilient natural resource-based livelihoods. Moreover, additional gender-responsive livelihoods related to the sustainable management of water resources will be provided for women and girls through the installation of solar pumps.

### Adaptation impact

#### Project interventions will directly benefit a total of 248,012 people (i.e., 2% of the Jordanian population) – 135,730 men and 112,282 women. Of these beneficiaries, 96,841 are in the northern Jordan Valley. The majority of this total (i.e., 144,065 people or 58,007 women and 31,839 men) are from the Yarmouk site – Khirbet Al Bai Waled and Al Sebu municipalities; 12,681 people (or 5,464 women and 7,217 men) are from the Kharabat site – Shoura Alawata and Mafnas municipalities; and 90,466 (35,420 women and 55,046 men) are from the Khitan site – Shoura Alawata and Mafnas municipalities. In addition to these beneficiaries, a further 187,000 people will benefit indirectly through increased agricultural activity. For example, under Output 3.2, 1,200 smallholder farming households (46,000 people – an average of 5 people per household) in the northern Jordan Valley will be provided with improved irrigation infrastructure and water resources management systems. These households are located across three sites: 780 households at the Yarmouk site; 239 households at the Kharabat site (14,000 people); and 151 households at the Khitan site.

#### The project will also benefit a total of 6,000 farmers in the northern Jordan Valley. Of these beneficiaries, 5,874 are men and 126 women. These farmers are located across three sites: 3,964 farmers (17,432 people) at the Yarmouk site; 1,200 farmers (5,404 people) at the Kharabat site; and 836 farmers (3,164 people) at the Khitan site.

#### Interventions at the regulatory and institutional level will indirectly benefit the Jordanian population as a whole. Among other things, these interventions will improve the capacity of local governments to implement climate-resilient WRM. The Water Fund will serve as a catalyst for public-private sector investment in water resources management and climate change adaptation. This will lead to long-term by maintaining and attenuating water resources under conditions of climate change. Public and private sector beneficiaries include 1,045 farmers (32,867 people) at the Yarmouk site; 900 farmers (2,700 people) at the Kharabat site; and 1,000 farmers (3,000 people) at the Khitan site.

#### The Water Fund will contribute to creating an enabling environment for future upscaling and replication of climate change adaptation interventions locally, nationally and regionally.

## Potential water resource benefits

Under the proposed project, water supplies in the Jordan Valley will be improved through the implementation of several interventions. These include: i) the restoration and rehabilitation of ecosystems on 12,000 ha; ii) the installation of solar panels to manage aquifer recharge; iii) no-till and in-field rainwater harvesting; and iv) the installation of floating solar panels.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 89 of 144

## Green Climate Fund Proposal

### Introduction

The project aims to increase climate-resilient restoration in watersheds.

#### Project Overview

- **Title:** Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0
- **Page:** 89/144

### Climate-resilient restoration in watersheds

281. Increasing vegetation cover across the project’s intervention sites will reduce runoff and the subsequent loss of typical sediment loads from these areas. This will decrease sedimentation levels in downstream rivers or dams and lower the rate at which their storage capacity is reduced. For example, the Himsa system has the potential to increase the climate-resilient restoration area by 120,000 ha (30% of its total area) with an estimated annual reduction in sediment loading of 574,951 tonnes/year.

#### Project Activities

- **Activity 2.1.3:** Water conservation and harvesting measures will be implemented on smallholder farms.
- **Activity 2.1.4:** In-field water harvesting interventions will be carried out to support improved agricultural production and reduce pressure on underground water sources.
- **Activity 2.1.5:** Construction of small-scale water harvesting infrastructure for MARR in areas where the canal is not available.

#### Project Benefits

- Improved vegetation cover will increase infiltration, soil moisture content, and aquifer recharge rates.
- Reduced sediment loads from upstream areas will decrease sedimentation levels downstream.
- Increased vegetation cover will reduce runoff and improve water availability during dry periods.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 90 of 144

## Transformation to climate-resilient integrated land and water resources management (LWRM) in Jordan is hampered by several barriers (as detailed in Section B.2: Theory of Change), including:

- Insufficient institutional and technical coordination capacity, as well as limited mandates of state and non-state actors for climate change adaptation.
- Limited awareness among decision-makers and the public about the impacts of climate change on land and water resources management.
- Limited awareness amongst decision-makers and the public regarding the financial and economic effectiveness of drought mitigation measures such as rainwater harvesting (RWH) and improved water use efficiency, including innovative financing models like the Water Fund.

## Removing these barriers will result in:

1. Strengthened governance and capacity for climate-resilient LWRM.
2. Climate-resilient LWRM practices and ecosystem restoration implemented in three pilot areas of Jordan under different climate change conditions.
3. Improved knowledge and awareness of climate change adaptation for land and water resources management, including the financial and economic effectiveness of drought mitigation measures such as RWH and improved water use efficiency.
4. Increased public participation in decision-making processes related to LWRM practices.

## The project will achieve these outcomes through a multi-faceted approach:

- Improved governance and capacity building (Activity 1.2).
- Climate-resilient LWRM practices implemented in three pilot areas of Jordan under different climate change conditions.
- Household behavioral change through improved water use efficiency, including innovative financing models such as the Water Fund.

## The project will be implemented across four components:

### Component I: Strengthening governance and capacity for climate-resilient LWRM
- Activity 1.2: Improved governance and capacity building (Activity 1.2).

### Component II: Climate-resilient LWRM practices in three pilot areas of Jordan under different climate change conditions
- Activity 1.3: Climate-resilient LWRM practices implemented in three pilot areas of Jordan under different climate change conditions.

### Component III: Household behavioral change through improved water use efficiency, including innovative financing models such as the Water Fund
- Activity 1.4: Improved household behavior and demand management through climate-resilient LWRM practices.
- Activity 2.1: Improved water use efficiency in the agriculture sector (BroCC) or FP150+ (see Section B.1, Table 3).
- Activity 2.2: Improved water use efficiency in the urban sector.

### Component IV: Broader scaling and replication
- Activity 4.1: Broader scaling of climate-resilient LWRM practices.
- Activity 4.2: Broader scaling of household behavioral change through improved water use efficiency, including innovative financing models such as the Water Fund.

- Jordanian government agencies (Activity 1.1).
- Non-governmental organizations (NGOs) (Activity 1.1).

# Upscaling potential of proposed water resource augmentation interventions

295. Several scenarios, ranging from conservative to optimistic, have been considered in the table below for the upscaling potential of the Project’s ecosystem restoration and managed aquifer recharge (rock dam) interventions under project scaling-up estimates after 6-, 20- and 25-year periods. Ecosystem restoration (13,200 ha under the with-project scenario) is the only intervention that has been considered for upscaling in all three scenarios. Managed aquifer recharge (rock dams) has a high potential, which does not change significantly across the three scenarios.

## Potential for upscaling, replication and strengthened regulatory frameworks
The proposed project has been designed to maximize the potential for upscaling and replication of interventions to achieve climate-resilient integrated water resources management (IRWM) in Jordan. This will be achieved through the establishment of standards and protocols for the adoption of climate-resilient integrated landscape and water resource management, which can be replicated at the national level. The project will also contribute to the development of a regulatory framework that will support the implementation of IRWM in Jordan. This approach focuses on the integration of landscape and water resources management into a single approach for climate change adaptation and mitigation.

The proposed project is designed to ensure that all interventions are fully integrated, rather than being implemented in isolation. Upscaling, replication, and the strengthening of regulatory frameworks will be supported by multi-level institutional arrangements at national, sub-national, and local levels. These include:

- i) developing land-use guidelines, standards and protocols for climate-resilient integrated watershed management in the Jordan River Basin;
- ii) establishing a national IRWM knowledge centre to provide technical support on climate change adaptation and mitigation;
- iii) training staff from relevant national and sub-national institutions to use climate data, and the results of climate change impact assessments, as part of their decision-making processes for land-use planning and water resources management; 
- iv) training policy- and decision-makers, and planners on the integration of climate-resilient IRWM into existing policies and plans;
- v) establishing a national IRWM knowledge centre to provide technical support on climate change adaptation and mitigation.

The project will also contribute to the development of a regulatory framework that will support the implementation of IRWM in Jordan. This will be achieved through:

- i) developing a set of standards and protocols for the adoption of climate-resilient integrated landscape and water resource management;
- ii) establishing a national IRWM knowledge centre to provide technical support on climate change adaptation and mitigation;
- iii) training staff from relevant national and sub-national institutions to use climate data, and the results of climate change impact assessments, as part of their decision-making processes for land-use planning and water resources management; 
- iv) training policy- and decision-makers, and planners on the integration of climate-resilient IRWM into existing policies and plans;
- v) establishing a national IRWM knowledge centre to provide technical support on climate change adaptation and mitigation.

# Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

293. Jordan’s National Capacity Self-Assessment (NCSA) report published in 2007 identified how capacity for knowledge management is a key barrier to climate change adaptation. Moreover, several other gaps exist in areas related to knowledge sharing and awareness, including inter alia: () the absence of a national database and monitoring system for water resources; () the lack of an integrated approach to land management at the project level (for monitoring and reporting on biodiversity); and () insufficient long-term awareness and education programmes for local communities. The project will address these gaps by developing a knowledge management strategy that will enhance climate change adaptation in Jordan by developing several knowledge-management tools that will increase policymakers, stakeholders, and community members’ capacity to make informed decisions about land use and water resources using approaches and their benefits. This will include the establishment of a national knowledge repository of lessons learned and generated through the project’s activities. The project will also develop a set of guidelines for integrating KM approaches under this project. These knowledge-management tools will enhance the technical capacity of decision-makers to integrate climate change adaptation into land-use planning, water resources management, and biodiversity conservation. This will be strengthened through the training of user groups in the use of these knowledge management tools. Moreover, the certification of local community members as trainers will ensure that this knowledge is disseminated beyond project boundaries. The project will also enhance the capacity of decision-makers to integrate climate change adaptation into land-use planning, water resources management, and biodiversity conservation by building on lessons learned from other projects in Jordan and elsewhere.

# Potential for the creation of an enabling environment

294. The Government of Jordan is committed to ensuring that Jordan is resilient for the sustainability of the abovementioned upscaling and replication strategies. The enabling environment will encompass strengthened governance and institutional arrangements, including the establishment of a national water fund, which will ensure that all stakeholders are sustained in the long term, with support from all levels of government. To achieve this, the project will establish WRMF at the national level to provide a platform for dialogue between upstream and downstream stakeholders and ensure the continuation of an integrated approach to land management at the project level. The WRMF will also serve as a focal point for coordinating climate change adaptation efforts with the capacity of major government agencies—including the Secretary Generals of the MoUV, MiAa, Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MoWI), and the Jordan River Basin Authority (JRBA)—and other relevant stakeholders.

The continued funding of climate-resilient WRMF in Jordan will be facilitated by the mainstreaming of this adaptation approach into national policies and strategies. The project will also ensure that the WRMF is linked to relevant land-management legislation. In addition, the establishment of a Water Fund will generate sustainable financial resources to support the continued funding of project interventions at the local level.

## Environmental, social and economic co-benefits

296. The climate-resilient restoration of watershed ecosystems along with the implementation of integrated landscape and water management practices will provide numerous environmental, social and economic co-benefits that are presented below and summarized in Table 12.

### Strengthened flood resilience
- Jordan's current sporadic rainfall and intense storm events result in flash flooding which can cause significant damage to infrastructure, destruction of crops, and loss of life.
- These floods lead to the degradation of water quality, increased sedimentation, and the likelihood of epidemics caused by vector-, water- and food-borne diseases.
- Frequent flooding of downstream areas is also a major concern in Jordan as it leads to soil erosion, reduced agricultural productivity, interruption, reduced volume of runoff and spread of overland water flow compared with a BAC scenario. This will lead to a significant reduction in the availability of freshwater resources.

### Enhanced food security
- Livelihood resilience and biodiversity through the restoration of degraded habitats.
- The LWRM approach will enhance the conservation of biodiversity and increase the availability of wildlife for hunting, increasing the availability of fodder for livestock. For example, after two years of passive Hima restoration in Jordan, the number of gazelles increased from 10 to 40 per km2, and the number of ibexes increased from 35 to 60 per km2.
- The LWRM approach will also enhance food security by increasing the availability of fish for Jordan's food requirements, with the average Jordanian family spending 48% of its income on food, much of which is imported. Additionally, the LWRM approach will increase the availability of livestock feed and improve water quality.
- Jordan is unable to match demand. National biodiversity is being negatively impacted by the expansion of agricultural land and urbanization.

### Gender-responsive development impacts

297 The proposed project will support a transformation in the water sector to one in which women are included in the decision-making process, with a focus on gender equality. This will be achieved by involving more women in the management of water resources and maintenance of community-level water infrastructure. This will allow for their specific water-related needs to be addressed and will ensure that they have access to clean drinking water. The project also includes promoting gender-inclusive climate-resilient livelihoods that will benefit female-headed households (can be implemented through small-scale irrigation systems, livestock farming, or crop production). Training and outreach programmes will also allow women to become leaders in the promotion of climate change adaptation in their communities.

Given the prevailing gender and social norms in Jordan, as well as challenges related to gendered vulnerability to climate change, it is important that this project explicitly addresses gender equality. The gender-responsive planning, resource-allocation and monitoring and evaluation framework will ensure that women’s needs are met and that the project contributes to gender-responsive sustainable development.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 96 of 144

## Implementation of the proposed project is a high priority for the Jordanian government.

The provision of gender equal benefits to men and women, as outlined in the gender action plan (GAP), will contribute to overall ownership of project activities and will result in gender-responsive adaptation benefits being conferred beyond the project lifetime. The GAP addresses gender equality through three main components: 1) ensuring that all project activities are gender-sensitive; 2) addressing gender-disaggregated performance indicators and targets; and as a result, the proposed project will contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

### Project's Gap Outlines Actions to Reduce the Potential to Perpetuate or Worsen Gender Inequality

The project’s GAP outlines actions to reduce the potential to perpetuate or worsen gender inequality in Jordan, by inter alia: ensuring that all project activities are gender-sensitive; addressing gender-disaggregated performance indicators and targets with respect to Jordan's water and land-management policy planning and legal frameworks; addressing women's needs and interests, including through the development of a gender action plan (GAP); engaging women in the design, implementation, and maintenance of small-scale water harvesting interventions; and 20% of women farmers are represented by the end of project.

### Disseminating Climate Change Knowledge Products to All Users

Disseminating climate change knowledge products to all users groups through a centralized platform will help to contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and ensure long-term gender-responsive adaptation in Jordan.

### Gender-Responsive Considerations in Policies

Gender-responsive considerations in all policies reviewed as well as acknowledgement of gender differences within government institutions in response to climate risks will ensure the continued and long-term impact of gender-affirming interventions.

## D.4 Needs of the recipient (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Vulnerability of the country

298. The project target area is located within the Jordan Valley—the primary agricultural region of the country. As a result of climate change, the existing water stress in Jordan through decreases in annual rainfall, higher annual temperatures and more frequent, extreme and prolonged droughts. Because of the country's existing vulnerability to water scarcity, these changes will have significant impacts on agriculture, food security, and energy production, which could have severe implications for the country’s water security. This water stress will particularly impact agricultural productivity, as well as the availability of drinking water and other water resources. The projected increase in temperature and decrease in rainfall will lead to an increasing financial burden on the Jordanian government and the country's inhabitants. The financial impacts are compounded by the fact that the majority of the population lives in urban areas, where demand for water is high. Extraction of groundwater and reduced recharge rates of groundwater basins have resulted in lowering water tables in many areas, which has led to a decrease in agricultural productivity. In addition, the increasing salinity levels of drilled deeper wells as reach water that is decreasing in quality, many farmers abandon wells, and consequently also the availability of drinking water from these sources.

### Vulnerability of beneficiary groups

299. The project target area is located within the Jordan Valley—the primary agricultural region of the country. The majority of households are small-scale farmers who depend exclusively for their agriculture as a livelihood. As agricultural activities are vulnerable to temperature fluctuations, increased exposure to droughts and greater precipitation variability, crop productivity in the current climate will be negatively impacted. In addition, the projected increase in temperatures and decrease in rainfall will reduce the income potential for local communities who already face high poverty rates (see Section B1). Moreover, the project area is characterized by a high proportion of women-headed households, which increases levels of gender inequality in the country, which disadvantageous and underrepresents women in the labour force and decreases their economic opportunities.

300. Future climate change will additionally present multiple health risks to Jordan’s most vulnerable inhabitants, including children, women and refugees. Climate change impacts on health include an increase in the incidence of heat stress-related illnesses and exposure to diseases from reduced water quality—Also, the health of the population is adversely

### Project Impacts and Benefits

**301. Project interventions will directly benefit a total of 245,842 people (2<sup>+</sup>% of the Jordanian population) – 153,736 women (50.9% of the total beneficiaries) and 92,106 men (37.3%) from the Yarmouk site;** 105,400 people (42.8%) from the Khasab Al Wied and Al Seri municipality sites; and **87,655 people (35.9%) from the Kalam site – Shara' Alwahda and Mafraq municipalities.** The project will also indirectly benefit 10,420 households across the sites (4.1% of Jordanian households) through increased water availability for irrigation.

For example, under Ouput 3.1, 2,000 smallholder farming households (-6,000 people – an average of 3 per household) will benefit directly from improved access to water resources and infrastructure (Table 27 and Figures 116-118 in Annex 2). The project is expected to benefit a total of 54,900 households across the sites (-22% of Jordanian households) through increased water availability for irrigation.

**302. 756,000 (48%) women people across the broader Jordan Valley will benefit indirectly from: i) The attenuation of water flow in the King Abdullah Canal; ii) The reduction of evaporation losses and surface runoff from the canal; iii) The reduced loss water through exfiltration from the King Abdullah Canal; and iv) Improved awareness of the impacts of climate change on water resources.** This group includes 40,368 women (53%) in the northern Jordan Valley who will not directly benefit from a project activity, or result of an activity.

This group also includes people located outside the project's three target sites in the northern Jordan Valley.


### Socioeconomic Impacts

**303. Jordan has a substantial public debt utilising USD$42 billion in 2018 (96.7% of GDP<sup>20</sup>), which is attributed to i) the cost of foreign aid; ii) the cost of providing social services such as education and health care, iii) the cost of infrastructure development projects, iv) the cost of food security schemes such as bread in an attempt to improve the food security of vulnerable groups in the country<sup>20</sup>; v) a reduction in foreign remittances; vi) the cost of providing social services for refugees and displaced persons (IDPs and refugees); vii) subsidisation of the National Electric Power Company (NEPCO) and the Water Authority of Jordan (WAJ).**


### References

<sup>20</sup> Approximately 25% of those living below the poverty line in Jordan rely on agriculture for subsistence and as a source of income. A significant proportion of these households are located in rural areas, where they have limited access to services such as education and health care. The majority of these households are increasingly moving from food security for many households. The average Jordanian family already spends approximately 40% of its total income on food.

## Institutional capacity needs

### D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The proposed project is based on the Jordan National Action Plan for Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation, having been developed in collaboration with, and validated by, project stakeholders at all levels across inter alia the water, agriculture and environmental sectors.

- The focus of the Aligned National Action Plan to Combat Desertification in Jordan 2015–2020 is to decrease the degradation of landscapes, thereby enhancing the resilience of the population by strengthening and diversifying their livelihoods. This will be achieved through a landscape approach that integrates land use planning with climate change adaptation into landscape management. The proposed project will be aligned with the Plan’s overall focus on landscape-based approaches for sustainable development and integrated water resources and landscape management.

- The proposed project is well-aligned with the 2020 Strategic Goals of Jordan's National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan 2015–2020 (NBSAP). Specifically, this alignment pertains to: (i) enhancing the national governance of biodiversity conservation into planning and policy frameworks; (ii) reducing the impacts of pressures on biodiversity—such as climate change, land use change, over-exploitation of natural resources, and pollution—from both human and non-human sources; (iii) enhancing the national understanding of dryland ecosystem benefits to national resilience, economic security, and food security; (iv) promoting sustainable livelihoods in vulnerable communities through adaptation at the cultural level; to inform policy and decision-making-supporting tools.

- The project will be implemented by a combination of institutional capacities, measures and instruments to facilitate Jordan's adoption to climate change in priority areas—including water, coastal zones, agriculture and food security, waste management, urban development, and energy. This will be achieved through the following components of the policy, including: (i) achieving a climate-resilient Jordan that is well prepared for the future impacts of climate change; (ii) enhancing the resilience of natural resources and ecosystems to climate change by conserving biodiversity and ecosystem services; and (iii) conserving thriving and productive ecosystems. The objective of the policy is to build the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities, particularly those in rural areas, through the sustainable use of natural resources and conservation of vulnerable zones—through increasing the resilience of ecosystems, as well as water and agricultural resources, to climate change.

- The use of integrated approaches to natural resource management is a fundamental principle of the Climate Change Policy. This policy is based on the Water Resources Management (WRM) approach upon which Jordan's National Water Strategy 2023 is built (alignments follow). WRM and its four pillars are: (i) Water Resource Management (WQM); (ii) Water Quality Management (WQM); (iii) Jordan’s Aquifer; (iv) Goal 2 – Sustainably manage and protect surface water resources and infrastructure; or (v) Goal 4 – Increase the use of renewable energy in the power sector, as well as the use of solar energy and renewable energy in the water sector. (Goal 1: Expand the use of renewable energy across water sector operations)

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 99 of 144

## Use of domestic accountability mechanisms

In 2015, following the recommendations of the National Climate Change Policy, the Climate Change Directorate (CCD) established a national focal point for climate change activities in Jordan, including the country's commitments as a party to the UNFCCC. Additionally, the CCD is the coordinating focal point at global and regional levels for the implementation of Jordan’s NDCs. The CCD also oversees the development of the internal capacity of Jordan’s institutions to implement its NDCs. Furthermore, the CCD coordinates with the National Climate Change Secretariat (NCCS), which will be a new entity responsible for providing accountability related to the project's policy and institutional interests.

Outside of the project's governance structure accountability during implementation will be supported by the National Committee on Climate Change (NCCC), which is convened and chaired by MoEnv, and includes the Ministries of Agriculture, Energy, Water Resources, and Planning. The NCCC will provide oversight for the project’s activities from public institutions, civil society, and academia. The NCCS was established in 2011 and is the highest coordination mechanism for climate change policy in Jordan.

#### Stakeholder engagement

A stakeholder engagement plan was designed following an extensive consultative process with MoGEC—which is responsible for the overall management of Jordan’s GCF portfolio—and the consortium of NGOs (as well as other relevant stakeholders). The plan includes a series of workshops and meetings to engage key stakeholders in Jordan, including representatives from the Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI), Ministry of Environment (MoE), Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), Ministry of Energy and Mineral Resources (MEMR), Ministry of Planning and International Cooperation (MOPIC), Ministry of Tourism and Antiquities (MTA), Ministry of Higher Education and Scientific Research (MHESR), Jordan Hydroelectric Fund for Human Development (JHFHD), Prince Al Faisal Foundation, the Jordan River Basin Authority (JRBA), the Jordan Valley Water Company (JVWC), the Jordanian Red Crescent Society (JRCS), the Jordanian Red Cross Society (JRCS), the Ministry of Health (MoH), and local communities. The plan also includes a series of workshops and meetings to engage key stakeholders in Jordan, including representatives from the Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI), Ministry of Environment (MoE), Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), Ministry of Energy and Mineral Resources (MEMR), Ministry of Planning and International Cooperation (MOPIC), Ministry of Tourism and Antiquities (MTA), Ministry of Higher Education and Scientific Research (MHESR), Jordan Hydroelectric Fund for Human Development (JHFHD), Prince Al Faisal Foundation, the Jordan River Basin Authority (JRBA), the Jordan Valley Water Company (JVWC), the Jordanian Red Crescent Society (JRCS), the Jordanian Red Cross Society (JRCS), the Ministry of Health (MoH), and local communities.

## 513. Community Consultations and Engagement Plan

In Jordan identified priority areas, which the project will contribute to by: addressing the risk and impact of drought—through improved water resource management and supply under climate change conditions (Outputs 1.1, 1.2, 2.1, 2.2, 3.1 and 4.1); increasing domestic production of grain crops, fodder and seed production in Jordan (Output 1.1); supporting the implementation of national policies on climate-resilient LWRM (Outputs 1.1, 1.2 and 2.2), and supporting the mainstreaming of climate-resilient LWRM into policy and legislation (Output 1.1) and by developing and implementing a project-level adaptation strategy through community consultations and engagement plan (Outputs 1.1, 1.2 and 2.2).

### 513.1 Community Consultations were held between October and November to gather and document community perspectives, opinions, concerns and priorities on the scope of the proposed project, as part of the project's validation process.

The group of respondents interviewed by the research team was a mixed social group with an average age of 40 years old (SD = 15.2). The sample included farmers from the King Tal Dam area and only downstream areas from the Kafarrah target area. The research sample was also almost entirely male, with only two females in the sample. Most respondents were involved in agriculture; community members involved in farming. A significant number of young people (i.e., youth groups with ages 18–35) represented by the sample, and a significant number of women (i.e., women aged over 40 years old) also represented by the sample.

## Modus operandi
- **Environmental assessment:** Conduct environmental assessments for climate-resilient restoration, including biodiversity assessments.
- **Development and implementation of protocols:** Develop and implement protocols for climate-resilient restoration, including monitoring and evaluation.

### Jordanian research partners
- **Local communities:** Oversee the development of programs for climate-resilient restoration, including gender mainstreaming.
- **RBG:** Oversee central and community-managed nurseries that will supply seedlings for restoration activities.
- **NARC:** Oversee central and local nursery management.
- **UNDP and GIZ:** Support implementation of restoration activities.

### Jordanian research partners
- **Local communities:** Identify local resource users.
- **RBG:** Develop guidelines on the use of traditional hand tools and agricultural practices in the project's three intervention areas.
- **Technical partners:** Provide guidance on the uptake of climate-resilient alternative livelihoods and agricultural practices.
- **Support the establishment and implementation of Water Fund programs:** Promote and train beneficiaries on beekeeping, vegetable gardening, and other climate-resilient alternative livelihoods.

- **NARC:** Identify private sector champions to mentor beneficiaries on climate-resilient agricultural practices.

### D. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
The Government of Jordan is requesting US$449,903 as a grant from the GCF to implement climate-resilient water management interventions in the Zarqa River Basin. This will be part of a national response to climate change. This will enable Jordan to balance its water demand with water supply in the long-term despite predicted climate change impacts on both. The project will be implemented by the Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI), which have committed approximately US$15,805,746 as co-financing and will be used to overcome the barriers to implementation. In addition to GCF grant funding, the country has committed US$329,133 as co-financing to address the additional impacts of climate change. In addition to co-finance, parallel finance of US$151,133 has been committed by the Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI) for the project's long-term monitoring and evaluation. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with Jordan’s water sector agencies.

The GCF grant funding will be used to cover baseline costs — such as business-as-usual repairs or maintenance to water supply infrastructure, which are not covered by co-financing. In addition to co-finance, parallel finance of US$151,133 has been committed for the project's long-term monitoring and evaluation. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with Jordan’s water sector agencies.

The project will be implemented through a consortium led by the Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI) and supported by the Consortium of NGOs (see Annex 30: Parallel Finance). With the support of GCF grant funding, the country proposes to implement climate-resilient water management interventions in the Zarqa River Basin. These will contribute towards the country's approach to land and water resources management towards the achievement of climate-resilient sustainable development goals. ## Cost-effectiveness of project interventions

### 317.
Jordan experiences intercontinental climate phenomena that increase the quantity of water resources in an already water-stressed country, characterized by a pattern of more frequent and prolonged droughts—a function of reductions in Jordan’s annual average rainfall and increases in the average annual temperatures projected under climate change scenarios. The project will address this challenge through the implementation of rainwater harvesting and conservation interventions on local smallholder farms. These interventions are expected to provide multiple environmental benefits. Rainwater harvesting and water conserving interventions will be introduced on local smallholder farms, with a focus on areas where rainfall is more reliable and where there is less risk of extreme weather conditions. Beneficiaries will subsequently be trained to use and maintain the rainwater harvesting and conservation measures as well as the other project components. The project will also provide technical assistance for policy development as well as a summary of international adaptation best practices and lessons learned used to inform the project's development.

### Cost-effectiveness of project interventions

#### 318.
The project is comparable to the cost of similar interventions within Jordan. For example, the GCF-funded project entitled "Building Resilience to Cope with Climate Change in Jordan Through Improving Water Use Efficiency in the Agriculture Sector (BRCCJ-2029)" will cost US$5 million and the GEF-funded project entitled Management of Natural Resources for Sustainable Development in Jordan (MNRSDJ) will cost US$1.3 million. The overall BCR is 3, meaning the economic benefits of implementing the project are expected to exceed the GCF investment by a factor of three. The cumulative economic benefits of implementing the JLM project are expected to be US$205 million over its lifetime. The total project cost (USD undiscounted costs incurred in years 1-8) is $10,379,874 USD.

| Not Present Value (NPV) | 10,379,874 USD |
|---|---|
| Benefit / Cost Ratio (BCR) | 3.00 |
| Economic Rate of Return (ERR) | 25% |
| Payback period (years) | 9 |
| Total project cost (USD undiscounted costs incurred in years 1-8) | $10,379,874 USD |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL (3.0) Page 107 of 146

## Climate Change Fund

### Potential to leverage public and private finance

Under Activity 2.1.1a, a Water Fund will be designed to channel resources from those who are willing and able to pay for the restoration of degraded ecosystems in Jordan (e.g., landowners, farmers, etc.). The Water Fund will be designed to stimulate public and private investments into climate-resilient LWRM as an adaptation approach. This will include a range of ecosystem services such as water supply, flood protection, carbon sequestration conditions, such as ecosystem restoration, MRV and flaring solar installations. During the Water Fund's maturation phase, a long-term financing strategy will be developed to ensure that the Water Fund is sustainable beyond the GCF commitment period. The long-term financing strategy will include a detailed plan for the establishment of a Water Fund Trust Fund (WFTF) as well as a Water Fund Investment Fund (which will include grant and results-based payments mechanisms) once GCF finance is no longer available post 2023.

## Application of best practices in land and water resources management

### Best practices from similar projects

- Ensuring that landscape planning, implementation of policies, strategies, and monitoring processes are integrated and harmonized.
- Acknowledging the need to engage stakeholders in an equitable manner to fairly distribute benefits and incentives of landscape restoration.
- Facilitating participatory monitoring and adaptive management by ensuring that information is widely accessible and shared with all relevant parties.
- Incorporating traditional knowledge into training for smallholders, pastoralists, and local communities on climate-resilient land use practices.
- Drawing on regional experiences and resources to increase institutional capacity to respond to the impacts of extreme events.

### Developing appropriate strategies

- Applying an integrated, systemic, and coordinated approach to disaster response through the establishment of effective early warning systems.
- Developing appropriate strategies for securing financial resources for project development and implementation.
- Ensuring that all relevant stakeholders are involved in the design process from the outset to ensure the effective implementation of project interventions.

### Supporting decision-making

- Facilitating the adoption and implementation of climate-adaptive interventions to ensure that the most effective and appropriate solutions are being implemented under future conditions of climate change.
- Promoting the use of participatory approaches for the identification, selection, and coverage buy-in from national and local-level decision-makers, therefore contributing to the sustainability of proposed adaptation interventions over the long term.

### Ensuring efficient use of financial resources

- Implementing effective risk management strategies to ensure the efficient use of financial resources and avoid delays during the implementation phase.

E. LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

E.1. Project/Programme Focus
- E1.1. Climate change adaptation
- Increased resilience (adaptation)

E2. GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1–2 pages)


| Assessment Dimension | Current state (baseline) | Potential future scenario (Description) | Rating | How the project/programme will contribute (Description) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Scale | Currently (2023), landscape and water resources in Jordan are compartmentalized and water is managed on a fragmented approach. This has resulted in an inefficient use of water, with isolated water resource management where water is allocated to meet immediate needs rather than addressing the vulnerability of communities to climate change.| The paradigm shift would involve a move away from the current fragmented approach towards integrated resources that are unconfined and interlinked across Jordan’s landscapes, economic sectors, and communities. This will be achieved through decentralized water resource management where water is managed at the landscape level to address the increasing water resource supplies at the landscape level under drying conditions.| Low | The project will result in strengthened governance structures for water resources management nationally and cumulatively improve water security across Jordan’s landscapes over a 25 years though ecosystem restoration and improving the resilience of communities. This will be achieved on the community level, before any mitigation takes place, through: i) developing a Water Fund; ii) scaling up the Water Fund as a long-term financing mechanism (an approach not yet introduced in Jordan); iii) establishing national standards and protocols for climate-resilient investments in water resources across the Jordan Valley; iv) trained staff in national and sub-national programmes to support the implementation of these standards and protocols, including through amendments to Jordan’s policy, planning and legal frameworks; v) developing a Water Fund programme across the Jordan Valley; vi) improving knowledge management on climate change adaptation in Jordan to support national and sub-national decision-making, and build communities of practice. |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 118 of 146

## Replicability

### Low

If a case for the LWRM approach in climate change adaptation in the water sector has not yet been demonstrated in Jordan, it cannot be replicated elsewhere.

#### Sustainability

While the Kingdom of Jordan is committed to sustainable development, including water resources management and one-and two-state actors for adaptation are needed. The lack of institutional planning and legislative frameworks, a limited number of public budgets to address climate change, and ongoing economic management does not consider adaptation in its policies. There is limited knowledge and awareness of best practices for adaptation in the water sector such as LWRM.

The potential target scenario would be improved access to water resources through sustainable approaches to adaptation in the water sector. This will be supported by increasing public awareness about climate change, future climate scenarios. Knowledge availability and capacity building at national and local levels, will support this, with the country as a vehicle supporting an LWRM approach to adaptation.

The mandates and capacity of state and non-state actors are limited for implementing sustainable approaches for enhancing water resource supplies. The Kingdom of Jordan is committed to LWRM but supplemented by increasing awareness in public institutions about climate change and the economic benefits of an LWRM approach. A case study on the Water Fund will be undertaken with project consultants, and at project mid-term and project completion.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 115 of 146

## Core Indicator (description)
Current unsustainable model of sectoralised water resource management exists in Jordan that does not contribute to climate change resilience. However, adaptation of the country's population and economy to climate change implies measures in land and water resources management and governance.

## Baseline context
Baseline gaps identified in the National Water Sector Adaptation Plan (2015) do not reflect the initial decline of available freshwater, nor the projected increase in demand due to an increase in availability from 2025 which is at odds with Jordan's NDCs communicated to the UNFCCC. Addressing this gap will require a multi-sectoral approach.

## Target scenario (description)
Under Component 1: The government will ensure that all actors involved in climate-resilient water management are engaged through its integration into relevant national and sectoral policies, laws, guidelines, and protocols. National-level operational protocols will be developed to support the implementation of cross-sectoral coordination and collaboration for climate change adaptation and watershed management.

## How the project will contribute
Enhanced access of decision-makers to relevant tools, knowledge and data on climate change adaptation interventions across the country.

## Coverage
National level (one country)

### Core Indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute in strengthening policy and legal frameworks for low emission development pathways in a country-driven manner

#### Baseline context
Broadened mandate of state and non-state actors for climate change adaptation - including climate-resilient water management. Integration of adaptation into relevant national policies, laws, guidelines, and protocols will be developed to support the implementation of cross-sectoral coordination and collaboration for climate change adaptation.

#### Target scenario (description)
Under Component 1: evidence of the economic benefits of LWRM

#### How the project will contribute
National level (one country)

### Core Indicator 3: Desire to which GCF investments contribute in strengthening policy, legal frameworks, tools, knowledge and data on climate change adaptation interventions across the country.

#### Baseline context
The development of a legislative framework for EDP has been identified as a gap through the NDCs. There is also need for a legal framework that supports climate change activities, in addition to enhancing the capacities of national actors to implement climate change projects. In addition, the Climate Change Policy Framework (CCPF) will address the gap between policy priorities and plans in the water sector.

#### Target scenario (description)
Enhanced access of decision-makers to relevant tools, knowledge and data on climate change adaptation interventions across the country.

#### How the project will contribute
Under Component 1: evidence of the economic benefits of LWRM

#### Coverage
National level (one country)

# E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Project Coordinator (PC), under the oversight of the UNEP Task Manager, will be responsible for monitoring progress against the project's results-based indicators during implementation. For on-ground monitoring of project interventions, a full-time Monitoring Officer will be employed to conduct and coordinate monitoring and evaluation activities. The PC will also be responsible for ensuring that all project partners are aware of their roles in monitoring and evaluating the project’s progress towards its targets. This will include: measuring performance against the project indicators (Section E.3-5) to evaluate the progress of the project, including any challenges encountered; identifying lessons learned from the implementation process; and assessing the application of gender-disaggregated indicators and ensuring that all gender targets are met. At particular milestones throughout the project – including at the mid-term review point, end-of-project review point, and final evaluation point – the PC will provide a progress report to UNEP for use in monitoring and evaluating the application of gender-disaggregated indicators and ensuring that all gender targets are met. These internal reports will be further reviewed by validators at the mid-term and end of project implementation, as described below. UNEP will be responsible for managing the interim and the final evaluation of the project.

## Interim Evaluation

The UNEP Task Manager will oversee the process of hiring an external independent consultant to carry out the interim evaluation, who will provide an assessment of progress towards achieving the project’s objectives at the mid-term review point. This will be a formal exercise involving a desk review and field visits to assess progress on track, problems and challenges the project is encountering, and what corrective actions are required so that the project will achieve its intended outcomes by project completion in the most efficient way possible. The interim evaluation report will be submitted to UNEP for consideration at the mid-term review point along with an implementation plan. It is the responsibility of the UNEP Task Manager to monitor whether the agreed recommendations are being implemented during the remainder of the project’s operational life.

## Final Evaluation

An independent consultant will be contracted by the UNEP Evaluation Office to conduct the final evaluation at the end of project implementation. UNEP's Evaluation Officer will manage this process and oversee the selection of an external independent evaluator, who will report directly to the UNEP Task Manager throughout the process. An independent assessment of project performance against standard evaluation criteria (e.g., strategic relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, learning) will be conducted by the evaluator in accordance with the GCF Evaluation Policy. Each evaluation criterion will be rated using a six-point rating scale, and a weighted average will be determined for each criterion based on its relative importance. The final evaluation report will include an overall assessment of project performance in ratings between the evaluation team and the Evaluation Office. A final determination will be made by the Evaluation Office when the evaluation report is finalized.

The draft final evaluation report will be sent to project stakeholders through a commenting process managed by the Evaluation Office. Formal comments on the report will be shared with the UNEP Task Manager, who will then provide feedback to the evaluator and make any necessary revisions in line with the GCF’s stakeholder engagement processes. The costs for results monitoring and performance evaluation are included in the project budget (Annex A under budget lines 95, 127, 136, 167 and 180).

The EEIs (MoE and IUCN) will submit semi-annual progress reports and quarterly financial statements to the PMU and the PMU will consolidate the reports and submit them to UNEP as the AE. In turn, UNEP will submit annual performance reports and semi-annual financial reports to GCF. The detailed reporting timelines are as follows:

332\. Under the PCAs, the EIB will be required to report to UNEP as follows:

a. Program reports: by 30 July for January to June;

b. Annual Performance reports on or before 1 February;

c. Mid-Term Assessment report: halfway through Programme; 15 April, 15 July, and 15 October;

d. Annual audited statements by 30 April;

e. Final Evaluation report: within 12 months of Programme completion.

333\. UNEP (AE) reports to the GCC:

a. Annual Performance Reports by 1 March

b. Mid-Term Assessment report: halfway through Programme; 15 April, 15 July and 15 October;

c. Final Evaluation report: within 12 months of Programme completion.

Final Evaluation report: within 12 months of Programme completion.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures (incl. 3 pages)

| Selected Risk Factor 1 | Category | Probability | Impact |
|---|---|---|---|
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description
High staff turnover could impede capacity building, the retention of skills and knowledge, and knowledge-sharing across the relevant institutions.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
To mitigate the potential impact of a high staff turnover, community, knowledge sharing, ongoing training and implementation processes will be spread across a range of individuals, thereby reducing the impact on the project if this factor is realized. The Project Steering Committee (PSC) and technical working groups (TWG) established as part of the project management arrangements (see Section B.4 for more detail), will ensure that staff turnover does not have an adverse effect on the project by ensuring that all staff have access to information on best practices and lessons learned related to climate change adaptation (including ILWM) is available to them, and by providing training opportunities as required. The PSC and TWG will also work with relevant stakeholders to facilitate the appropriate training of all new staff further contributing to the mitigating this risk.

## Selected Risk Factor 2
| Category | Probability | Impact |
|---|---|---|
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description
The implementation of proposed project interventions will cease after the conclusion of the project.


#336. An Exit Strategy has been developed to ensure the proposed project's long-term sustainability and to decrease the likelihood that this risk will occur (Section B.5). The primary aspects of the Exit Strategy include: 1) the integration of the project into existing institutional structures, including the establishment of a Water Fund at the project sites; 2) the development of an exit strategy for the project staff; 3) the identification and training of local institutions to ensure the maintenance, replication and upscaling of project interventions post project (Activity 1.1.2); 4) capacity building in climate change adaptation and water management for the project staff and local stakeholders (Activities are sustained (Activities 1.1.1 and 1.1.3)); 5) the improvement of knowledge management for climate change adaptation and water management at the project sites (Activity 1.2); 6) the establishment of a Water Fund at the project interventions sites, which will leverage funding for the long-term sustainable restoration of upstream watershed areas in the Jordan Valley (Activity 2.1.4).


## Selected Risk Factor 3
| Category | Probability | Impact |
|---|---|---|
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description
Community trust in the project factors leading to conflict, lack of ownership and ineffectiveness of interventions. This risk is mitigated by ensuring that all stakeholders are involved in the planning process (Activity 1.2), the effective integration of local knowledge into project activities and/or lack of effective benefit sharing (for example, large farmers may gain control over certain project interventions, including water).
