# Mitigation Measure(s)

334. The proposed project’s development has involved extensive engagement with local-level stakeholders to ensure their support for project activities. Extensive consultations and workshops with communities and stakeholders will continue during project implementation, as detailed in its project’s Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Annex 6).

335. The development of standard operating procedures (SOPs) by the project's Executing Entities (EE)—MoGriW and IUCN—will ensure that all relevant stakeholders are involved in the development of these SOPs, which will be included in the contracts of all Technical Partners, Contractors, Consultants, etc., engaged under this project. Adherence to these SOPs will also be monitored.

340. The development and signing of agreements between target communities and project EEs related to implementation. Any Technical Partners, contractors, consultants, etc., shall also be bound by these agreements.

341. Project implementation activities will build on and/or draw from existing local committees/groups, systems and processes in the WMCs. These groups are already established as part of the Community Forest Management (CFM) Component 1 of the project, which will facilitate vertical as well horizontal coordination and collaboration between communities and other stakeholders. The CFM Committees have been designed to ensure that all views and concerns are considered in the development and implementation of water monitoring and management plans. Relevant information is always made available to them for their review and feedback on project design, decision-making processes and ensuring that relevant information is always made available to them. These community representatives will also be involved in the identification of priority areas for restoration activities within their respective areas, such as Development Advisory Committees, Stakeholders’ Advisory Groups, agricultural cooperatives, women’s cooperatives, youth groups and Water User Associations).

342. The project will ensure that all relevant stakeholders (including community groups) are represented in WMCs. The representation of and engagement with communities will be built into the mandate/TOR of all technical partners, contractors, consultants, etc., engaged under this project. This will also ensure that communities are recognized as ecosystem restoration. Outputs 2.1 and 2.2) are only undertaken after resources users, who have traditional rights over the land and water resources, have been consulted on their impact on these resources. The project will benefit sharing under the project’s activities, ensuring that no stakeholder group, such as large commercial farmers, gain an unfair advantage in terms of access to resources or benefits from the project. This will also ensure recognition of WMCs in relevant environmental legislation (via MoGriW) will ensure their recognition and authority, including the right to participate in decision-making processes related to land use planning and management.

343. Community support for interventions will also be fostered by undertaking of livelihood assessments at start-up of the restoration sites, including consultations with local resource users to draw on their knowledge and foster their FPIC for the project’s activities. The results of these assessments will be consulted during the development of restoration protocols to ensure that their traditional knowledge is considered.

344. Should access by local resource users to restoration sites be restricted, then suitable—and upon agreement with communities—alternative livelihoods should be provided. These alternative livelihoods must be adopted before the commencement of any field interventions. During periods when resource users have limited access to resources, they will be supported through provision of alternative income-generating activities and/or income. These individuals will also be targeted for the additional climate-resilient livelihoods introduced through the project’s activities. In addition, where necessary, alternative grazing areas will be provided via the project, should this lead to a reduction in access of resource users to grazing areas.

345. The impact of awareness outputs (Output 2.1) will be monitored using an outcome-level indicator for this output through the use of community-based participatory monitoring and evaluation change in climate resilience, as well as water security (Outputs 2.1.1).

Changes in political leadership at local level, as well as within ministries and other participating governmental institutions could result in delays in implementation.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

### As a constitutional monarchy, Jordan's government is considered stable – particularly in comparison to other countries in the region. The stability will be further enhanced through elections via personal shunts in ministries, which are to be anticipated; the project will be fully anchored through the involvement of multiple ministers and their deputies. This will ensure that the project implementation will not be affected by political changes or events that could continue with major interruptions.

The integrated approach project implementation also aims for strong mitigation measures against risks related to political instability. These include:

- Regular engagement with and capacity building of non-political actors – such as NGOs, government agency officials, and local communities.
- Formation of local ordinances (including TWGs and WMCs), mainstreaming of project interventions into local planning processes, and strengthening of systems will ensure that political issues will have negligible impact on the sustainability of project interventions.

### Description

Execution could also be delayed due to the lack of resources leading to potential changes in the project's implementation plan, design and budget under the GCF’s Policy on Restructuring and Cancellation.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- The project’s procurement plan (Annex 10) will be reviewed and further detailed during project inception to mitigate delays. This plan will be reviewed and updated at least annually during project implementation to ensure that lessons and changes are considered.
- A dedicated procurement officer will be appointed in the PMU, who is a full-time experienced procurement officer based in the PMU who will also be supported by a financial officer to ensure that all procurements are carried out in accordance with the GCF’s Procurement Policy. These roles will be absorbed by procurement, recruitment and financial experts as well as the use of systems of IUCN on various duty clerks.

## Selected Risk Factor 12

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Gender targets not met and gender-related benefits do not accrue as expected. This may result from: women being excluded from project activities; the lack of adequate participation of women in the planning, implementation or monitoring of activities not suiting the availability of women given their responsibilities at home including child minding; certain work being performed by men only; and/or a lack of awareness among the beneficiaries on when the ground interventions involving beneficiaries are taking place.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
- A full-time gender officer will be seated in the PMU to oversee the implementation and monitoring of all gender-related activities, including the tracking of progress against relevant targets in the logical framework and coded gender action plans. The gender officer will also ensure that gender considerations are integrated into all aspects of project design and support the implementation of gender-related activities, ensuring that they are appropriately implemented based on their experience and knowledge.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 3.0 | Page 136 of 144

#### Gender-responsive sensitization to the participation and roles of women in relevant activities will take place both at project inception (activity 4.1.1) as well as prior to the commencement of relevant activities to ensure awareness and understanding across beneficiary groups regarding the project's gender targets and gender-related benefits. The project team, including TPs, implementation partners, and other stakeholders, will be responsible for ensuring that any concerns are addressed. All beneficiary groups will also be made aware of the project’s GRM, including how to access it.

The implementation of relevant activities will be balanced to ensure that timing is suitable to female beneficiaries and that childcare is provided as needed. Where certain work may not be appropriate for women to undertake, this work will be allocated to male beneficiaries or other appropriate individuals. The project team, including TPs, implementation partners, the gender officer, project staff, TPs and implementation partners will ensure that harassment is mitigated by providing oversight of all training and information sites where beneficiaries are working. Access to the project’s GRM will also be made as convenient as possible.

Please see additional details presented in the Gender Action Plan (GAP) in Annex II.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## D. Environmental and Social Safeguards (see annexes index, [508 words, approximately 1.9 pages])

The project is being proposed as a Category B or "moderate risk" according to the GCF project classification. This classification is because any adverse environmental or social risks of project activities will have few impacts that are anticipated to be mitigated by the measures described in this document.

### Summary of risks

| Risk | Description |
| --- | --- |
| The risk of changes to the terrestrial and natural drainage systems | Through the correct selection of intervention sites related to project activities and the adoption of appropriate mitigation measures, including the use of best available technologies for implementation, substantial changes to biophysical features of the landscape will be avoided or mitigated. |
| International best practices for ecosystem restoration will be adopted. Project planning and design will be based on training on guidelines and techniques to ensure that these best practices are adhered to. |
| The risk of solid and liquid waste generation | Mitigation measures include the limited use of single-use plastics and the use of water-efficient appliances. Proper training in waste management procedures, including the segregation of waste at source, will be provided as part of the project implementation. All construction machinery will receive periodic maintenance to ensure efficient performance. Waste generated on construction sites will be disposed of by specialized waste management offices or specialist environmental stations to avoid the release of liquid waste. |
| The risk of air and noise pollution | Mitigation measures include the recycling and reuse of as much of solid waste as possible, in accordance with all legislative requirements. All temporary solid waste dumping sites during the project will be rehabilitated after completion. |
| To reduce emissions, the transportation of excavated material over long distances will be minimized by using local transport where possible. This measure will also help to avoid the accumulation of any excavated material near the intervention site and minimize the risk of noise pollution from heavy machinery. The use of electric or hybrid vehicles for transporting excavated material will further reduce air and noise pollution. Activities will be avoided at night to reduce noise generation during construction activities. |
| The risk of natural ecosystem deterioration and fragmentation | Mitigation measures include the selection of intervention sites based on interim decisions related to the design and planning of interventions. In addition, this project will not result in any significant changes to the landscape or vegetation that could affect existing species. Facilities required for project management and support will be located as far as possible from sensitive areas such as wetlands, floodplains, and riparian zones. |
| The risk of disturbance of ecological corridors and the survival of migratory species | Mitigation measures include the selection of intervention sites based on interim decisions related to the design and planning of interventions. In addition, this project will not result in any significant changes to the landscape or vegetation that could affect existing species. Facilities required for project management and support will be located as far as possible from sensitive areas such as wetlands, floodplains, and riparian zones. |
| The risk of disturbance of wildlife habitats or breeding or migratory birds | Mitigation measures include the selection of intervention sites based on interim decisions related to the design and planning of interventions. In addition, this project will not result in any significant changes to the landscape or vegetation that could affect existing species. Facilities required for project management and support will be located as far as possible from sensitive areas such as wetlands, floodplains, and riparian zones. |
| The risk of the deterioration of protected natural resources | Mitigation measures include the selection of intervention sites based on interim decisions related to the design and planning of interventions. In addition, this project will not result in any significant changes to the landscape or vegetation that could affect existing species. Facilities required for project management and support will be located as far as possible from sensitive areas such as wetlands, floodplains, and riparian zones. |
| The risk of the introduction of invasive species | Mitigation measures include the selection of intervention sites based on interim decisions related to the design and planning of interventions. In addition, this project will not result in any significant changes to the landscape or vegetation that could affect existing species. Facilities required for project management and support will be located as far as possible from sensitive areas such as wetlands, floodplains, and riparian zones. |

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 138 of 144

#### Risks to the disturbance of local livelihoods and environment
- **Environmental management plan:** An approved environmental management plan will be in place.
- **Special training will be provided:** To contractors and workers on the scope of land tenure arrangements, including access rights and user responsibilities.

#### The risk of involuntary resettlement
- **Ongoing consultation process:** Ongoing consultations with affected communities to ensure that the restriction will gradually be removed as restoration and rehabilitation activities progress.

The project will not affect official and clearly recognized land-tenure arrangements. The proposed project area is within a buffer zone for the Jordan River National Park, which has been designated by the Ministry of Environment (MoE) in accordance with the law. The MoE will be consulted to express its concerns regarding any violation of their access rights and user responsibilities.

#### Risks to labour and working conditions
- **The project will involve small-scale work activities:** and temporary employment.
- **To prevent the loss of existing jobs:** The project will not displace workers from existing jobs. Workers will be recruited locally, and they will be provided with training and support for their new roles.

#### Risks to gender equality
- **The project incorporates a Gender Analysis Action Plan:** with specific gender targets and indicators.
- **Gender considerations are integrated into all aspects of the project design and implementation:** Responsibilities of both women and men are assessed during the planning and implementation phases. The specific nature of any residual environmental and social risks that remain will be assessed as part of project monitoring.

### Risks will be managed as described in the list below.
- **Undertaking planning, design and implementation processes with close involvement of an experienced environmental and social management team:**
  - Ensuring the project officers (forestry, fisheries, agriculture and water) recruited onto the project are sufficiently experienced to manage the environmental and social aspects of the project.
  - Establishing methodologies for the environmental and social assessment of each of the activities under the project.
- **Ensuring that all project staff have received training in environmental and social considerations:** (this will be done by an M&E specialist with specific experience in reviewing environmental and social considerations).
- **Assigning formal roles and responsibilities for environmental and social management across the project team:**
  - Establishing a clear line of responsibility.
- **Incorporating environmental and social safeguard considerations into the training, capacity building and technical assistance provided to project staff.**

### The environmental and social management processes will draw upon the following documents:
- GEF Environmental and Social Management System;
- UNDP’s Gender Equality Policy;
- summaries of stakeholder consultations;
- baseline data on gender equality conditions;
- project ESMIP: including specific management frameworks and baselines (Annex 6);
- project-level GRM (see Annex 8); and
- project-specific Gender Action Plan (Annex 8).

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
During pre-project planning, a gender assessment was undertaken to determine the differential vulnerability to, and capacity for adaptation to climate change among women and men in the Jordan Valley in particular. From the findings of this assessment and an analysis of the proposed project solution, a gender action plan (GAP) was developed. The GAP includes specific actions that will be taken during implementation to ensure the provision of supporting documents related to gender mainstreaming in climate change, as well as previous gender analyses undertaken by the GEF Jordan Valley Project.

The gender assessment also identifies gender considerations that are relevant to the project and examines potential gender mainstreaming opportunities. Stakeholder consultations were undertaken and focus group discussions (FGDs) were


---
**Page 143**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | Page 139 of 146

carried out in three target areas selected to benefit from the project with a total of 60 participants, of which more than 50% were women. This was done to highlight gender and social norms as its key entry point for women’s participation in project activities within the study area.

## 360. The Government of Jordan has established that gender-responsive planning, decision-making, and implementation are critical components of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Landscapes Management Initiative (LMI), which is a priority. Therefore, supporting transformational change both in the lives of female beneficiaries as well as in promoting gender mainstreaming within the project will be achieved through the following actions:

### 360a. In order to ensure that women’s voices are heard and their needs addressed, the project will engage with key stakeholders.

- **Only 30%** of the FGD women participants knew about climate change and its disproportionate impacts on women and girls.
- The majority of FGD male participants (82%) said that the climate change conditions will affect both men and women in the targeted areas.
- Men control access to resources in which they own the key productive assets such as land, livestock and medium to large-scale irrigation systems. Women have limited access to these resources because they are not recognized as owners of the use of productive resources like land or livestock should include both men and women in decision-making. Secondly, any project activities that aim at improving women’s economic productivity must be designed with the participation of key stakeholders.
- **Women’s empowerment** is a key constraint in improving women's economic productivity in the selected areas. Therefore, the project will focus on improving women's access to credit through the Water Fund — which will be established under Action 2018-2023 of the Jordan National Climate Change Strategy (JNCCS) and the GCF LMI.
- In the targeted areas, rates of women’s illiteracy, poverty and unemployment are high, which make them more vulnerable to climate change. Therefore, the project will focus on improving women's access to credit in order to build their capacity for economic productivity and reduce their vulnerability groups in the targeted areas, particularly women-headed households.
- More specifically, the project will work with local communities to identify women’s needs and interests through participatory methods, to ensure that their rights and interests are recognized.
- The project will also provide support to the Ministry of Environment to activate gender mainstreaming actions stated in the climate change policy and involve women in the decision-making process.

### 360b. In order to ensure that women’s voices are heard and their needs addressed, the project will engage with key stakeholders.

The project will focus on improving women's access to credit through the Water Fund — which will be established under Action 2018-2023 of the Jordan National Climate Change Strategy (JNCCS) and the GCF LMI. In the targeted areas, rates of women’s illiteracy, poverty and unemployment are high, which make them more vulnerable to climate change.

### 360c. To mitigate against introducing gender biases amongst project participants and stakeholders during implementation, the following actions will be taken:

- Ensure that the project does not unintentionally contribute to perpetuating or worsening gender equality and gender-based climate vulnerability in the targeted areas.
- Ensure that women’s voices are heard and their needs addressed by ensuring that women participate, represent themselves, and awareness as well as ensuring that training programmes are gender-responsive. ### Objective of the project with regards to gender

### Financial management and procurement
- **(Annex 509, pages 17–24)**

#### Financial management and procurement within the project
- Guided by UN financial regulations, rules and policies.
- UNEP’s Multi-Country Programme Management System (MCPMS) and the Integrated Project Accounting and Sector Accounting Standards (IPAS), are promulgated pursuant to the Financial Regulations and Rules of the UN.
- Within the framework of the UNEP Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) system, the project will follow a proposal evaluation process, quality assurance and control, project monitoring and supervision. UNEP is updated annually on the progress of the project.

#### Funding of the project
- Established through a distinct grant within the Trust Fund.
- The grant itself being set up in the UNEP Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) as a “Work Breakdown Structure” (WBS), which is itself a Work Package (WP).
- The grant will be linked to the WBS to fund activities contributing to the delivery of specific outputs (as per the project’s logical framework). This will ensure that the project is managed in accordance with UNEP rules independently and separately of the GCF project grant funds.
- In line with UNEP procedures, the project will appoint a Financial Control Authority (FCA) for the project. The FCA will be responsible for ensuring financial management on a quarterly basis for the activities executed by the Ministry of Environment (MoE). Reports to summarize the disbursement and expenditure of funds will be submitted to the FCA on a quarterly basis.
- UNEP will appoint an independent auditor, who will conduct project supervision, in line with reporting standards and methodologies applied in past projects such as the GCF. The audit will be conducted at least once every two years or more frequently if required by UNEP rules and safeguards to ensure compliance with UN financial rules and regulations.
- In addition, a Fund Management Officer (FMO) will be appointed for the project. The FMO is responsible for ensuring that all procurement activities are undertaken in line with UN procurement regulations, rules and policies.

#### Procurement
- All procurement will be undertaken in line with UN procurement regulations, rules and policies.
- UNEP’s modality for procuring goods and services under the GCF is the Competitive Negotiation (CN) procedure. The PAs have satisfied the conditions that are defined under the legal incorporation instrument – including the Project Cooperation Agreement (PCA), which has been signed by the MoE and UNEP, and the Memorandum of Understanding Division and MoE.
- The PCA will include specific obligations for the EUs on financial management and reporting. It will require the EU to ensure that all procurement activities are undertaken in accordance with UN procurement regulations. This requirement also applies to any other entity that is required by the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA).
- As a GoI authority, MoE/UNEP follows the government’s financial and procurement rules and standards.
- During the proposed project’s inception phase, UNEP will undertake a thorough assessment of MoE’s capacity to undertake procurement activities in accordance with UN procurement regulations. This assessment will be undertaken as part of the plan which will be agreed upon between UNEP and MoE.
- The assessment will be undertaken following project approval by UNEP and MoE. The assessment will include an evaluation of MoE’s financial management, procurement accountability, etc., inter alia, review of the existence and quality of policies, legal and institutional framework, and systems for procurement activities. The results of this assessment will determine whether MoE is capable to undertake procurement activities in accordance with UN procurement regulations.
- The results of the assessment will determine the threshold for procurement that is undertaken by MoE/UNEP, which will be reflected in the project’s logical framework. This will also determine the extent and frequency of UNEP’s monitoring of procurement as required by the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). The procurement monitoring plan will also be reviewed biennially.
- The project’s investments in equipment will be undertaken in accordance with UN procurement procedures.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP232

## Proposal name: Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative (JILMI)

### Accredited entity: United Nations Environment Programme

### Country: Jordan

### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remark:

| Strengths | Points of reflection |
| --- | --- |
| Comprehensive approach: Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative employs an integrated land and water resources management strategy to reduce the negative impacts of climate change on water resources through improved ecosystem services, increased groundwater recharge, rainwater harvesting, evaporation reduction, improved agricultural practices and forestation. | The structure of the latter fund will be designed during the first two years of implementation. This offers a unique opportunity to factor in further consultations and ensure a very robust design of the fund. |
| Wide-reaching impact: the project targets significant environmental and social outcomes, including the improvement of water security for households, increased resilience against climate change, benefits to 246,942 people in the Jordan Valley, reducing their climate vulnerability |  |
| Collaboration and co-financing: the project involves collaboration between the Government of Jordan, various non-governmental organizations (NGOs) with substantial co-financing. This collective effort enhances project credibility and resource pooling. |  |
| Alignment with Sustainable Development Goals: the project addresses urgent adaptation needs, ecosystem sustainability in Jordan, all of which are crucial for Jordan's resilience to climate change impacts. |  |

**Page 154**
---

# Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

## Project background

1. Jordan is considered one of the most drought-prone countries in the world with agricultural consumption accounting for more than half of the water resources available in the country.
2. Jordanian food systems face significant challenges, with climate change having an irreversible impact on current and future food production as well as a direct negative impact on the ecosystem services that underpin food security and livelihoods across the country.
3. Farmers and communities face challenges in rapidly transitioning to the less drought-prone, diversified and more resilient local food systems necessary to secure future food production, while simultaneously managing, supporting and restoring their ecosystems in their crucial role as water resources.
4. The proposed project seeks to overcome these climate challenges through focusing on ecosystem restoration, improving local food systems and, through improved governance and water management practices, increasing the resilience of Jordanian communities for long-term benefits. The project presents strong adaptation impacts with significant mitigation co-benefits.

The project is expected to benefit 105,243 direct beneficiaries across three target watersheds in the Jordan Valley – Yarmouk, King Talal Dam and Karafin – achieving ecosystem restoration aimed at water resources benefits across 12,000 ha.

## Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Climate change adaptation governance in Jordan

Outcome 1: Increased public budget for adaptation (total costs: USD 238,394; GCF costs USD 156,505 or 65 per cent)


---
**Page 155**
---

# Component 2: Integrated landscape and water resources management under conditions of climate change

## Outcome 2: Climate-resilient integrated landscape and water resources management practiced in three Jordan Valley watersheds (Total costs USD 132,945 GCF costs USD 122,995.68 or 68 per cent)

### Component 2 of the project emphasizes the implementation of climate-resilient ILWRM across three watersheds in the Jordan Valley, aiming to enhance adaptation to climate change through integrated landscape and water resources management practices.

#### The component focuses on managing environmental degradation by integrating various restoration practices that focus on both passive and active methods. It aims to operationalize these practices throughout Outcome 2, which promotes climate-resilient ILWRM practices, and targets 2.1 million ha of land in the Jordan Valley.

#### In particular, the component will establish and design a water fund to operate as a key mechanism for financial resources to support restoration activities, ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA), and other related initiatives. The project will employ a phased approach, with the first phase focusing on establishing the water fund and developing a solid and robust design model on a payment-for-ecosystem services basis.

#### The component also addresses critical issues such as vegetation loss and soil erosion exacerbated by climate change, employing both passive and active restoration methods.


---
**Page 156**
---

### Component B: Improved protection, supply and use of water resources under conditions of climate change

#### Outcome 3: Enhanced water security for households in target areas (total costs USD 15,891,345; GCF costs USD 12,758.44 or 80 per cent)

This component will aim to achieve enhanced water security for households in target areas. The key interventions under this component include: (i) rehabilitation of active freshwater springs and building of natural check dams; (ii) establishment of rockfall dams to capture runoff from the surrounding mountains, particularly in areas with high rainfall; and (iii) installation of rainwater harvesting systems, specifically in communities that are vulnerable to water scarcity, primarily in rural areas. The implementation will focus on improving the quality and quantity of surface water and groundwater, subsequently enhancing water security for households in target areas. This component is expected to save a significant amount of the total domestic demand if applied across Jordan.

Furthermore, the installation of floating solar panels over the King Abdullah Canal should create additional storage capacity by reducing evaporation rates due to increased shade and wind, thus increasing water storage efficiency, improving water availability and reducing overall water consumption in target areas. Water management plans will also be developed for target areas to ensure better management and sustainable use of water resources. The development of these plans will consider the needs and concerns are considered and that information is made available to them. Training and livelihood support related to water management will also be promoted, including skills such as plumbing, maintenance of water systems, and installation and maintenance of solar pumps.

Overall, this component targets improving communities' resilience to droughts, contributing to increased adaptive capacity, and enhancing awareness of climate threats and risk-reduction processes.


### Component C: Knowledge management and awareness on climate change adaptation

#### Outcome 4: Gender-responsive household behavioral change achieved through improved information and outreach (total costs USD 8,279,500; GCF costs USD 3,229,500 or 80 per cent)

The strategic goal of this component is the consolidation and enhancement of Jordan’s climate change adaptation knowledge management system. This will be achieved through cross-sectoral learning. This would be achieved by the establishment of a national repository for climate change-related knowledge with a view to strengthening awareness of climate threats and risk-reduction processes.


---
**Page 157**
---

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

The adaptation impact potential of the project is significant, focusing on enhancing the resilience of Jordan's water resources and agricultural systems to climate change. The project aims to significantly enhance the capacity of Jordan to adapt to climate change, particularly in terms of water security and food production.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

The proposed project's vision for paradigm shift in Jordan's management of land and water resources under climate change conditions is centered on transforming a fragmented system into an integrated one that can better respond to climate change. The project aims to catalyze transformative change by addressing the institutional, technical, and financial barriers that have hindered progress towards sustainable water resource management.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The project's vision for paradigm shift in Jordan's management of land and water resources under climate change conditions is centered on transforming a fragmented system into an integrated one that can better respond to climate change. The project aims to catalyze transformative change by addressing the institutional, technical, and financial barriers that have hindered progress towards sustainable water resource management.

The project effectively identifies and proposes solutions to the most binding constraints facing Jordan's water sector. By targeting institutional and awareness barriers and leveraging a participatory approach to engage stakeholders, the project is well-positioned to foster a supportive environment for sustained change.


---
**Page 158**
---

23. The proposed project holds substantial potential for sustainable development across several dimensions. It has several economic, social and environmental co-benefits. Regarding economic co-benefits, the project aims to offer community members training in areas such as plumbing, carpentry, electrical work, and other skills that can be used with the installed solar pumps. This capacity-building could significantly contribute to job creation and boost income levels for the communities involved.

24. In terms of social co-benefits, the improved water supply will provide increased access to water which is likely to have a ripple effect on health and safety. It could reduce incidences of waterborne diseases and cut the time spent on water collection, especially for women and girls. These savings in time can be used for other productive activities such as education, thereby enhancing school attendance rates. Furthermore, the upgrades in sanitation facilities that the project entails will also have a positive impact on health.

25. In terms of environmental co-benefits, the use of rainwater harvesting systems and solar energy should contribute to reduced water pollution and improved soil quality. This would enhance soil fertility and biodiversity. The project’s focus on using renewable water sources and minimizing water loss due to evaporation will also help in preventing desertification.

26. An aspect unique to the project is its emphasis on gender empowerment. It seeks to bridge gender inequalities by building capacities, involving women in water management committees, and targeting women as beneficiaries of rainwater harvesting systems and solar pumping technology – to benefit female-headed households. This focus on empowerment can lead to greater economic power for women, equitable access to resources and enhancement of livelihood opportunities.

27. In addition to these benefits, the project aligns well with the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 4 (Quality education), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation), SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy) SDG 13 (Climate action) and SDG 15 (Life on land).

### Needs of the recipient Scale: High

28. The proposal project addresses the financial, economic, social and institutional needs of Jordan, particularly in the context of barriers to accessing climate-related finance. It outlines the country’s vulnerability to climate change and its impact on water resources management and mitigation. By focusing on strategic interventions such as improving water management and enhancing access to renewable energy sources, it aims to address these barriers, ensuring a clear pathway to alleviating the financial and knowledge constraints identified.

29. The proposal highlights Jordan's pronounced vulnerability to climate change due to its critical reliance on rainfall for both domestic consumption and agricultural production. Extreme temperatures and frequent droughts on the nation’s water supply and agricultural productivity. The project aims to address these challenges by focusing on improving access to renewable resources, which is crucial for the local agricultural communities who are at the front line of climate change impacts. It also seeks to empower women and girls in rural communities, including women and children, enhancing their resilience to climate-induced changes.

30. Institutional capacity constraints are also a significant focus of the proposal. Achieving this will require a comprehensive approach that includes strengthening policy-making and sectoral financial planning. The project aims to fill these gaps by fostering better access to information for local authorities and providing training opportunities for practitioners to implement effective climate responses. This comprehensive consideration of Jordan’s socioeconomic context ensures that any intervention not only mitigates immediate climate vulnerabilities but also builds long-term resilience.

### Country ownership Scale: High


---
**Page 159**
---

## 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

### Scale: Medium to high

12. The total investment from GCF is USD 44,904,203, supplemented by USD 5,648,748 in co-financing from the Government of Jordan and consortium of NGOs, with an additional USD 13,330,000 provided as a grant. This demonstrates the project's ability to leverage additional funding relative to the GCF contribution, emphasizing the project’s efficiency in mobilizing resources.

15. In terms of cost-effectiveness, the project presents a favourable economic profile. The economic analysis predicts a net present value of USD 105,279,843 and an economic rate of return of 6%, indicating that the benefits significantly outweigh the costs. This high level of economic justification for the investment is reflected in the benefit-cost ratio of 3.00 further underscoring its cost-effectiveness.

16. The project's proposals detail the application of best practices in LWRM, which are essential to Jordan’s specific climate challenges and water resources management. The project leverages lessons learned from similar initiatives and incorporates adaptive management strategies, particularly focusing on participatory approaches that ensure interventions are both sustainable and community-focused. This approach is expected to enhance resilience across vulnerable communities and ecosystems, demonstrating a comprehensive application of international best practices tailored to local conditions.


---
**Page 160**
---

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**Project overview:** The project aims to reduce the negative impacts of climate change on water resources in Jordan through an ILWRM approach. It has four components covering enhanced governance, resource management practices such as restoration, protection and use of water resources, capacity building for local communities, and awareness raising and outreach activities. The environmental and social co-benefits of the project include reduced sedimentation in reservoirs, improved biodiversity conservation in wetlands and habitats, decreased water pollution due to reduced eutrophication, sedimentation and improved soil quality. The project will also contribute to climate change adaptation by improving access to water and improve conflict management on water resources.

**30. Environmental and social risk categories and safeguards alignment:** The project fully aligns with the environmental and social risk category of mitigation. The Secretariat confirms the risk category, which is also within the environmental and social risk accreditation level of the GCF. Given that the project will have mainly positive impacts on the environment and society, potential negative impacts may vary, they are expected to be minor, dispersed over an area and essentially temporary in nature. As such, no specific mitigation measures for environmental management plans, which can potentially affect specific groups or sectors, will be conducted through a consultation and participation approach. It is noted that any potential adverse impacts would be carefully identified and managed during the planning phase of the project. The environmental and social risks associated with this project are assessed as low to moderate.

**31. Compliance with GCF standards on Environmental and Social Risks:** The following sections assess the environmental and social risks and their impacts for the project and explain mitigation measures.

**32. Assessment and Management of potential environmental and social risks and impacts:** This section provides an assessment of the potential social and environmental impacts and risks of the project activities. It provides background information about the physical, biological and socio-economic characteristics of Jordan’s water resources and waste water resources and waste management. The ESMS has assessed that the project will have mainly positive impacts on the environment and society. However, some negative impacts have been identified and their corresponding mitigation measures provided.

**35. ESMS (Environmental and Social Management System):** The ESMS enumerates the country laws and regulations and international conventions that are relevant to the project activities in Jordan. It also provides an overview of the physical and socio-economic profile of the target areas, as well as the procedures and requirements for assuring compliance with national laws and regulations. The ESMS includes a description of all subprojects/activities under the project. An environmental and social management system will also be established to ensure that all project activities are carried out in accordance with the ESMS.

**352 Labour and Working Conditions:** The project will include small-scale work activities which would create temporary employment. The ESMS has identified the issues of child labour, gender equality and health and safety at work. Measures have been put in place to manage these risks by adhering to the procurement principles and national laws requirements of the AE. Measures include maintaining regular inspections or occurrence and cases of tracking in persons, including the recording and reporting procedures for documenting and reporting


---
**Page 161**
---

such case, consistent monitoring of the labour status and compliance of contractors with national labour law regulations will also be implemented.

41. ESS3 (Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention). The project is expected to significantly contribute to resource-efficiency in terms of fuel and water use as addressing these issues is a priority for the project. In addition, construction activities are expected to generate significant amounts of pollutants apart from minor noise, dust, air emissions from vehicles, sedimentation, etc., which can have adverse effects on human health and environment. These can be addressed through standard mitigation measures that can be implemented by the contractors. Pollution prevention is also a priority for the project as it is expected to reduce pollution levels long run as a result of the adoption of integrated pest management principles and sustainable practices in the agricultural production to be supported by the project.

42. ESS4 (Construction Safety). The construction-related safety hazards to nearby residents, visitors and passers-by. However, these are expected to pose no significant risk to public health or safety as a result of the project. Construction and installation of solar panels are the only major works under this project are and not large-scale operations and will not be concentrated in one area. The risk of accidents at construction sites and road traffic accidents can be mitigated by following standard safety procedures that are applied on site and in transport routes.

43. ESS5 (Environmental Impact). Construction of the area could also alter the ecological balance and result in negative impacts such as resurgence of vector-borne diseases. The increased availability of water due to the construction of rood dams could create an environment where vector borne disease vectors can thrive. Therefore, a comprehensive environmental impact assessment is required for this project. In addition, an environmental and social impact assessment and, if necessary, integrated vector management approach will be conducted in consultation with the local communities. The project will also apply the project's consultative mechanisms such as those of the watered management committees and participants' meetings.

45. ESS5 (Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement). The project is not expected to involve displacement or relocation of people and the infrastructure development activities are likely to be limited in terms of land acquisition. However, the construction of 1 MW floating solar panel or in public lands (e.g. wadi areas for rood dams). Therefore, there is a possibility that some local communities may be affected by this project as their grazing forests and rangelands being subjected to rehabilitation. The temporary restrictions are expected to last up to two years from the start of construction until completion. The aim will be for a maximum level of local user engagement and participation the development of land use restrictions/plans schemes based on mutually acceptable agreements. Moreover, the project will also apply the government's existing resettlement mechanism (GEM) will also ensure that local users can express their concerns with regard to any restrictions imposed by the project.

45. ESS6 (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources). In the long run, the project is deemed to have mostly positive impacts on the biodiversity in the area as it is expected to contribute towards sustainable development enhancing biodiversity and conservation of genetic resources. These positive impacts are expected as a result of the adoption of integrated pest management principles and sustainable practices, co-management of existing protected areas and habitats; promotion of nature-based alternative livelihoods for local communities; and implementation of appropriate policies and regulations, among others. Enhanced activities in the project area, particularly those related to agriculture and livestock production, will have some negative impacts on natural habitats, including vegetation loss alteration of surface morphology and natural water run-off schemes; and wildlife disturbance. The construction activities are expected to cause temporary habitat degradation and destruction, soil erosion, littering and disturbance to wildlife around the facilities. It is therefore recommended that


---
**Page 162**
---

The formation of the integrated landscape and watershed management plans has been confirmed by a thorough ecological study and robust ecological monitoring for each watershed and that an adaptive management approach can also be employed, when needed.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESAT (Indigenous Peoples)
The presence of Indigenous Peoples in the project area has been confirmed in the vicinity areas, which and an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework has been provided. Potential impacts on Indigenous Peoples will be assessed for their lands, territories, and natural resources and changes in the current use patterns of their territories. Restrictions on land and resource use will be identified and agreed upon with Indigenous Peoples. Documentation of customary laws and practices regarding a project is subject to respecting the free, prior and informed consent, and the utilization of indigenous peoples' rights to the knowledge holders, it is recommended that the All with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group (IPAG) for guidance.

### ESSB (Cultural Heritage)
Jordan is renowned for its abundant historical and cultural heritage sites. Hence, this is a risk that project activities could potentially damage artifacts or historic sites. The presence of antiquities in the project area has been confirmed by AECOM. To address this, the ESMP required the conduct of comprehensive cultural assessments for each project component. However, the assessment of the potential impact on cultural heritage is unavailable. It also required that construction contract documents incorporate provisions allowing for the recovery and preservation of any artifacts or archaeological remains discovered during construction activities. Claims found will be promptly reported to the Director General of Antiquities and Monuments (DGAM) in Amman.

### Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH)
The SEMP identified the contextual risks related to gender-based violence (GBV) and SEAH in Jordan, underpinned by the Syrian refugee crisis. The project area is considered a high-risk area for GBV and SEAH. Risks are identified that insufficient legal frameworks and enforcement contribute to the persistence of GBV and SEAH. The project will implement a multi-faceted approach to address these risks, including awareness raising activities at the community level and a parallel process in the GMR for SEAH-specific complaints in a survivor-friendly environment. The project will also leverage existing service provisions based on a network of GBV and SEAH support services organized by UNHCR in Jordan Valley.

### Implementation arrangements
The project will be overseen by a steering project committee and implemented by a national-level project management unit (PMU). Hosted by MoWR, the PMU will include a Project Director, a Project Manager, an Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer and a Gender Officer. The Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer will be engaged as the lead technical advisor on environmental and social management matters. The role includes providing guidance to the project team in relation to the implementation of project components. This includes the implementation and oversight of specific mitigation actions as described in the ESMP, including the development and implementation of environmental and safeguards instruments; monitoring and reporting of the environmental and social performance of the project; assessment and capacity-building towards executing entities and/or


---
**Page 163**
---

implementing partners and coordination with other members of the PMI such as the Monitoring and Evaluation Officer and the Gender Officer where required.

50. **Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure**: The project was crafted through an extensive consultation process involving various stakeholders encompassing women, men, youth, farmers, local authorities, government officials, and civil society. This included a funding proposal (Stakeholder Consultations and Engagement Plan). The interactions involved a wide spectrum of activities including workshops, meetings, focus groups, and consultations with stakeholders at both the national and the local level, including farmers from the three designated pilot areas.

51. **The EMF and the Stakeholder Consultations and Engagement Plan** also provided a structured plan for engaging stakeholders throughout the project's execution. Prior to initiating any of the activities, the project team will ensure that all relevant stakeholders are informed by the PMI. A framework outlining key components and requirements for this implementation phase Stakeholder Consultation and Engagement Plan has been developed. This includes level stakeholder engagements which align with the participatory planning requirements that have been integrated into the design of project activities such as the development and implementation of the gender action plan.

52. **Grievance redress mechanism**: A project-level GRM, which will be made accessible to all stakeholders through various communication channels, will be established. The project team will utilize various communication channels (email, telephone, contact person, personal address) and would consist of four levels. Concerns can be lodged at the lowest appropriate levels, starting with the local level, then moving up to the Jordan Valley Water Users Association (JVUA), the IUCN regional office level and the IUCN Head of Oversight. The Subdargraf Specialist will be responsible for documenting and reporting on all grievances received and how they were addressed. A project-level grievance redress mechanism (GRM) has been established, with a focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

### 4.2 Gender policy

53. The GAF provided a gender assessment and action plan that complies with the GCF gender policy requirements. The project acknowledges the critical role that women play as agents of change in addressing climate change. Women are disproportionately affected by climate change with unequal access to finance, resources, and barriers to decision-making and limited mobility, to name but a few disadvantages. The gender action plan provides an overview of the gender-related issues within the project area and covers baseline indicators, targets, timelines, gender and social expert budget and the interventions that will be implemented in order to address these issues. This includes stakeholder consultations and recommended actions by the Gender Unit of the Ministry of Water and Irrigation.

54. The project will focus on addressing the practical needs of women by including interventions through the water fund for the Jordan Valley, which focuses on improving local women's access to water resources and increasing their participation in decision-making processes on their own communities. Further, it will work on enhancing women’s agency in dealing with climate change impacts and adaptation strategies. The project will also include the Wise Women initiative as central to the gender action plan and includes measures to support women's leadership capacity building and the development of a gender strategy for rural women's voice and leadership capacities to advocate for gender-sensitive strategies and policies related to adaptation to climate change.

### 4.4 Fiduciary

- The United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) will serve as the AE overseeing the project, Motiv and IJCN acting as executing entities. Motiv will manage component 1, while IJCN will handle components 2 to 4. Responsibilities for the AE include overseeing implementation of the project, ensuring compliance with UNEP policies, providing technical coordination with a national level project steering committee and MoU. UNEP will ensure compliance with United Nations rules and procedures, including financial management, monitoring financial management through established procedures, including annual audits and internal controls.
- The entities will adhere to United Nations regulations, with UNEP managing fiduciary aspects. Financial management and procurement will follow the UNEP Programme Manual, guided by the UNEP Financial Management Rules and the UN Common System Financial Rules. Each executing entity will sign subsidiary agreements, clarifying roles and ensuring compliance with United Nations anti-trust frameworks and GCF policies. The legal capacities of Motiv and IJCN allow them to enter into contracts for the implementation of their respective components.
- An official officer within the PMU will oversee financial operations, ensuring compliance with United Nations financial regulations. This includes quarterly fund requests and reporting, supervised by a UNEP Task Manager. Project funding will be disbursed in tranches to executing entities based on progress reports and adherence to project milestones as outlined in the internal cooperation agreement.
- Motiv and IJCN are financially responsible, following national financial rules, and its capacity for procurement will be assessed by UNEP to ensure alignment with United Nations standards. This assessment will influence the procurement monitoring plan, a critical part of the project cooperation agreement. The executing entities will also adhere to United Nations procurement procedures, with MoU/ver conducting an annual compliance review on behalf of the project.

## Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 December 2016, and became effective on 20 February 2017, which was amended and restated pursuant to the revised version of the agreement dated 13 July 2021, and which became effective on 2 August 2021 (the "AMA").

The Accredited Entity has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals necessary for the implementation of the GCF project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, any legal action taken against the GCF in relation to this project (which needs to be further assessed), the GCF Secretariat has drafted a draft agreement on privileges and immunities for the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan, which was submitted to the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan most recently on 19 November 2022.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have confirmed that they are not legally liable for any legal action taken against this project and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Any claims or actions brought by third parties against the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

The Board of Directors has approved the project, and it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in its form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 10 days from the date of Board approval;

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP232

## Proposal name:
Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative (JILMI)

## Accredited entity:
United Nations Environment Programme

## Country:
Jordan

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1. Overview
1. This funding proposal (FP) for a medium-sized adaptation project in environmental and social safeguards category II is submitted by the Government of Jordan through the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) as the executing entity (EE). The accredited entity (AE) is the United Nations Environment Programme.
2. The total cost of the funding proposal is USD 60.5 million, of which USD 44.9 million is proposed to be financed by the Global Environment Facility (GEF) through the GEF implementing agency (GIA), Jordanian Government (JG), and the consortium of NGOs – see section 1.6 below for more detail.
3. The project aims at improving water security in Jordan, particularly in the Amman-Zarqa and Jordan Rift Valley Basins - where agriculture and human well-being are threatened by water scarcity worsened through climate change. The proposed integrated land and water management approach will be implemented across three pilot sites: Zarqa River Basin, Amman Zarqa and Jordan Rift Valley Basins.
4. The project involves implementation of four interlinked technical components (as well as monitoring and evaluation, and project management):

| 1. Climate change adaptation governance in Jordan | Improved governance and capacity for climate-resilient IWRM |
| 2. Water resources management through land restoration, improved agricultural practices and managed aquifer recharge (MAR) | Strengthened water security and resilience to climate change |
| 3. Integrated land and water management approach | Strengthened governance with long-term finance through a Water Fund for upscaling IWRM across the Jordan Rift Valley Basin |
| 4. Monitoring, evaluation, and project management | |

## Impact potential

### Scale: High

The project targets three of the GCF results areas for adaptation, aiming to enhance the resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities; its health and well-being, and food and water security.

Adaptation impact. The funding proposal highlights how the hot and arid climate baseline of Jordan, in which over 90 per cent of its limited annual rainfall is lost through evaporation, and shows how this has been exacerbated by a warming trend that has increased temperatures more common in the past 50 years, with the worst recorded droughts in 900 years occurring between 1976–2003. This has led to an increase of 4°C above average temperature for Jordan and precipitation below long-term averages, resulting in severe agricultural, socioeconomic and environmental impacts. Climate projections indicate that by 2050 the climate will be even more extreme than it is today: for the period 2070–2100 anticipate an increase in average annual temperature by 2-4°C, and a decrease of up to 30 per cent in rainfall compared with long-term averages. This will lead to increased frequency of high-intensity rainfall events that can also cause flash flooding. Staple barley production is expected to decline by up to 50 per cent under future climate conditions, with projected yields falling from an average of 2.4 tonnes/ha today to just over one tonne/ha.

Natural ecosystems and agroecosystems in the country are already being degraded by non-climatic factors such as overgrazing, deforestation, urbanization, and expansion on steep slopes, accelerating water run-off and promoting infiltration. With climate change, it is anticipated that shifts in rainfall will reduce river baseflow and aquifer recharge, and higher temperatures will increase evaporation rates from the soil surface and vegetation. At the same time, rising temperatures will increase demand for fresh water for irrigation and domestic consumption. The Jordan River Basin has already experienced a 20 per cent decrease in water resources over the past decade, with an average annual reduction of 15 million cubic metres at 265 million m³ per year in 2022, and is expected to climb steeply to 835 million m³ per year by 2050.

Project work on the ground in the Yarmouk, Tassur Tal Dam and Kafarina areas of the Jordan Valley in components 2 and 3 (under IUCN) will engage communities in implementing participatory watershed management plans, developed in

# Green Climate Fund

## Project Description

The project aims to implement a comprehensive ecosystem restoration strategy across Jordan’s three river basins: the Hasa, Al Wadi and Al Seru. This initiative focuses on restoring degraded lands through various techniques such as traditional irrigation systems, rainwater harvesting, and agroforestry practices.

### Restoration Components
- **Component 1:** Includes activities like afforestation, reforestation, and agroforestry to enhance soil moisture retention.
- **Component 2:** Focuses on women and men’s involvement in climate-resilient agricultural practices such as crop diversification and permaculture. It also supports the development of microenterprises by local farmers.

### Key Activities
1. **Afforestation and Reforestation:** Planting trees to improve soil moisture retention.
2. **Rainwater Harvesting:** Installing rainwater harvesting systems on community buildings, schools, and mosques.
3. **Irrigation Systems:** Implementing traditional irrigation methods like the ‘drip’ system for efficient water use.

### Benefits
- **Ecosystem Productivity:** Enhancing ecosystem productivity through agroforestry practices.
- **Soil Moisture Retention:** Using techniques to retain soil moisture, such as installing shallow aquifers and managing vegetation.
- **Sediment Reduction:** Managing sediment in small channels to enhance water quality.
- **Water Scarcity Mitigation:** Reducing water scarcity by increasing groundwater levels through rainwater harvesting.

### Project Impact
The project is expected to directly benefit 24% of the Jordanian population (45% women), particularly those living in the Jordan Valley, by reducing their climate vulnerability. It aims to increase the cumulative area of afforestation and reforestation projects over a 25-year lifespan.

### References
- [Source](https://www.greenclimatefund.org/en/project/10390)

# Green Climate Fund

**GCF/PJ9/02/MAP**

## Project Title: Jordan Water Fund

### Background and Objectives

The project aims to address water scarcity in the northern Jordan Valley by implementing a comprehensive approach that includes on-farm interventions, improved water resource management, and enhanced water use efficiency. The project is expected to benefit approximately 756,000 people (48% of them female) across the broader Jordan Valley.

### Project Components

1. **On-Farm Interventions:**
   - Irrigation improvements
   - Water harvesting infrastructure on rooftops
   - Improved water use efficiency through better management practices and technologies

2. **Water Resource Management:**
   - Improved water resource management in the northern Jordan Valley
   - Reduced water loss from evaporation at the King Abdullah Canal
   - Enhanced water quality and quantity of surface water resources

3. **Regulatory and Institutional Level Interventions:**
   - Establishment of a Water Fund Council to oversee project implementation
   - Development of a Water Management Plan (WMP) for the Jordan Valley
   - Regular monitoring at 12 sites downstream of the rockfall dams in the northern Jordan Valley

### Project Impact

The project is expected to have significant benefits, including:
- Improved water resource management and use efficiency
- Enhanced water quality and quantity of surface water resources
- Reduced water loss through evaporation from the King Abdullah Canal
- Improved water security for 10 million people

### Scale: Medium

#### Paradigm Shift Potential

The proposed project intends to catalyze an institutional paradigm shift within the water sector in Jordan, moving away from a compartmentalized, sector-specific approach to land and water management.


---
**Page 171**
---

resource management, in one that is integrated, participatory and adaptation focused, contributing to climate-resilient sustainable development in the country.

### Enabling environment

The project includes legal work to support the Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MWI) to expand the remit of the Jordan Valley Authority (JVA) to include livestock water supply. The JVA will also be supported by the MWI to develop a new WMC structure, with a focus on integrated climate-resilient sustainable development in the upstream basin stakeholders and undertake collective management actions. In response to a question from ITAR about the project's budget, the AE indicated that the project is not expected to require a separate budget beyond the project; the AE indicated in general terms that "Government buy-in has been secured for the project, and it will be integrated into existing MWI projects." The AE noted that the project from MWI with other ministries, through the climate change national committee.### At national scale, the project design includes activities to strengthen the mandate and capacity of the JVA to manage water resources in a more sustainable manner, including drought and water irrigation. An analysis of relevant national policy and legislation will be undertaken to identify any gaps or inconsistencies that may impact on the project's objectives; though there is no guarantee that these will be made. Other interventions to remove barriers to integrated climate-risk informed resource management will include the development of land-use guidelines for the Jordan Valley, which will be developed in collaboration with MWI officials to use the results of climate analysis in planning and decision-making, including disaster risk reduction. The project will also support the development of a Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP) for the Jordan Valley, which will identify priority adaptation actions for project beneficiaries set out by policymakers and planners.

### Knowledge-sharing and learning

The proposed project includes specific interventions to build capacity at national level on climate-change adaptation in Jordan, extending the project's influence. This will include the establishment of a national network of climate change practitioners from government agencies, research institutions, universities, and gender-responsive best practices for climate-change adaptation, focusing on the LWPD. The project will also support the development of training materials, including knowledge tools; and the implementation of an outreach programme, with local knowledge hubs supporting the dissemination of information to stakeholders. In addition, 50 per cent of educational practitioners (30 per cent of whom will be women), will be trained on integrating climate-change adaptation into the syllabuses and curricula of Jordanian universities.

### Scaling up

Some of the key project components have been identified as having strong potential for upscaling. The FP outlines four possible scenarios for upscaling the key project components, which are: (i) the use of rainwater harvesting; (ii) the use of rooftop rainwater harvesting and evaporation reduction (solar panels); Table 11 in the FP shows the potential impact on water supply under each of the scenarios - conservative, mid-conservative, best-case scenario and worst-case scenario. The results show that upsystem restoration interventions alone were to be upscaled across the entire northern Jordan Valley (13,742 ha) would result in an increase in water availability from 50 million cubic metres per year from 34 million m³ under a conservative scenario, to 344 million m³ under the best-case scenario. The same upscaling of rainwater harvesting and evaporation reduction across the JCVY could make a cumulative total available of between 297 and 359 million m³ (or an average of 11.86 to 14.05 billion cubic metres per year).

### Sustainability of results

The project's main strategy for resource mobilization is to sustain the gains of the interventions in the three target sites, replicate these in other sites in the target basin, and scale them up across the northern Jordan Valley (and potentially beyond), by


---
**Page 172**
---

The establishment and capitalization of a Water Fund (Activity 2.1.A). The fund is planned as a mechanism for leveraging financial resources for watershed management in the long term, from water-dependent stakeholders in the private sector (industry and commercial agriculture) and public sector partners. The fund will be used to provide a sustainable source of funding for the maintenance and restoration of ecosystems in the three target watersheds and beyond. It is intended that the fund will be governed by a multi-stakeholder board, and to establish the necessary legal mechanisms and operational capacity. Anchored in the JVA, the fund will not govern water resources directly but will have an advisory steering committee that includes representatives of WMCs from the target watersheds, NGO partners, the three key ministries, water utilities, and private-sector partners.

21. Activity 2.1 also makes USD 7.2 million available for community-level HREEM subprojects through a GCF grant on-granting facility, which "will function alongside the Water Fund's grant mechanism." The ITAP asked the AE if the option of using the GCF on-granting funds to kick-start the actual Water Fund had been considered, thereby leveraging immediate private-sector investment. The AE responded that this was not a feasible option at present. The AE explained that establishing the Water Fund will be a lengthy and complex process, requiring that legislation be passed by Parliament and that the fund be approved by the Cabinet before it becomes operational before the fourth year. The project's on-granting facility would need to start after the Water Fund is established, as it would not be possible for the grant mechanism that will be up-scaled by the Water Fund[17].

Key potential investors in the Water Fund are listed in the FP - large agricultural entities, the Allied Farmers Group, and private sector industry, including the Marwa Group, Arab Potash Company and Jordan Phosphate Mines Company; and water utilities such as the Yarmouk Water Company and the Amman Water Supply Company. The AE noted that these investors will be private-sector players, who will be engaged only during implementation. Given the centrality of the proposed project to the livelihoods of farmers in the region, and given the presence of powerful vested interests in skewed water delivery in Jordan, the failure to engage these key players during the design phase represents a serious risk to the project's sustainability.

24. The Water Fund will be funded by three sources: USD 10 million from the GCF core fund; USD 35 million from the private sector; and USD 87.6 million from the community-run tree nurseries, which will be established as a commercial set-up pre-project in the FP budget note B1-B[18]. The ITAP asked how this shift would be made to a commercial set-up pre-project, in which nurseries would cover their own costs and find their own ongoing customers. The AE responded that this was not possible at present due to lack of information. In response, the AE stated that the community-based nurseries will "act as run income generating enterprises, with the objective of providing a sustainable livelihood for farmers, etc., ensuring sustainability." It is unclear, however; what model is envisaged to achieve a successful transition from fully subsidized project nursery to self-sustaining business.

26. Based on all the above, the paradigms shift potential of the project is assessed as medium.
