1.4 Sustainable development potential

Scale: High

The proposed project is aligned with the United Nations 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development and will contribute towards the efforts of Jordan to meet the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), namely: SDG 2 (Zero hunger); SDG 3 (Good health and well-being); SDG 5 (Gender equality); SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation); SDG 8 (Decent work and economic growth); SDG 10 (Reduced inequalities); SDG 12 (Responsible consumption and production); SDG 13 (Life on land); and SDG 16 (Peace, justice and strong institutions). Specific co-benefits anticipated from the project include:

- **Socioeconomic benefits:** Improved access to clean water will improve farming households' income and nutrition, and reduce healthcare costs accordingly. Social cohesion should also improve through community-based livelihood interventions through activity 2.1.3, which will introduce new climate-resilient alternative livelihoods for farmers in degraded rangelands. The project's intervention of reforestation as well as support for microenterprises for post-harvest processing, should lead to diversified and improved livelihoods. Improved water availability from the restoration activities on shrubs in degraded rangelands through the Hima passive restoration approach should improve livestock productivity and income generation opportunities.

- **Environmental benefits:** The project's interventions in the landscape will have numerous environmental co-benefits beyond the core adaptation results in relation to reduced vulnerability to climate change, restored water scarcity. The restoration and recharging measures should also lead to decreased soil erosion, increased vegetation cover, and decreased sedimentation of dams. Promoting climate-resilient agricultural practices will reduce the need for irrigation and fertilizer use. In addition, with the implementation of agroforestry restoration, freshwater and riparian habitats will be less affected by pollutants such as fertilizers and pesticides from agriculture. Soil quality and water retention capacity to humans by riversite will be reduced.

- **Mitigation benefits:** Some flood reduction co-benefits are expected from the project as well: improving vegetation cover through artificial interception, and reduce the volume of run-off and speed of overland flow water. This will decrease the potential for flash flooding of downstream areas and reduce sedimentation in dams. The project is not directly related to a question from the ITAP about why the project is primarily focused on drought and not flood, this is because the project's main focus is on climate change adaptation engineering to capture and divert floodwaters to prevent downstream flooding and maximize water for recharge, and that the existing antiquated flood reduction by revegetation and more capturing of floodwater through agroforestry restoration.

- **Mitigation cost-benefit:** The floating solar panel energy system to be installed over the King Abdullah II Dam will have a capacity of 1 MW. The project is expected annually through reduced evaporation, will also produce 1 MW of clean energy, contributing to the country's aim of increasing its production of renewable energy and reducing the reliance of the RWE on fossil-fuel-generated energy to supply pumping stations in the area.

### Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: High

10. The climate of Jordan has historically been arid, with a high degree of rainfall variability and irregularity. In recent years, temperatures have increased above 35°C. Approximately 94 per cent of the annual rainfall of Jordan is lost through evaporation, limiting freshwater availability. These dry conditions are expected to worsen in the future as climate change continues to impact the country in the world, with only 125 m³ of water available per capita per year. This baseline water stress is being exacerbated by a number of other factors, including population growth and projected decreasing increases in annual rainfall, higher annual temperatures, and more frequent extreme and prolonged droughts. This water stress will particularly impact upon agricultural productivity, resulting in lower crop yields.

15. Rising costs of food imports are compounded by the increasing cost of extracting usable water from limited water resources. In particular, the 2019 budget for the needs of the proposed project and the 10th replenishment period (RP10) of GCF funding, the FP noted that Jordan is in arrears with the country’s indebtedness that are now five years out of date. ### Country ownership

The proposed project has been designed following extensive consultation with government and a range of stakeholders, and is aligned with the country’s policies. The roles of the AU and other partners in supporting the implementation of the project are clearly set out in the FP package.

#### Policy and strategy alignment

The proposed project was well aligned with the national priorities identified by various policy and strategic documents, including the country's updated nationally determined contribution (2021) which acknowledges gender-responsive management of water resources as a key element for climate change adaptation strategy, and highlights the need for interventions in managed aquifer recharge, rainwater harvesting, and other sustainable water use practices. The project is also aligned with the priority sectors for climate change adaptation that were identified in the country’s national action plan to combat desertification in Jordan 2015–2020; the gendered national action plan to combat desertification in Jordan 2015-2020; and the gender policy for women in Jordan (2020–2025).

#### Implementation

The project aligns well with the climate change policy for a resilient water sector (2016) which sets out the integrated water resources management approach upon which the national water strategy (2023) for Jordan is based – including the following:

(a) “Sustainably manage groundwater resources to restore safe yield levels and protect Jordan’s aquifers”;

(b) “Sustainably manage and protect surface water resources and infrastructure”;

## Roles of key entities

The two main EEIs are the Jordanian Ministry of Environment for corporate governance and the BON-ROWA for project implementation. The roles of these EEIs for their rules is demonstrated in the FP and its annexes 23, and can be summarized as follows:

### International Union for Conservation of Nature Regional Office for West Asia (BON-ROWA)

Through its members, BON-ROWA provides technical assistance to Jordan on environmental legislation, policies and practices; funds, operates or manages thousands of field projects worldwide in Jordan through the Jordan River Basin Water Management Project (JRBWMP) which is based in Amman, with locally based Jordanian staff who are familiar with the national context and experience in working with stakeholders in government, communities, NGOs and other relevant organizations.

According to the BON, "the selection of BON as EEI, as opposed to UNEP, for example, is strategic in that it provides a strong operational foundation for the implementation of the project" (FP, para. 236).

### Ministry of Environment: The MOENV leads the country's environmental governance system and coordinates with other relevant organizations – in initiating projects, policies, legislation, strategies, and monitoring; and to the national designated authority for the preparation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and climate change policy formulation and implementation, facilitating the participation of all relevant stakeholders.

MOENV is responsible for the development of Jordan’s NAP, as well as providing technical and financial management capacity. The ministry leads the development of the country's NAP in collaboration with UNEP and other partners under the UNFCCC and its Framework Convention on Climate Change and national adaptation plan process, and has been involved in a number of related projects in recent years, focusing on, inter alia, climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction.

### Technical partners for implementation: The required expertise to implement project technical components is provided by a consortium of NGOs, whose track record and capacity are set out in detail as annexes 23 to the FP. In addition to BON-ROWA, these organizations include the Jordan Royal Society for the Conservation of Nature (JRSCN), the Jordanian Society for Environmental Protection, Horizons for Green Development, Jordan Hashemite Fund for Human Development, Royal Botanic Garden Edinburgh, and the University of Jordan. The technical partners will be contractually bound through legal agreements signed with UNEP, and could seek cash advances to carry out activities, dependent on cleared expenditure and progress reports.

### Engagement with stakeholders: including civil society

The project proposal was designed in close collaboration between the MOENV, BON-ROWA, and the Ministry for the overall management of the country's GCF portfolio, and the consortium of NGOs, as well as other relevant organizations. The project proposal was developed through consultations with representatives from the Ministry of Agriculture, MWI, JVA Water Authority of Jordan, Ministry of Energy and Mineral Resources, Ministry of Planning and Development, and others, as well as participating NGOs and local communities. All stakeholder consultations were held in Arabic (with translation provided where necessary) and gender sensitivities were considered. Stakeholders were also consulted through the UNEP GCF project management system (communications).

## Efficiency and effectiveness

### Scale: Medium to High

The GCF is requesting US$490,283 in grant finance from GCF to implement climate-resilient IWBM as part of a larger project portfolio. This would make a contribution towards Jordan’s efforts to balance its water demands with the supply over the long term, despite evolving climate-change impacts. The GCF investment will be supported by co-financing contributions from the Jordanian government and other donors, both in-kind and co-finance from a consortium of NGOs. The combined co-finance forms 26 per cent of the total project cost.

The co-finance is described as covering "all baseline costs - such as business-as-usual repairs or maintenance to water supply infrastructure necessary to ensure the sustainability of climate-resilient IWBM interventions, and all baseline costs associated with the long-term impacts of climate change". In addition to co-financial reflected in letters of commitment, parallel funding from other donors is also being sought. The co-finance contributions included under aligned initiatives and projects supported by the consortium of NGOs (detailed in annex 1).

### Cost-effectiveness

Jordan experiences interconnected climate-change impacts that increase the scarcity of water resources in an already water-stressed country, requiring effective adaptation strategies to ensure sustainable water supply. To support these interventions, a full economic analysis across all four project outputs was undertaken using a cost-benefit approach and is presented as part of this document and included within the FP. This builds on previous studies indicating that the benefits of ecosystem restoration to Jordanian society far outweigh the costs,[1][2] including improvement of the country’s water supply.

The results of this analysis, assuming a 25-year lifespan for the project and a discount rate of 5 per cent, show social discount rates on the grounds of the project's multiple social and environmental public benefits, and not intending to provide a benchmark for competing with other projects.


[1] Becker K & Naim DL. 2009. An economic assessment of Damai forest preservation and restoration. In: The Jordan River and Dead Sea Basins: A Shared Future. pp. 35-48.

[2] Wijaya MM & Wetterberg R. 2014. An Economic Valuation of a Large-scale Biodiversity Restoration Project through the Water Sector in Indonesia. Report for the GEF Initiative by International Union for Conservation of Nature, Nairobi, Kenya. Available at www.eid-initiative.org

market rates of return(*) are summarized below. The results show that the total investment of USD 509,913 across all four project options will generate a net present value of USD 105 million. The overall benefit to cost ratio is 3.06, meaning that the economic benefits of implementing the project are expected to be three times greater than costs (to its all parties).

According to sensitivity analysis, the economic summary results above are robust to changes in input values. For example, when the value of groundwater to agriculture is reduced by 20 per cent, the internal rate of return (ERR) for the project is still positive at 22 per cent on an annual basis and ERR of 0.57 over a period of 13 years. A second key parameter is the additional groundwater recharge from the rockfill dams. Given that there is more than one year’s worth of rainfall in Jordan, this is likely to be significant. However, this still results in a favourable ERR for the project — with a rate of 20 per cent over an annual basis and ERR of 1.35 over a period of 14 years. Even if the values used are at levels 8 and 9, the ERR remains favourable at 17 per cent. Overall, this project therefore represents a cost-effective investment.

The economic feasibility analysis also makes an assessment of financial sustainability assessment post-project (in section 6 of annex 3a). For project interventions to be sustainable, there is a need for sufficient funding to cover maintenance costs and the replacement of key infrastructure, equipment, and livelihood and household practices established via project activities. The results show that the annual maintenance cost of USD 105 million per year manual framework (annex B to the FFP) are assessed against the results of the benefits analysis in order to demonstrate that the project’s cost strategy is financially feasible. The study also points out that the annual maintenance costs for the rockfill dams and the value of rainwater harvesting benefits exceed maintenance costs by a factor of five, which means that building up sufficient funds to cover these costs can be achieved through the financial benefits achieved from decreased purchase of water from other suppliers or income generated using the water. The study also points out that the annual maintenance cost for the rockfill dams and maintenance of the rockfill dams and the ecosystem restoration sites. The analysis shows that the annual maintenance cost is USD 105 million per year, which is less than the associated annual maintenance costs, indicating that sufficient funds could, at least in theory, be leveraged via the fund.

The extent to which the Water Fund will be successful in practice is unknown, and the absence of engagement of the key potential private-sector funding partners increases the risk that large-scale investments in water infrastructure and other projects within the Jordanian model will turn out not to be realistic. There is also a discussion in the FFP on specific long-term public revenue sources which might be utilised to support the project. Section 15 of the feasibility study reports on the potential for funds globally from three sections: international studies on Jordanian farming communities’ willingness to pay for the preservation and provision of water resources; global studies on the willingness of farmers to pay for water users. A detailed application of global models and lessons to Jordan’s circumstances is not attempted in this report, but it is suggested that a more detailed analysis (see annexes 7 and 8), will a long-term financing strategy be developed, detailing how financial resources for the project can be generated. It is expected that FFP investments will be leveraged from public and private sources (see subactivity 2.1.4.9).

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP believes that the Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative project applies a best practice FSA approach to enhance the resilience of small-scale farmers in the northern governorates of Jordan. These areas, which are already water stressed and highly vulnerable to the negative impacts of climate change, will benefit significantly from this project. The project also develops national capacity for integrated landscape management and contributes to Jordan’s efforts as an important contribution to the efforts of Jordan to adapt to climate change intensified water scarcity.

The ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

ITAP’s feedback is well noted. During the design phase of the Water Fund (Activity 2.4-1.1) in the first two years of the project, the options for the long-term financing of the fund will be identified. The lessons learned from the feasibility study being built throughout the project period, contributing to the development of the long-term financing strategy during year 5 and R. Much exploratory work would have already taken place by the time of the design phase. The Water Fund’s financial sustainability in the project design phase will also include the detailed application of global models and lessons learned from other projects. This will be further developed through the feasibility study, during Feasibility Study development (Annex 2), best practices and lessons related to the design, establishing a long-term financing strategy, and the identification of potential co-benefits and captured (see Section B5 of Annex 2). Very few examples of Water Funds in a similar context to Jordan exist. The lessons learned from these projects are not directly applicable or lessons relevant to Jordan’s context are sparse. However, were possible, relevant lessons and models were applied to the Jordanian context.

ITAP provided feedback on the design of the feasibility study. We would also like to note here that there are many benefits value associated with ecosystem restoration that were not captured in the cost-benefit analysis.

Ecosystem restoration will reduce surface water runoff, thereby attenuating downstream flood risks. However, whilst the supply of this service is acknowledged as a co-benefit of ecosystem restoration it has not been modelled due to the uncertainties and complexities surrounding the relationship between vegetation cover and hydrological processes (e.g., an increase in surface albedo, reductions in methane emissions from livestock, capture of methane in the soil). The fact that these services are not yet quantified does not preclude meaning that the ecosystem restoration benefits can only be seen as part at best.

With regards to algae, the restoration is likely to lead to an increase in the cover of indigenous vegetation and a reduction in the amount of light reaching the water surface (‘light shading’), because grasses, particularly in the dry season, have a lighter coloration than the shrubs that prevail in degraded ecosystems in Jordan. In terms of methane emissions, it is well documented that livestock grazing on restored land has lower methane emissions per unit of forage produced compared to livestock grazing on degraded lands (see Table 1).


---
**Page 183**
---

livestock, which in turn greatly reduces the methane production from livestock. The amount of methane produced per head of cattle can, for example, potentially be reduced by tens of kilograms per year². This effect will contribute to climate change mitigation as methane is a powerful greenhouse gas. In addition, it has been shown that restoration of rangelands will also capture methane in topsoil (in the order of 10 kgC/ha/year)³. Lastly, with regards to biodiversity, the restoration will in time increase the number and diversity of species on the restored land. This will provide habitat for a wide range of indigenous animals, including insects, birds, reptiles and mammals.

The details can be found in Annex 4: Financial and Economic appraisal.


Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

The AF welcomes the positive feedback from the ITAP, as well as its recommendation for the PF to be approved by the GCF Board.

1 Background

The overall objective of the Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative (JILM) is to contribute to enhancing resilience of representative Jordanian ecosystems and vulnerable communities in the Jordan Rift Valley, Zarqa River Basin and the Gulf of Aqaba through integrated landscape/watershed resource management (ILWRM). This will include the promotion of climate-smart land- and sea-use planning, small-scale renewable energy opportunities, and the diversification of local livelihoods and economic options.

Experience and evidence indicate that women, youth and children are among the most vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change. Women in Jordan are particularly vulnerable as they are dependent on local natural resources for their survival and are children. The Government of Jordan has recognized this vulnerability and is committed to addressing it through the implementation in the context of the Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative will be the promotion of gender equality and women’s empowerment. In order to ensure that gender mainstreaming is prompting gender mainstreaming in national climate change policy making and implementation was therefore considered a priority in stakeholder consultations.

To fully prepare for the Gender Action Plan, an assessment was undertaken in accordance with GCF guidelines to inform the project design on gender barriers, constraints and opportunities for women to benefit from the projects activities. In addition, the Gender Action Plan will ensure women’s participation in all aspects of the project implementation. The Gender Action Plan is based on the proposed project logical framework and focused on avoiding and minimising the negative impacts of climate change on women and girls while maximising positive impacts and social benefits focusing on women and marginalised groups.

1.1 Desk review and Field Research

To design this gender analysis as part of preparation for a Gender Action Plan, an accelerated review was undertaken of supporting documents related to gender equality and climate change, as well as previous research and studies conducted in Jordan—in addition to DSS available data.

This gender assessment identifies gender considerations that are relevant to the project and examines existing gender mainstreaming opportunities. Therefore, stakeholder consultations were undertaken and focus group discussions (FGDs) were carried out in three sample provinces selected for this study. A total of 10 FGDs were conducted with a total of 85 participants, of which more than 50% were women. This was done to highlight gender and social norms related to learning key entry points for women’s participation in the project activity within the study area.

The consultations helped to identify constraints/barriers to women’s participation, their opportunities and prioritize the areas for capacity building to mainstream gender in the future activities.

2 Jordan context on gender equality

Jordan is ranked 138th out of 153 countries in the World Economic Forum 2020 Global Gender Gap Report—it is part of five countries with the highest absolute decrease in its overall score in recent years. The country has made significant progress in terms of women’s education, but challenges facing women's economic participation include: i) low wage levels and for minimum wages; ii) high unemployment rates; iii) failure to regulate foreign (migrant) labor; iv) a large


---
**Page 188**
---

unregulated sector; and v) non-alignment between (academic and vocational) education outputs and the labor market needs?

Inequalities remain in participation in economic and political spheres, as well as in legal rights related to marriage, inheritance, divorce and employment. There are indications that, within society, boys are given more opportunities than girls, which is reflected in their access to education, husbands, and male family members. Structural and systemic gender inequality is a major obstacle impeding women's empowerment in Jordan, preventing women from exercising their right to lead a full life with dignity.

Women's economic empowerment

The socio-economic situation in Jordan is categorized by high unemployment rates, economic inequalities between men and women, discrimination against women, and a mismatch between educational skills and jobs sought by the labor market!

In the second quarter of 2019, the Labor Force Participation Rate in Jordan decreased to 34.60%, from 35.10% in the first quarter of 2019. From 2017 until 2019, the Labor Force Participation Rate averaged 37.73%, reaching an all-time high of 40.50% in the first quarter of 2017 and a record low of 34.60% in the second quarter of 2019. The decrease in participation is attributed to women closing the gender gap in education. However, it has been slow to recognize how this translates into opportunities for women's contribution to the economy.

Figure 1. The number of economically active Jordanians (aged 15+ years) by sex and sector.

The Jordanian labor market is characterized by large gender differences. Labor force participation among women in Jordan is significantly lower than that for men. As a result, unemployment rates among women are almost twice as high as for men. These differences are strongly related to the traditional social roles of men and women within the family and society. Women have traditionally been responsible for household responsibilities which have traditionally been defined within the household context. As a result, any economic benefits women can gain tend to be considered for family and household, rather than individual benefit.

Figure 2. Employment Age 15+ years by Economic Activity Status, Sex, Educational Level & Nationality (% of Employed/Unemployed)

Women in Jordan are expected to take on an disproportionate amount of family-related responsibilities which creates pressure on them because it is challenging to balance professional and personal life. Women also face a higher risk of unemployment than men due to the same reasons as well as because of the perceived difficulties in balancing professional and household responsibilities. The working environment, whether there are many men or not, influence women's decisions. Generally, workplaces that do not require long commutes and which are "female friendly" are considered desirable.

Unemployment in Jordan increased to 19.2% in 2019, although this was not completely out of line with other countries in the region. DGGS statistics for 2019 place the rate for men at an average of 17.1% and women's unemployment at 27.2% (Figure 2).

| Q2 / 2019 | **19.2%** |

Although educational opportunities for young women and men in Jordan are equal, their ease of entry into the labour market is not. This occurs because of several reasons, including the limited range of jobs considered to be appropriate and acceptable for women and employers. There are also psychological barriers that prevent women from entering certain sectors or industries which provide for families. For university graduates (holding a bachelor's degree), male unemployment is 18.6% compared with female unemployment at 30%, underlining the difficulties faced even by educated women in entering the Jordanian job market.

In Jordan, women’s participation in the labour market is limited by gender-specific social constraints, such as cultural and class-based expectations regarding women's household roles and responsibilities. These factors also limit their access to education and training opportunities for women. Some legal regulations reinforce notions of a woman's work being secondary to that of her husband. For example, Jordanian law states that female employees in the public sector are not entitled to receive family allowance if the fathers of their children have received such allowances. Similarly, a woman is not entitled to be transferred to her husband unless he is unfit for work and does not have any other source of income.

There are extraordinary differences for women in the work force by sector, with the public sector employing 50% of all female workers compared with only 12% employed in the private sector. Women working in male-dominated environment, such as construction or engineering. Many poor women work out of economic necessity, although a substantial number of working women come from middle-class and upper-middle-class families (38% of self-employed women are from middle- to upper-empolyed women), and technicians and associate professionals (24.0%). Women constitute 54.6% of all administrative and service workers in the public sector.

The 1996 Labor Code applies to all employees in the formal sector, with the exception of domestic agricultural and unskilled workers. It stipulates normal working hours, allows for temporary contracts and prohibits child labour above age 12 as well as giving the Minister of Labour the power to either temporarily or permanently shut down any enterprise in which an incident of sexual


---
**Page 191**
---

harassment (as defined in the Penal Code) has taken place. The employment of civil servants is governed by the Civil Service Statute amended in 2007.

Cultural constraints also clearly mitigate against women working in areas of low prestige, in jobs requiring travel and unusual working hours or contact/privacy with strangers; these often lie in areas with the most critical labor shortages, such as tourism[1].

### Women's political participation

A gender quota system for political participation in the Parliament was adopted in 2003 and revised in 2017. The current law requires that at least one-third of all candidates be women (54 out of 165 total available seats for elected representatives). However, women’s political participation in Jordan remains low[2]. The Political Gender Gap Index ranks Jordan at 113th under the 189 countries included. Moreover, the 2020 Global Gender Gap Report ranks Jordan 110th out of 153 countries. Traditional gender stereotypes are used to justify low levels of women’s political participation in Jordan[3]. Women are often perceived as not having the skills or experience that hinders them from effectively contributing in the decision-making process by not conveying the actual needs and interests of their constituents, which is considered a male domain. Women have experienced a male domain and decisions are taken by men and therefore generally serve male interests.

2.3 Peace, and Security

Jordan is one of 80 countries that have developed a National Action Plan for the implementation of Security Council resolution 1325 on Women, Peace and Security. Resolution 1325 is a landmark achievement in international law that recognizes women’s rights as human rights and their full participation in sustaining peace and building security. The Jordanian National Action Plan (JONAP) included actions to drive women's engagement in local, regional, and national processes of peacebuilding and conflict resolution.

### 2.4 Rule of Law

Jordan ratified the International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1992. However, the country maintains reservations on some articles, such as giving women equal rights with men in marriage and divorce [1], rights and responsibilities as parents, as well as personal rights — including inheritance laws. In 2015, the Jordanian Constitutional Court ruled that a 2014 Supreme Court decision lifted its reservations on giving women freedom of mobility and choice of residence without consent of their husbands or other male family members. Some progress has also been achieved in the area of family law with the adoption of new laws to protect women's rights in general, but improvements are still needed. As the Arab Women Organization (AWO) notes, "the personal status law is not a law for all Jordanians; it is a law for Jordan itself as there is no political will to genuinely promote equality in the public and political sphere." [2]

Legislations in Jordan is civil, with the exception of the Personal Status Law that is based on religion. Therefore, women are subject to both civil and religious laws. Although the country has made progressive amendments, significant legal inequalities persist in issues related to personal status. The AWO notes that "the law does not provide for equal rights between men and women; it is not sufficiently addressed." This situation contributes towards the legal perpetuation of the inferior status of women in society, which plays an essential role in the continuation of gender stereotypes.

### 2.5 Health

The health situation in Jordan is amongst the best in the Middle East because of the secure and stable conditions in the Kingdom. It is also a result of a set of effective development plans and projects which included health as an important element and essential part of sustainable development strategies. The country has made significant progress in improving its health sector, reflected positively on the wellbeing of citizens. The general health 2018 indicators have reflected the quality and efficiency of the delivered health services, putting Jordan in an advanced rank globally.

# Life Expectancy & Fertility Rates

| Figure 5 | Life expectancy and fertility rates |
| --- | --- |
| ![Life Expectancy & Fertility Rates](https://example.com/image.png) |

There was a comparatively rapid decline in fertility between the 1990 and 2002 — according to JPFHS surveys—from 5.6 children per woman to 3.7 children per woman (Figure 5). The TFR remained relatively stable between 2007 and 2012, ranging from 3.8 to 3.6 children per woman, before declining slightly in the most recent survey.

Among women age 30–49, the median age at first birth is approximately 25 years. This is related to educational level; as the most educated women tend to marry later (by approximately five years) than women with less education.

Approximately half of married women (49%) want no more children or have been sterilized. However, ~7% of married women are undecided about whether they want more children, but men are much less likely to be uncertain (2%). Women who are undecided tend to be younger and plan for future childbearing. The mean ideal family size among currently married women (age 15–49) is 2.9 children; compared with men, who have an ideal family size of 3.8 children.

The proportion of married women age 15–49 currently using any contraceptive method decreased from 60% in the 2007 survey to 50% in the most recent survey (Figure 6). In ~26%, men in some governorates tend to decide about use of family planning for their wives which can impact women health and rights.

Mortality rates are generally higher among children born to Syrian women than among children born to Jordanian women or women of other nationalities. Overall, the under-five mortality rate is 25 deaths per thousand live births (Figure 7). The highest mortality rates were observed for live births among children whose mothers are Jordanian or of other nationalities. However, women face some challenges in accessing health services during pregnancy and childbirth, including difficulty from getting medical advice or treatment for themselves when they are sick, particularly during pregnancy and at the time of delivery. 42% of married women age 15–49 in Jordan report having at least one problem with access to family planning services. The most common problems reported were: i) having to take transport (25%) and not wanting to go alone (24%); ii) distance to a health facility (23%); iii) fear of being judged by others (18%). Other problems included lack of money; the problem reported least often was getting permission to go for treatment. (% of respondents).

The total fertility rate in rural areas is higher (3.1 children) compared to urban areas (2.7). This result is in line with the average size of the Jordanian family of 4.8 person, as determined by the General


---
**Page 194**
---

Population and Housing Census – 2015. The largest disparity in total fertility rate is among Syrian women (at 4.7), compared with 2.6 children per Jordanian woman and 1.9 per woman from other nationalities. Among women overall, mean ideal number of children is slightly higher in rural (4.0 children) than urban (3.8 children) areas; this number is higher among women of Syrian nationality (4.1 children) than among women of Jordanian nationality.

### 2.6 Education

Jordan has made great progress in increasing access to education, with nearly universal primary enrollment, gender parity in Jordanian schools, and a 98% adult literacy rate (Figure 6). Despite widespread access to education, challenges remain.

Figure 6. Indicators of Education (2006–2018).

Schools are overcrowded and in varying states of disrepair; teachers receive limited training and support, and many are underqualified, with only a few second- and third-graders reading at grade level. The growing population of Jordanians and the influx of refugees have placed additional pressure on schools. Children from poor socio-economic backgrounds, children involved in child labor and those with disabilities and special needs face particular challenges. Children from poor socio-economic backgrounds are at greater risk of either dropping out or being out of education altogether. Early childhood education provides opportunities for young children; only 13% of children aged 3 to 4 years old accessing Kindergarten 1 (KG1), and 59% of those aged 5 to 6 years old attending Kindergarten 2 (KG2).

Sending a girl to school allows her to build confidence and contribute to the country’s economic, social and political development. Although education in Jordan has reached gender parity since 1998.


---
**Page 195**
---

social norms and traditions, along with other factors, block Jordanian girls from fully utilizing their education in the job market. Most female students are enrolled in secondary school than male students. Boys are more likely to drop out of school due to poor academic achievements, violence, bullying, and lack of interest in studies. Girls are more likely to drop out because of limited availability of male teachers, violence in schools, availability of quality school environments and unfavourable student-teacher ratio.

Meanwhile, girls are more likely to be kept at home because of protection concerns and household responsibilities such as looking after younger siblings or caring for parents. Girls are also far more likely to be married at an early age. There is no specific gender disparity in Jordanian primary schools. Over 94% of girls and boys attend school, with a higher percentage of girls attending school than boys in urban areas to attend school. Approximately 10% of girls who are of secondary school age (12-17) do not attend school because they are married or have other responsibilities at home.

Since 14% of the countries live below the poverty line, child marriage occurs often among Jordanian girls in order for parents to be relieved of their financial responsibility. Around 13% of girls in Jordan marry before turning 18 years of age. Among men aged 20-49, by contrast, only 1% were married before they turned 18.

The Syrian refugee crisis has strongly impacted on education in Jordan. It has created overcrowded classes and increased the operational costs for the government. The government strives to improve its educational standards for girls and boys alike, despite the setbacks caused by the humanitarian crisis. UNICEF is partnering with the Ministry of Education to educate refugees and supply clean furniture for learning spaces. Pre-schools provide Jordanian children safe child-free zones for Syrian refugee children under the age of 5, which increases their chances of attend primary school.

## Figure 7. Higher education in Jordan.

Jordan has one of the highest literacy rates for girls in the Middle East, which is estimated to be as high as 97%. However, this educational advancement does not translate over to the workforce. Jordan ranks last among all countries in the region with regard to female participation in the labour force. If a gender gap in Jordan’s workforce continues to exist, the country will experience a reduction in potential GDP growth of up to 0.3% per annum.

A good education is a guarantee that girls will find employment. Approximately 30% of Jordanian women with a university degree or above are unemployed. The percentage of woman that believes there are no jobs for them is 25%. Many Jordanian women who have a cultural and religious background that pressures women to stay at home, as well as a lack of women job opportunities. There is a large socio-economic gap that exists in Jordan. In 2008 only 16% of girls from wealthy households were unemployed compared with 45% of girls from poor households. Only 73% of girls from wealthy households attend university, while 91% of girls from wealthier households are reserved for private education.

Learning outcomes in Jordan are poor throughout basic and secondary education. 70% of students in Grades 2 and 3 read without comprehension[25]. Students consistently perform poorly in international assessments such as PISA and TIMSS.

Education for women and men as well as vocational training programs in the growing sectors of the country’s economy will contribute to macroeconomic growth and also gender equality[26]. According to the World Bank, Jordan has one of the highest female literacy rates among countries where a large portion of its population is educated. In Jordan, education has progressed much faster than changes in behaviour concerning motherhood. This is due to the low employment rate of women because of social constraints and lack of job opportunities. The number of births per woman (total fertility rate) has decreased. This is especially the case for the poorest women, while the gap narrows in the wealthiest categories.

Family Health Survey 2017-2018, showed a decline in the Total Fertility Rate from 5.6% in 1990 to 3.7% in 2004 and further down to 3.1% in 2017-2018, which means that a woman in Jordan has an average of 2.7 children throughout her life.

2.7 Gender norms

Jordan has witnessed changes over the past decades that included the social construction and the prevailing norms as a result of major changes in the educational, economic and technological aspects. These changes appeared as family patterns began to shift from the extended family to the nuclear family. This change was accompanied by the economic situation: As a result, the perception of women's education and work, reproduction and social roles changed – particularly in urban and civilized areas — and the change of the standards that control behavior.

However, the tribe still represents the primary social structure (paternal/masculine), and its orientation towards family values, norms, and traditions define an individual’s awareness and concepts, and form their culture and behaviour especially regarding women. The husband is considered to be the head of the household and the main source of income for the family and the woman as wife and mother. According to prevailing social norms, the role of a woman in her family is to take care of children and the home; family honor and dignity can be maintained. The norms and values that underscore this pattern present obstacles in the way of women who hope to fully balance their productive and reproductive roles.

Decision-making in the household depends on the issue at hand and is influenced by level of education, residency, age, status in the family and control of assets. With the exception of decision on working outside the home, women are not involved in major decisions regarding health care, major household purchases, and visits to relatives or friends. Women generally have no say in the increasing trend of men's involvement in major household purchases[1].

2.8 Women-headed households in Jordan

Women-headed households constitute about 17.5% of Jordan’s households. By region, religion, and household size they differ little from households in the country at large. 392,300 families out of 2.2 million are headed by women. The majority (64%) of these women are aged between 25-49 years old. Most of them (81%) have completed primary education or higher and are economically inactive. 105,642 households received monthly assistance from the National Aid Fund for Women and Children. These women are more likely to be living below the poverty line, compared with about 13 percent in the population at large. Their annual spending is less than one third of that of men[2]. The average age of these women is 40 years old; their average income per home: 78 percent of them are not and have never been employed. Their education levels are low: about 53% did not complete primary school, while only 16% completed secondary or higher secondary education. The problems faced by women household heads include malnutrition (a problem in nearly one in five of these families); social stigma attached to themselves and their children; lack of access to health care services; difficulties with schooling for their children at school and become socially withdrawn; and pressures from their own or their husband’s family[3].

2.9 Women in the agricultural sector

Although the contribution of the agriculture sector to Jordan's Gross Domestic Product (GDP) and labor force has been declining over decades, women’s participation in the sector remains a critical source of employment for the country’s poorest citizens, and also serves as a major source of subsistence income for many rural households[4]. While only 2% of the total working population is employed in agriculture in Jordan – and 9.5% of its total female labor force – was employed in agricultural jobs as of 2014.

approximately 23% of the total number of poor people who live in rural areas continue to depend on agriculture as a primary source of livelihood[1].

The agricultural sector in Jordan is also known to have the highest proportion of informal workers compared to other economic sectors. Approximately 16% of women who work in the agricultural sector are employed informally, compared with only 5% of men (~5%). According to FAO, women in Jordan are also actively involved in home-based agricultural activities, typically managing small household gardens and looking after the family plot[2].

Despite women's seemingly active role in the agriculture sector, a review of existing literature and secondary data sources reveals that there is limited information on their level of economic involvement in the sector, possibly due to its informal nature[3]. While some research exists on the challenges faced by rural women in Jordan, particularly regarding land ownership, gender-disaggregated data is available on the challenges specific to women working in the sector.

The majority of the rural population depend either directly or indirectly on agriculture for their livelihoods. Women are more vulnerable than men because they have less access to resources and because of their greater dependence on agriculture; their relatively lower ability to adapt and the high share of income they spend on food. Climate change could undermine the progress that has been made in poverty reduction, especially among rural women who are particularly vulnerable for vulnerable rural populations. The negative impacts of climate change will be strongly women.

The role of women in the rural economy is substantial. Women are traditionally responsible for the household and family welfare[4]. They play a significant role in managing small-scale agriculture and rural enterprises in drylands as farmers, animal caretakers, workers and entrepreneurs. Women's participation in agricultural activities is essential to ensure food security and provide for food, nutrition and income security. However, while having the legal right to own land, women face numerous barriers that prevent them from fully participating in agriculture[5]. Legal and social restrictions on land inheritance have particular implications for women's access to land and their ability to participate in agricultural activities.

The role of women is further constrained by a number of factors including: gendered disincentives for women to purchase land[6], legal and social restrictions on land inheritance have particular implications for women's access to land and their ability to participate in agricultural activities. Women are also subject to the customary patriarchal inheritance systems, where property is transferred to and through male members of a family. In Jordan, for example, given the customary patriarchal inheritance system, women account for only 5% of agriculture holders in Jordan; though the number of large landowners has increased over time[7]. According to a recent study, it was found that the majority of female respondents were workers on and not owners of their farms. Only about one-third (34%) of female farmers are landholders, while only 11% is shown reported that her farm ownership is of private partnership nature[8].

Women empowerment programs may be another source of support for farms as these farms already utilize women labour in agricultural operations. In fact, 59.8% of the farms surveyed have women among their labour. The nature of work performed by women include cutting and picking (62.3%).

# 2.10 Women and land ownership

## Property ownership is a male domain in Jordan, where women are dependence on men for housing.

A patriarchal society that dominates both inheritance and property. While the inheritance rights of women are formally enshrined in the constitution; in Islamic law (Sharia), and in the customary law practiced by Bedouin tribes, women have no legal right to inherit land or other property. Women renounce their rights in favor of male heirs.

Most women either do not receive the share of inheritance that is due them under Sharia law, or they are forced to sell their shares for a fraction of its value. The Jordanian Sharia Court data show that in 2014 a third of heirs fully relinquished their inheritance rights and sold their shares at a fraction of their worth.

The Jordanian Sharia Court data show that in 2014 a third of heirs fully relinquished their inheritance rights and sold their shares at a fraction of their worth. The Jordanian Sharia Court data show that in 2014 a third of heirs fully relinquished their inheritance rights and sold their shares at a fraction of their worth.

## Women owned only 6.8 percent of all houses in Jordan

Women owned 19.5 percent of registered apartments in Jordan in 2014. But since apartment complexes are usually built on land that is owned by men, women own only about one-third of the land where they live. In 2013, women owned a mere ten percent of all apartments.

In comparison, women owned 22.5 percent of commercial and industrial properties in Jordan in 2014. Women also own 68.7 percent of shares in joint-stock companies and 90.5 percent of shares in private limited liability companies (LLCs). They hold securities in the name of their wives to protect their assets from creditors, if their business goes bankrupt.

Women are not allowed to inherit land or other property under Islamic law. Women are also barred from owning large tracts of land and are significant players in the nation. Sharia law has been applied to Christian property inheritance since the Ottoman period in Jordan, and although Christian tradition stipulates that women should be given a share of their father's estate, this is not always respected.

## As a consequence of such cultural norms

only 6.8 percent of ever married women owned their houses and only 1 percent owned land in 2012. A recent trend has seen women managing to register residential properties under their own names or those of their children. Women owned 19.5 percent of registered apartments in Jordan in 2014.

## Women's rights activists have succeeded

in amending the Jordanian Personal Status Law No 36 of 2015. The law was amended by the Sharia Supreme Court (Qa'di) dr. Qutaibah, the country’s highest religious and legal institution. In 2011 seven amendments were made and two articles added: articles 318 and 319. Article 318 states that a woman can inherit from her father or husband if she is not married within six months after the death of the owner before either exclusion (baka'a) nuqra or power of attorney (wakla) is being invoked. Another amendment stated that an attorney dealing with an estate was


---
**Page 200**
---

under obligation to inform heirs of all properties included in an inheritance. Another specified that a judge must explain to his heirs the legal consequences of the exclusion of inheritance: this has resulted in judges asking for better education for women so that they can better defend their rights. This important role of women as inheritors is also reflected in the UN’s 2013 poverty reduction strategy, which calls for policies and programs aimed at reducing poverty and a means of preventing women from falling into destitution. Property rights and security of tenure are key to improving women’s economic status and increasing their participation in social and sustainable development. The United Nations’ human settlement program, UN-Habitat, and the Office of the High Commissioner for Human Rights support property rights and the security of tenure under the 1949 Geneva Convention relative to the Protection of Civilian Persons in Time of War. Women’s economic status is also linked with their role as inheritors: women’s access to land, credit, and women in the economy, and inheritance should be a major dimension of this policy[20].
### 2.11 Women and Water
Jordan suffers from water scarcity—i.e., fact that scarce percent strain on its social and economic development. In Jordan, about 60% of Jordan’s water is used for irrigated agriculture. Water allocation for domestic use comprises less than 30%. In terms of drinking water allocations, cuts below a certain threshold endanger the very survival of people.
Women play an important role in water management. They are often the collectors, users and managers of water resources. Women collect rainwater from roofs, harvested and rainfed crops. Because of these roles, women have considerable knowledge about water resources, including quality and reliability of water sources, and how to manage water resources for household use and other productive resources development[21]. Decisions about water rationing have a major impact on women; since they are the ones who collect and distribute water in their households. A study conducted by USAID in 2012 highlighted that, both women and men mentioned that water scarcity was one of the main problems affecting their lives. At times, women must be at home during those periods in order to complete their household chores[22].
Women are not only responsible for food preparation, but also for keeping the family healthy, which means they have a great deal of knowledge about health and hygiene practices. Women can become potentially strong advocates for clean water. Therefore, it is important to educate women on the connection between safe drinking water, contamination of the water supply, and the impact on the health of their families[23]. In Jordan, women are often involved in water management activities; they are women advocates who are showing interest in policymaking and political engagement, making them potential leaders. The role of women as water managers is also reflected in the fact that women involved in these activities were very successful in achieving their objectives. They had a greater impact on water resources than men[24].
### 2.12 Climate change
In the fight against climate change, the role of women as agents of change is frequently overlooked. Women are often perceived as passive members of households or indirect victims of climate change. However, they can be powerful agents of change in their communities and among other practitioners, and influential agents of change. Empowering women to harness their leadership capacities is therefore essential. Women can inform and implement actions and strategies related


---
**Page 201**
---

to water management, energy, agriculture and food security, economic growth and livelihoods,
waste management, and policymaking.

In this regard, a study released in 2010 by the International Union for the Conservation of Nature (IUCN) demonstrated the strength of women's ability to enact social change by utilising their knowledge and experience. The Climate Change Adaptation Programme (CCAP) outlined a gender-sensitive Climate Action Plan (coGAP) outlined a framework for integrating a gender perspective in climate change efforts, acknowledging the fundamental role of women in the sectors of water management, energy, agriculture and food security, economic growth and livelihoods,
make to climate change adaptation and mitigation[2].

### 3. Demographic Information

Jordan, as an Arab kingdom in the Middle East. It is located between the Mediterranean and Red Sea and is drained by the Jordan River. The land itself is mostly flat and covers 34,495 square miles (89,342 square kilometres) of surface area.

95-97% of Jordanians are of Arab decent, but there are many other ethnic groups in the country.
There are ~500,000 Iraqis, over 500,000 Syrians, ~30,000 Kurds, ~5,900 Armenians Assyrian Christians and ~12,000 Circassian. The population of Jordan is approximately 8 million people,
which makes it the 89th most populous nation. Jordan is an extremely urban country, with nearly all its population living in cities or towns.

Jordan has become the destination of several waves of migrants from Palestine and recently from Iraq and Syria. According to UNHCR, there are currently over 100,000 refugees registered as Syrian refugees.

The overwhelming majority of the population of Jordan is Arab, and mostly Sunni Muslim. Jordanian society also includes ethnic minorities, including Circassians, Kurds, Turkomans, Chechens, and Armenians. The Bedouin are a significant part of the rural community, and there is a small Druze community. The Jordanian state as a whole is very supportive of all these groups, which coexist in Jordanian society. This coexistence is reflected in the family and tribal system, as well as modern institutions — for example family associations and charitable organizations that are often run by ethnic or religious groups. They also play an important role in supporting a collective Jordanian cultural identity. Political parties, civil leadership, as well as civic-society organizations, are also very influential in preserving diversity.

The Jordanian Constitution stipulates freedom of religion to all Jordanians, regardless of ethnic or religious origin[5]. It guarantees civil and political rights and guarantees to all Jordanians by the Constitution. Although in many matters it is heavily influenced by Arab jurisprudence, the Christian community has its own judicial councils to resolve disputes between followers of the various Christian denominations.

Jordan’s economy is among the smallest in the Middle East, with insufficient supplies of water, oil, and other natural resources, underlying the government’s strong reliance on foreign assistance.

Other economic challenges for the government include chronic high rates of unemployment and underemployment, particularly among young people. The country has a relatively large and growing young population. In 2018, about 35% of Jordanians was under the age of 15 (male 1,635,094 and female 1,739,772), whereas 20% are 15–24 years of age. However, spending increased in July from the same month a year earlier, which should have supported the economy somewhat, although the government’s fiscal deficit widened in January-July to JD2 billion (3.5% of GDP), up from JD1.4 billion (2%) in the same period last year and supported private consumption[2].

Jordan’s real GDP registered a growth of 2% in 2018, a bit lower than growth in 2017, constrained by structural weaknesses and a difficult regional setting. On the supply side, a strong services sector (contributing to about 63% of GDP) followed by industry (about 35%) provided the dominant sector. On the demand side, growth from a significant improvement in net exports was offset by a major contraction in private demand during the first half of 2018. Investment growth was moderate due to its a major reduction in public capital expenditures and low inflows of private investment.

## 4 Legal Framework

### 4.1 Jordan Constitution

The Constitution of the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan stipulates that all Jordanians shall be equal before the law, and there shall be no discrimination between them as regards to their rights and duties on grounds of race, language, or religion. Reference in the Constitution to the rights of Jordanians includes women’s rights. The 1952 Constitution specifically prohibits discrimination.

In 2018, a first woman was appointed to the Court of Cassation, but no women serve in the Sharia Courts. Although qualified women are permitted to serve in this role, those who oppose the idea of having women in Sharia courts state that women are too emotional to occupy such positions. The presence of women in the judiciary is important because it enables women to claim their rights through the legal system and increases their knowledge about their legal rights.

### 4. Jordan's International Commitments

Jordan is signatory to all major international instruments pertaining to women’s rights and gender equality, including: i) the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), enacted in 1992; ii) the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights; iii) the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights; iv) the International Covenant on the Protection of the Rights of All Migrant Workers and Members of Their Families; v) the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), enacted in 1992; vi) the United Nations Convention Against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment; vii) the Protocol to Prevent, Suppress and Punish Trafficking in Persons, Especially Women and Children; viii) the Arab Charter on Human Rights.

However, the country maintains reservations on the article 9 (2) (which is supposed to give equal rights and responsibilities during marriage and at its dissolution), (3) (same rights and responsibilities as parents regarding children’s custody, education, and upbringing), and (4) (right of a woman to choose an occupation). Jordan maintains its reservations on article 16 (1) (c) and (d) because these are not compatible with Sharia provisions. In 2009, a Cabinet decision lifted its reservations on paragraph (a) of the article 3 (which is about the right of a woman to choose her husband and choice of residence without consent of their husbands or other male family members).

Although the government made improvements in the legislations, the provision remains ambiguous. For example, Jordan should address the Jordanian Nationality Law, which does not allow Jordanian women married to foreign-born spouses to pass on their nationality to their spouses and children, and stop what is


---
**Page 206**
---

called "administrative detention", which is used as a protective measure for women considered to be at risk of being killed by a family member, and allows her indefinite detention under the country's 1954 Crime Prevention Law. It is worth noting that, despite a minimum age of marriage set at 18, judges can issue temporary marriage agreements for girls <15 years old, which contributes to the rise of child marriage in Jordan, including in the Syrian refugee community.

### 4.4 National Women's mechanisms

#### 4.4.1 The Jordanian National Commission for Women (JNCW)

The Commission was established in 1992; it was the first national mechanism mandated to achieve gender equity and social justice by improving the status of women, enhancing their economic, social and political participation, and promoting equality between men and women.

It is assigned to JNCW and translated in its mandate focused on policy, laws and legislations, networks, exchange of information and dissemination of knowledge.

JNCW lack of government commitment to integrate gender within its various ministries which results in scattering JNCW's efforts here and there instead of systematic approach in the handling of women issues. This affects JNCW role and transforms their talks to a more ad hoc basis. JNCW remains an active player in the national dialogue on women's rights, gender equality, and addressing women issues. JNCW works strategically with Ministries of Planning, Justice, Labour, Social Development and Public sector centers for Women Empowerment and Gender Equality (WEGE) to promote women's rights.

JNCW also built strategic partnerships with Working NGOs for effective results on the ground and youth groups to challenge the current cultural practices and spread new culture of tolerance and respect for women's rights.

JNCW drafted the first national strategy for women in 2003. It serves as road map and guiding document for government to promote the rights of women under each component. The latest national strategy was launched in 2015, which is a comprehensive plan that addresses political empowerment, economic empowerment, media and ICT, integration of gender in policies and legislation, and capacity building for organizations working on women issues and the popular culture on women empowerment.

### 4.5 Governmental Mechanisms to promote Gender equality and women empowerment

The governmental gender focal point network was established by Cabinet decree in 1996. The network is composed of 20 government agencies and organizations. They are gender units of four Ministries namely, Ministry of Labour, Planning, Social Development and Health.

#### 4.5.1 Directorate of Women, Ministry of Labour

In 2006 the Ministry of Labour had a division for women. It was renamed in 2008 to take on the responsibilities of a directorate according to the new organizational structure. The tasks of the women's section include economic empowerment, as well as awareness and education.

**Page 207**

### Gender Unit, Ministry of Planning and International Cooperation

The department develops economic policies through conducting research and studies. It contributes to determining objectives, policies and development indicators with full coordination with JNCW and the gender focal points in other ministries and international organizations.

As other gender units in other ministries, the gender unit does not have the proper implementation mechanisms to ensure gender is properly addressed at all levels of the ministry, besides the limited financial and human resources to provide technical assistance on gender mainstreaming.


### Gender Unit, Ministry of Social Development

The gender unit falls under the supervision of the directorate of Studies and Policy Development. The directorate formulates studies, prepares strategic studies, as well as follow up on progress and implementation.

In addition, leadership in the area of gender analysis and mainstreaming is required. There is also a need to provide technical assistance to the gender units on how to mainstream gender in policies, plans, programmes and budgets.


### Ministry of Health

There is a gender focal point that has received advanced trainings on gender and gender responsive budgeting through the provisions by UN Women and other EU programs.

The gender focal point is responsible for providing guidance and support to the gender unit within the Ministry. However, the position, authority and resources of the gender units should be strengthened in the structure of the ministry.


### National Laws

Since 2007, the Government of Jordan has taken significant steps to address issues raised by civil society regarding the status of women in national law.

Jordan has ratified the International Labour Organisation (ILO) Equal Remuneration Convention and included its provisions in Jordanian law. legally discriminating against employment on the basis of gender or marital status is prohibited. Sexual harassment is an offence and abuse at work in the workplace is liable to punishment. However, no monitoring system is in place which indicates that there are still many challenges related to women's access to education, training capacities, prevalent patriarchal norms and gender roles, and the domination of men in the public spheres.


---
**Page 208**
---

Therefore, a significant gap remains even for the same type of work, the wage gap between genders has reached 13.8% in the public sector and 46.2% in the private sector. In the health sector, the gap was at 31.8% and in private education it had reached 30.2%.## 5.7 Personal Law status
The Personal Status Law (No. 61 of 1976) which is also known as “Family Law,” organizes all matters related to personal status including children. The Law is adapted from Islamic jurisprudence.
The Personal Status Law is meant to guide decisions and regulation in matters of marriage, divorce, custody, and inheritance. In 2012, the Government of Jordan introduced some positive major revisions to the law. These changes included a new provision that allows women to choose whether or not they want to be covered by their husband’s family name. Under which the amended Law establishes a three-month waiting period before a woman can waive her inheritance rights in favor of her husband. The law also requires the husband to approach his wife and ask her to sign documents that would waive her inheritance rights without her knowledge/guidance. Another major change was the introduction of the right for women to choose their place of residence, allowing divorced parents are able to see their children at their homes instead of police stations or local organizations.
Under the Nationality Law, women cannot pass citizenship to their children or spouse in the same way as men. Jordanian women married to non-Jordanian men cannot pass their citizenship to their children and spouses. In 2014, the government approved granting Jordanian women married to foreigners and their children a set of rights that allow them to pass their nationality to their children and spouses. This allows Jordanian women to pass their citizenship to their children and spouses (a right Jordanian men enjoy) but substantially eased their lives.
### 5.8 Election Law
Women are poorly represented in the political sphere, which prevents them from successfully contributing in the decision-making process that would shape the political priorities, policies and practices for women. The quota system was introduced in the 2003 elections through a legislative amendment to Article 14 of the Electoral Law. This amendment required at least one-third of all candidates on lists to be women. In the Election Law 2010, resulting in a total number of 13 women in the Lower House of Parliament.


---
**Page 209**
---

Parliament, 12 filled by quota and one by direct competition. The new electoral law implemented in the 2013 parliamentary elections increased the number of parliamentary seats to 150 and women's quota from 12 to 15 seats; it kept the level of representation of women to around 11%, far short of the Millennium Development Goals.

On the other hand, some steps have been made in easing the atmosphere for organizing as part of the government’s recent efforts to promote democratic reform in the areas of freedom of assembly, freedom of association and freedom of expression. However, these changes in legislation have not been translated into practice yet.

### 4.5.9 Protection against Domestic Violence

In Jordan, domestic violence is widely regarded as a family matter that affects large numbers of women, children and adults across their lifespan. In cultural and religious matters, Jordanian society has considered the family as the basic unit of social organization. The structure of the Jordanian family is patriarchal in nature. The husband is the head of the family and his role is to provide for his wife and children. Men, according to the cultural norms have the power and exercise complete authority over all the family’s affairs due to the financial power they have. On the other hand, women are expected to fulfill their roles as wives and mothers within the framework of the patriarchal structure of the family and the regulation of their families; otherwise they are condemned socially and may be exposed to physical punishment by their husbands or male relatives.

In Jordanian society, women are considered inferior to men. Women should obey all instructions and orders given by men to avoid punishment[1]. In this regard, women in Jordan have no legal rights as they are not recognized as independent individuals. As a result, women do not seek help from governmental agencies.

Article 98 of the Penal Code was applied to reduce penalties for men who perpetrated violent crimes against their wives and children if they had committed a disorderly act.
Article 98 was amended in 2017 to prevent it being used to reduce penalties for so-called honour killings. The law also provides for the establishment of a national strategy on judicial and legislative measures to combat and eliminate domestic violence. It has created many public welfare institutions and has given them a general mandate to provide protection programmes, promote awareness campaigns and establish shelters for victims of domestic violence.

The Ministry of Justice is responsible for implementing this strategy in collaboration with both the Public Security Directorate and the Ministry of Social Development, and a National Council for Family Violence Prevention was established.

### 4.5.10 National government strategies and policies for climate change and gender

The Ministry of Environment (MoE) is primarily responsible for overseeing the policy and legal frameworks that guide climate change mitigation and adaptation efforts of the country, including the development of Jordan’s national strategy on climate change and its implementation plan.

Jordan has prepared climate strategies, communications to the UNFCCC, and action plans including:

- Initial National Communication (1999)
- Women's Rights in Jordan, Arab Women Organization, 2013

### 4.5.10.1 The National Climate Change Policy

The National Climate Change Policy (2013-2020) provides an overview of Jordan’s strategies to combat climate change across various sectors and actions that the Jordan will implement in the coming years. It also outlines the potential impacts of climate change on Jordan, as well as the negative effects of climate change, as well as strategies to address gender imbalances between men and women, especially in the Middle Eastern country to include gender considerations in its national climate policy changes.

### 4.5.10.2 Third National Communication

Third National Communication (TNC) (2014) builds on the objectives and proposed actions of the Climate Policy. The TNC includes projected impacts of climate change on Jordan, as well as a comprehensive vulnerability assessment for the country. According to the TNC’s socio-economic downsizing projection models, it reports that Jordan will be highly vulnerable to climate change impacts in terms of water availability, food security, and economic development. The TNC notes that this is an extreme concern for a country that is among the poorest in availability of water resources per capita in the world. It also highlights the vulnerability of the country as a host of refugees from neighbouring countries. The TNC contains comprehensive vulnerability assessment results for Jordan’s key sectors, including agriculture and livestock. The TNC identifies opportunities for mitigation and adaptations that Jordan will pursue with support of the international community.

## 5 Situational Analysis — Jordan Valley Targeted Areas

### 5.1 Socio-economic profile

The Jordan Valley forms part of the larger Jordan Rift Valley. The internationally recognized World Heritage Site, the Dead Sea is located in this valley. The Jordan Valley has a unique economic and environmental value due to its rifting valley topography. The lower part of the Jordan Valley extends from the Sea of Galilee and meanders 200 km down to the Dead Sea through the Jordan Valley.

Climatically, the Jordan Valley is characterized by hot dry summers and mild wet winters, becoming progressively drier towards the south. The climate in this region is expected to become warmer with impacts are likely to intensify the water supply-related problems in the Jordan Valley[2]. Impacts of climate change includes a foreseen reduction in local annual water resources with a maximum of 20 % by 2050, increased evaporation rates and related surface water evaporation rates based on 2038 climate change impact assessment.

The northern part of the East Bank of the Jordan Valley in Jordan will be impacted most negatively by climate change, with a foreseen substantial reduction of annual and winter rainfall, although summer rainfall will increase slightly.

The Jordan Valley houses large number of informal foreign workers originating mainly from Egypt and Iraq, who are employed in agriculture or construction. In addition to these workers, there are 964 households live in the Jordan Valley today, some of them employed in the agricultural sector###.

13% of the total population lives in rural areas, where poverty is more prevalent than in urban areas and living conditions are difficult. As an arid country with less than 5% of land available for farming and little to no rainfall, prospects of self-sustainability through farming are limited. The most vulnerable groups include single-parent families, households headed by women or young people, elderly-headed households, households 12% with sick or elderly people, and households that do not own land or have very little land.
