Local citizens of the Jordan Valley are involved in mixed farming systems that covered crop and livestock production systems. In addition, some Bedouins also live in the Jordan Valley and use the lands as grazing areas for their animals. The climate change will have a significant impact on the availability of fodder for their animals. The livestock will be impacted by climate change, whereas self-sufficiency degree (SSD) for most food items would decline by 2050. Of great concern, production


---
**Page 214**
---

of red meat would be highly affected by climate change, as rangelands would not provide enough forage for sheep and goats.###


In the Jordan Valley there is a small group of wealthy agricultural entrepreneurs, but also to a large group of labourers who live close to the poverty line of JD 32.6 per person per month. The negative impact of climate change on agriculture in Jordan (1970-2050) will be felt by all sectors of society: agriculture and rural enterprises as farmers, animal caretakers, workers and entrepreneurs. Land is the most important asset to support production and provide for food, nutrition and income security, however, it is also a source of conflict in Jordan. The land ownership structure in Jordan is skewed favoring male inheritance and providing disincentives for women to purchase land.

Industries in the Jordan valley are mostly small industries for the construction sector and package industry. There are several quarries that produce materials for the construction of buildings and infrastructure projects. In addition to these, there is also a cement factory located in the northern part of the valley as well as metal processing plants in the central and southern part of the Jordan valley that produce metal products such as pipes, fittings, and other metal components. Small industries are also present in the region, which produce wooden and plastic crates and boxes for the commercial farmers, for packing their produce in accordance with the demands of the export markets.

In terms of women's engagement in the agricultural sector specifically, FAO statistics show that as of 2014, 0.9% of the country's total female population was employed in agriculture, and this could be related to the fact that most women are engaged in home-based agriculture. The majority of small-scale agriculture was found to be the most common type of agricultural activity undertaken by women.

Most of women in the targeted areas are in health and education sectors (56% and 62% respectively), while the majority of men are working in agriculture (89%) compared with only 11% of women[3].

Meanwhile, women were found to be engaging in home-based agriculture to produce food for household consumption or to sell their produce at local markets. This was done as a means of supplementing their household's income that enhanced their ability to independently take decisions within the family.

The FGD participants stated that, the majority of women who engaged in home-based agriculture in are Jordanian, compared to Syrian women which is linked to their or their family’s ability to own land, unlike Syrian women where cultural norms and variations within them are important to take into account when identifying avenues for programming.

# Literacy Rate and Education Status

The literacy rate for women aged 15+ is relatively high in the targeted area (97%), with 82% of women enrolled in Ain El Basha compared to 3.6% among men. However, school enrollment rates are similar for both boys and girls, but the enrollment ratio is higher among girls enrolled in secondary education (55%) compared to 40% for boys, where they enroll in vocational education (98%).

#### 5.3 Access to Finance and Resources

The majority of women are not engaging in any financial banks, FGD said that they used to get revolving funds from the donors but these funds were not well controlled by the donors and became a financial burden for women. Most of these funds were for the purpose of family issues such as schools and universities fees, medical expenses, etc., and women did not have control over them. Women usually refuse to guarantee these funds to be used for other purposes. Men usually refuse to guarantee their fund for their wives, as it means that they are responsible for the money.

Many research assessments on the revolving funds impact indicated that women entrepreneurs in Jordan face considerable gender-specific barriers to accessing loans, such as having to provide a guarantee or collateral and being required to have a male guarantor. The study also found that men are usually interested in acting as guarantors if they can have control over the money; few women have the means to guarantee loans.

However, FGD participants mentioned that few women owned lands and farms (10%), most of them are as sons or daughters-in-law. Women also have limited access to family members such as husbands, brothers and sons. ### 5.4 Women participation in the public sphere and the women CSOs involvement in the decision-making process in their communities

The Jordan Valley Solidarity Network is supporting community-based organizations (CBOs) that provide services to vulnerable groups, individuals, households and Syrians. Some of the CBOs in the targeted areas participated in the FGD and contribute to the discussion of social impact on projects implemented by the Jordanian Water Development Fund (JWDF). The Jordanian Water Development (JWDH), JODHD supports national initiatives to improve the management of scarce water resources, through a wide range of water related projects. To build sustainable awareness and to support the development of local communities.

The other key CBO is the Jordan Environment Society (JES). The objective of JES includes, but is not limited to, protecting the environment and its basic elements of water, air, soil and wildlife, based on the belief that the protection of nature is a human right. JES works in partnership with the Royal Society for Conservation of Nature to create, manage and advocate for a national network of protected areas and other conservation sites throughout Jordan, including the Dead Sea area while also promoting wider public support and action for the protection of the natural environment within Jordan and neighboring countries.

Finally, IUCN Jordan aims to encourage and assist society to conserve the integrity and diversity of nature in order to ensure that any use of natural resources is equitable and ecologically sustainable. A range of women CSOs operate in their areas; there are some focus on empowerment, such as providing training for women in business skills or access to microfinance funds. On the other hand, CBOs may enjoy a level of public trust and be considered to be safe or acceptable by local communities.

However, these organizations are seen as a powerful tool to communicate with women and other vulnerable groups in the local communities; it was explained that they don’t have a network relation


---
**Page 218**
---

with each other nor the other official or private entities in the community. Those CIDs are approached by donors, providing them with trainings such as food processing and handmade crafts, however these training are not reflected or support for women in enhancing their social situations because they are not given any income. Some CIDs are active in delivering awareness sessions for the local communities such as JOUHD community centre in A Yarmouk on water management, environment and drugs for youth.

### 5.5 Social norms and cultural constraints

There was much evidence to indicate a gendered division of labor within households in the three areas. Women were responsible for cooking, cleaning, childcare, and other forms of 'indoor' domestic work and men responsible for working outside of the home. The family size reported ranged from 2-10 people with an average of five members per household. One participant reported where the man had more than one wife. Women participants indicated that there is a high rate of female-headed households in their communities which lead them to be more vulnerable because they are unemployed and don't have financial power.

Where feasible, participants were asked to discuss the decision-making dynamic around child spacing (as a proxy indicator for women's access to decision making within the family). Nearly all said that men made decisions about when children should be born. Women reported little involvement by either mother in law.

In terms of financial decision making, participants reported. There was a fair degree of consensus that women were responsible for managing daily expenditures such as food, though while women said they were given a spending allowance by their husbands, the latter mostly denied providing an allowance for their wives to manage. Instead, men and older sons were seen as having more authority in decisions to be taken by both men and women together. The general picture that emerged was one where, though women had some say over how money is spent, decision making ultimately lies in the hands of the husband.

Norms around mobility, woman's mobility was not entirely restricted by all husbands, it is dependent upon the husband's preference. Most men and women reported that if the husband was not at home and, for example, a child needed to be taken to school or a doctor, then they would go themselves. Similarly, however, most participants reported that if the husband or an older son was available, it would be more likely that he would take them somewhere. Women also reported that in general there is a significant percentage of youth are still unemployed and still actively looking for suitable jobs. Employment opportunities were seen as different unsustainable jobs, in the military sector, or in daily -palluating credits (job per day basis).

### 5.6 Specific findings in line with Gender Analysis Framework

1. It was observed that only, on 30% of the FGD women participants know about the climate change and how it may affect women and children more, saying that women are responsible for taking care of children and they are more vulnerable to the effects of climate change such as risks associated with rain floods and poor sanitation facilities. While the majority of men (80%) participants said that the climate change is a serious issue in their communities, only 25% of them know about it. There is a different understanding between men and women related to the impacts of climate change on their lives. Women are more aware than men regarding the effects of climate change. Awareness-raising interventions under Output 4.1 — see Activity 4.1 in the GAP below. This will also be supported by awareness raising activities under Output 3.2, which is a gender-based activities (Output 3.1), where the specific needs of female beneficiaries related to sustainable livelihoods and adaptation are emphasised during implementation — see Activities 2.1.3 as 3.1 in the GAP.


---
**Page 219**
---

2. Men control access to resources in which men own the key productive assets such as land, livestock and medium to large businesses. For climate change interventions especially in dry lands, this means that activities which include the use of productive resources like land or livestock are often controlled by men. Therefore, for climate change adaptation strategies relating to dry land conservation should include both local men and women as key stakeholders. Women need to be included not only for their role as users of natural resource bodies to be enhanced, as well as focused on improved productivity and income generation that promotes the participation of women and their access to resources. Under Activity 1.1.4, the establishment of a gender focal person in each project site will ensure that all decision-making bodies focused on the climate-resilient management of watersheds and their resources are gender-sensitive. This will be achieved by ensuring that women participate in the management of their local wetlands and associated resources to support climate change adaptation strategies.

3. Access to credit is a key constraint in improving women’s economic productivity in the selected areas. Hence it is important that the project focuses on improving women’s access to credit through the establishment of a revolving fund for women in each project site, as well as an upper-catchment area of the project's three target sites. The on-granting facility and the Water Fund’s grant mechanism (Activity 2.1.4) provides an opportunity for women to access grant finance for small-scale irrigation projects. This will enable women to diversify their income streams, enabling them to contribute to climate change adaptation in their own communities that directly benefits local watersheds. The revolving fund will be established with a gender component and need to be gender responsive, promoting the inclusion of women, considering their roles in society and their contribution to household livelihoods and community development — proposals will be reviewed by JILIMA’s gender expert to confirm this.

4. The project can provide support to the Ministry of Environment, to activate gender mainstreaming in the formulation of policies and plans for climate change adaptation in the decision-making process. Output 1.1 includes the drafting of amendments to Jordan's planning, legal and policy frameworks (Activity 2.1.3) that will promote women’s participation in climate-resilient ILWRM (Activity 1.1.2). This provides an opportunity to further mainstream gender into policy- and decision-making processes, contributing to a gender-responsive cross-sectoral response to climate change adaptation. The draft amendments must be approved with at least 50% female representation — see Activity 1.1.2 of the GAP.

5. Women’s illiteracy, poverty and unemployment are high in the targeted areas, which make them very vulnerable to climate change impacts. Therefore, empowerment is needed to improve women's participation in alternative livelihoods, to ensure that their rights and interests are recognized and protected. Under Activity 1.2.3, a gender-sensitive approach will be applied for vulnerable groups in the targeted areas and particularly woman-headed households. Under Activity 3.1.4, women will be trained on climate-resilient agricultural practices (up to 75% of beneficiary households will be female headed, depending on the livelihood). Livelihoods will be diversified and will be tailored to meet the needs of women in each project site — see Activity 2.1.3 in the GAP. Activity 3.1.4 will see local communities, including women, empowered to monitor and manage their water resources through participatory gender-sensitive and responsive community-based water use monitoring and managing plans for project beneficiary communities. This will be achieved by training women on climate-resilient agricultural practices to monitor and manage the use of clean water (100% female representation); and promoting additional livelihoods that are not only climate resilient but also the use of clean water (including at least 50% female beneficiaries) — see Activity 3.1.4 in the GAP.

6. Increased collaboration and partnership with CBOs, NGOs and the entities working on climate change issues can help increase awareness of gender- and environmental sustainability issues


---
**Page 220**
---

in the targeted areas. This awareness raising should seek to increase women’s knowledge and equal rights in particular. In addition, CEOs could support gender mainstreaming by involving whole communities, men, and local leaders, to overcome sensitivity to the traditional divisions of labor and gender roles. The outreach program will be implemented through a series of workshops that will see the implementation of a gender-responsive climate change adaptation outreach programme in the region of Jornada, bringing together local CEOs and other stakeholders. The outreach programme will create a multi-faceted approach around climate change issues, including those which are gender specific, through a specific model prioritizing gender and youth — see Activity 4.1 in Annexure A.

# Gender Action Plan (GAP)

## Impact Statement

Research and anecdotal evidence indicate that women, children and youth are among the groups most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. In addition, a large number of vulnerable people in communities that are highly dependent on local natural resources for their livelihoods will be affected by climate change.

The Jordan Integrated Landscape Management Initiative (JILM) is a project that aims to improve the resilience and adaptive capacity of communities living within the project area through improved governance and resource-allocation and implementation. In the context of the JILM, gender mainstreaming in national climate change policy-making and implementation was therefore seen as a priority in stakeholder consultations.

## Expected Total Numbers

Expected total numbers of direct and indirect beneficiaries (reduced vulnerability or increased resilience), are:

- 342,786 people directly benefiting from project interventions (153,786 men and 190,200 women)
- 758,006 people indirectly benefiting from the project (340,006 men and 418,000 women)

The project will also:

- ensure gender inclusion in all the policies reviewed to be more gender sensitive;
- ensure short-term and long-term impact of gender-affirmative actions;
- build capacity for government institutions in acknowledging gender differences in responding to climate risks;
- ensure that women should take leadership with regard to project commitments related to them, however, without the marginalization of men who are also key resource users and negative service; and
- increase social accountability.

## Outcome Statement

Interventions will contribute to climate change of at least 111,206 women in project area through direct improvement of their capacities to resources for agricultural/forestry practices and water management, climate smart agriculture and agroforestry practices and gender equality.

### Sub-total of dedicated costs: Gender Officer and Gender mainstreaming costs

The Gender Officer will oversee all activities related to gender mainstreaming, including the implementation of action plans. The officer will ensure that the project-level grievance redress mechanism (GRM) (See Annex 6: ESMP) will be accessible for women and girls in a manner that is sensitive to their special confidentiality and protection needs and refer cases to existing protection mechanisms accordingly.
